# Changelog

This file is the changelog of the PostgreSQL extension. The SQLite extension (`sift` for SQLite, artifact `sift-sqlite`) has its own: [sqlite/CHANGELOG.md](sqlite/CHANGELOG.md).

## Unreleased

- Changed: pg_sift: VACUUM's space step of a `sift_col` index moves the highest runs of a segment larger than `sift.vacuum_rewrite_limit` down into the free blocks below it, a round at a time (up to 64 rounds, the lock let go between them), and cuts the file where it ended: the segment keeps its generation, its header page is written again at the lowest free block with the extents it now has, and nothing is read through the decoders. A merge writes its one segment above the blocks of the segments it replaced, and the file stayed at twice the live bytes for good when that segment was larger than the allowance: a 75.3 MB segment above 76.9 MB of free blocks, allowance 12 MB, went from a 152.2 MB file to 75.35 MB in 6 rounds of 8 to 18 ms each (0.10 s, 76.9 MB of WAL for 75.3 MB moved).
- Improved: pg_sift: the first plan of a backend on a `sift_col` index read the zone arrays of every leaf (about 11,000 buffers and 40 ms more than the second plan at 61,670 units, again in every backend a pooler recycles): a segment of 64 units or more now keeps the sums of its leaves and a sample of its zones in a block after its last section, and the first plan reads about 3,000 buffers at 61,670 units (11,700 before) (+0.4% bytes, no measurable cost to `CREATE INDEX`). No format minor: 3.4 reads such a segment and ignores the block; a segment written before is read from its zones. `sift.col_verify` checks the block.
- Improved: pg_sift: `key_columns = 'auto'` writes a bloom filter for a column it chose that got no list, bitmap or runs, in what the share of the unit has left, so `WHERE id = x` of a unique id scattered over the units skips the units whose filter lacks the key; the NOTICE at `CREATE INDEX` names the column and what `point_tid_columns` would add in MB. An `int8` column keeps key lists and runs like `int2`, `int4` and `date`.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.json_build_object(VARIADIC "any")` returns the text of `json_build_object` (the same bytes for every type of argument and key, and the same errors) and looks up the category and the output function of each argument once for a call site, where the core function asks the type cache for every argument of every call; with `sift` before `pg_catalog` in the `search_path` every call of the name takes it. A grouped statement over 300,000 rows that calls it 5 times takes 166.4M instructions where `json_build_object` takes 177.1M, and one that calls it 12 times 925.5M where 975.3M.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a promotion of a write buffer of a `sift_col` index whose `key_columns` is `auto` wrote its segment without the key lists of a column the cube holds (the report after a build drops it from the option, the units keep their lists), so an equality on the key read every unit whose zone held it; it keeps the lists the segments hold, as a merge does.
- Improved: pg_sift: `key_columns = 'auto'` takes a unique `int2`, `int4` or `date` column named `*_id` when the index is key cut or the column is not in heap order, and a unit whose keys come in runs of consecutive values keeps them as a list of runs (column format minor 23) where the offsets and the bitmap do not fit 3% of the unit, so a lookup by id, an `IN` list or a range of ids reads the few units that hold them where it read every unit. A 3.4 library refuses a segment with a list of runs: `sift.upgrade_check('3.4')` says how to write it without.
- Improved: pg_sift: an index scan that returns tuples (not an index-only scan or a bitmap scan) of a lookup in a `point_tid_columns` directory (`WHERE grp = 7 LIMIT 10`, an `IN` list of a few long lists) decodes the addresses of its keys as the scan asks for them, a cursor over each list of the keys merged in order, where it decoded every address of the keys before the first row (8,300 addresses of the key at 800,000 rows, 1.5 million instructions): `grp = 7 LIMIT 10` runs in 0.021 ms (0.133 before; 0.012 to 0.015 for a btree), `LIMIT 100` in 0.036 (0.21), `LIMIT 1000` in 0.22 (0.36), and the scans that read every row of the key take what they took (4.9 ms for the 8,300 rows). The units with a dead row are read after the addresses of the others; a scan with other conditions, or an `IN` list of more than 8 lists, decodes whole as before.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the index scan of a lookup in a `point_tid_columns` directory by what it does: the start is the open of the source and a probe (12 plus 4 a key, where it was 24 plus 4: 67,000 instructions, 16 us measured), a key that is in every unit counts its listed rows and not the rows of the units (the filter of 800,000 rows was charged for `grp = 7`), and the index correlation is the share of units whose directory lists addresses, since the scan returns them in heap order. `grp = 7 LIMIT 10` of 800,000 rows (`grp` has 100 values) took the sequential scan (0.21 to 0.39 ms, cost 68.4) and takes the index scan now (0.016 to 0.024 ms, cost 22.2; a btree on `(grp, ts)` is 0.42..37.5). Planner picks of the 41 queries of `bench/col_scan.py` (800,000 rows, one index) that changed, warm median: `IN` of 10 ids count 0.063 to 0.030 ms, `IN` of 1,000 ids rows 0.92 to 0.65 (bitmap to index), rows of an equality plus a range 0.42 to 0.28, an equality on a key in every unit 3.16 to 1.22, text `LIKE` rows 0.27 to 0.06, count 0.65 to 0.05, prefix count 1.16 to 0.15; none is slower by more than 0.015 ms, and the plan-choice gate (30,000 rows, stock lane) has 99 shapes, 0 loses, 0 failures.
- Improved: pg_sift: a lookup of a `point_tid_columns` directory with other conditions (`WHERE grp = 7 AND ts BETWEEN ..`) decodes the addresses of the key only in the units the other conditions leave, and stops at the last of them, where it decoded every address of the key first (about 8,700 at 800,000 rows, 0.9 million instructions): `count(*)` with the aggregate scan runs in 0.21 ms (0.34 before; 0.12 for a b-tree), the index scan and the bitmap scan of the same statement take 25% fewer instructions.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the rewrite of a `key_cut` `sift_col` index wrote all its live bytes as one segment whatever `sift.col_auto_merge_partition_kb` and `sift.vacuum_rewrite_limit` said, and re-merged every partition into it at the next VACUUM: an index over the limit was one segment larger than the space step could ever move, and the file stayed at twice the live bytes (105 MB live after 12 churn cycles: 209.5 MB file after VACUUM). The rewrite now cuts the index into partitions of the key (under three quarters of the size, so that a step of the space step moves one whole), and a partition the churn did not touch is not read. The merge of an index over the limit (a parallel build's tier, `sift.col_merge`) writes partitions too: 19 segments of a 75 MB index became 9 of at most 9 MB, and the file went from 150 MB at the merge's peak back to 75.5 MB at the next VACUUM, where the one segment of 75 MB it wrote before stayed at 152 MB.
- Changed: pg_sift: VACUUM's space step of a `sift_col` index runs rounds, up to 16, each copying at most `sift.vacuum_rewrite_limit` under the lock and letting it go before the next, and a round moves the segments that end the file down into the free blocks even when it cannot cut the file yet (the next round or VACUUM does): a rewrite writes beside the segments it replaces, and the file went back to its live bytes only after as many VACUUMs as the limit fits in the index (105 MB live, 12 MB limit: file after VACUUM 199.9 MB without rounds, 115.1 MB with them, 12 cycles).
- Fixed: pg_sift: the scan of a statement whose lookups are a CTE or a sub-select of ids that PostgreSQL has no statistics for started no workers when it read many rows: PostgreSQL expects a handful of rows from the join, the scan was priced at a few milliseconds for them, and the 3 to 4 ms the workers take to start never paid (two aggregates over the elements of a list, 353,000 rows: 793 ms alone, 262 ms with 4 workers). The path with workers is now priced for the rows the scan reads, and the price of the scan without workers (the one the plan above and the normal plan are compared with) is unchanged.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.agg_par_min_units` (default 128): a scan whose workers were priced for the rows it reads starts them only when it keeps that many units after the zones and the keys of its lookups; at 60 units kept the workers made a 40 ms scan 58 ms, and the setting is 0 to start them for any scan.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a query that joins a table to a `sift_col` index with `point_tid_columns` could crash the backend (signal 11) in the executor: the bitmap path that reads the addresses of a lookup keyed by another relation (`WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM t WHERE t.id = e.key)` over partitions, in a merge join) was planned without that relation, so the key of the outer side was read from a plan that did not wait for it. The path needs the relation now, as the index path under it does. The releases 3.4.0, 3.4.1 and 3.4.2 have the fault.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the builds of a release line could not be told apart in a running server: `sift.version()`, the extension version and, on PostgreSQL 18, `pg_get_loaded_modules()` said `3.4` for 3.4.0, 3.4.1 and 3.4.2. The module version is now the release and the commit (`3.5.0+2a7e47598`; `scripts/build-id.sh`: `git describe` of a checkout, the `BUILD_ID` that `git archive` fills in for the deb and rpm builds, `unknown` for a tarball with neither). `sift.version()` and the SQL scripts are unchanged.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices a lookup of a point directory by the rows it lists: the aggregate scan charges the tests of an `IN` list or an equality only for those rows, where it charged every row of the kept units, and the index scan no longer charges the filter of the kept units' rows when the directory is what keeps them. A `count` or `sum` over 1,000 ids of 800,000 rows took SiftAgg with two workers (5.6 to 7 ms) over the index (0.9 ms); it runs in 1.3 to 2 ms on one process now (cost 2,048 against 8,167 for the index), and a list of 100 stays on the index scan (0.1 ms).
- Improved: pg_sift: an `IN` list of up to 65,536 keys is looked up in the point directory of a `sift_col` index (`point_columns`, `point_tid_columns`) by the aggregate scan whatever the number of units, where a list of more keys than twice the units made it read every unit; and the scan's start for an `IN` list no longer walks the table of its range twice (about 0.4 ms of 800,000 rows for any list). `count`/`sum` over 100 keys of 800,000 rows run in 0.6 ms (1.1 to 1.8 before), over 1,000 keys in 1.3 ms (3.3 before), with the scan forced on one process.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a scan with two lookup tables keyed by the same column (a chain `B.id = A.id` that the planner rewrites to the scanned column) tested the column against the keys of each: the keys of one within the keys of the other are now the only join key conjunct (`Join Key Filters` counts it once), so the wider set is not tested over every scanned row, which cost the platforms without a vector kernel for the lookup table (arm64) a pass over the rows.
- Improved: pg_sift: the first lookups of a backend in a text or uuid column of a `sift_col` index with `point_columns` (`WHERE s = x`, a range or a prefix of a dictionary of 2,048 strings or more) no longer pay for the whole dictionary at the 33rd test: the backend keeps the head of the dictionary and the sorted positions its searches read (about 300 kB for a segment and a leaf, under `dict_cache_kb`, by segment generation: nothing to invalidate after a merge, `VACUUM` or `REINDEX`), and parses the dictionary only once the searches have cost what the parse does. 1,000,000 rows, a new backend, the first 100 different keys: uuid 69-92 ms to 14-23 ms (480 M to 51 M instructions), text 92 ms to 16-23 ms (790 M to 123 M); 300 keys: uuid 85 ms to 32-37 ms, text 88 ms to 52-56 ms; 1,000 keys: uuid 107-148 ms to 85-96 ms, text the same (117-163 ms to 140-164 ms); from about 2,000 lookups the old parse at the 33rd test is paid back, and 3,000 keys cost uuid 175-217 ms to 246-304 ms (1,040 M to 950 M instructions, the bisections miss the cache more than the parse does) and text 202-214 ms to 224-241 ms; no format change
- Improved: pg_sift: `count(*) WHERE col IS NULL` (or `IS NOT NULL`, alone, one `count(*)` and nothing else) of a `sift_col` index reads the null counts of the zones of the units, also for a column that has no histogram in them (more than 256 values in a unit): 1,000,000 rows, 30% NULL, 1.01 to 0.03 ms (9.6M to 0.26M instructions); a unit with a dead row, an exception or a NaN is read as before.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a merge or `sift.col_refresh` (and the rollup worker after `ALTER INDEX .. SET (cube_minmax = ..)` or `rollup_dims`) of a `sift_col` index with `key_columns = 'auto'` rewrote the units without the key lists, and the key table, that the build had kept for a column the cube holds (the segment came out as column format minor 14), so after churn an equality on that column read every unit whose zone held it and the planner priced the scan above a btree plan (a SiftAgg node lost on statements with a filter on the key and a lookup join); every rewrite now writes the lists again where a segment holds them.
- Improved: pg_sift: `WHERE key = x` (an `IN` list, a range of the key, `GROUP BY key`) with `count`, `sum`, `min` and `max` of a numeric, date or timestamp column of a `sift_col` index reads one record of each segment, not its units, when the key is a foreign key or a `*_id` column of `key_columns` with more than 256 values in a unit (the cube of a unit keeps no histogram of it): a key table per segment of at most 3% of the segment, written by the build, promotion, merge and VACUUM (+2.6% instructions of `CREATE INDEX`, +0.9% of an insert and promotion, +4.3% of a merge, +3.0% of a VACUUM that rewrites a segment, at 100,000 rows and 3,000 keys). 300 keys at 900,000 rows: `count(*), sum(amount), min(ts), max(ts) WHERE key = 17` 5.20 ms before, 0.107 ms now (0.49 ms and 0.054 ms at 100,000 rows); the segments of minor 22 are not read by 3.4, and `key_table = off` writes the minor 15 ones as before.
- Improved: pg_sift: a `sift_table` keeps the key table of its foreign key and `*_id` columns too, so `WHERE key = x` (an `IN` list, a range of the key, `GROUP BY key`) with `count`, `sum`, `min` and `max` of a numeric, date or timestamp column reads one record of each segment whose superblocks are all plain, not its units. 300 keys at 900,000 rows: `count(*), sum(amt), min(amt), max(amt) WHERE key = 17` takes 0.577 ms without the table and 0.068 ms with it (`k IN (3 keys)` 0.835 and 0.069 ms), the segments grow by 0.27% (2.7% at 100,000 rows), and the write paths cost +0.4% (load and promotion) to +1.5% (a rewrite) instructions. The segments of minor 22 are not read by 3.4; `sift.table_key_table = off` writes the minor 15 and 18 ones as before.
- Fixed: `sift.col_verify` could report that a unit's exceptions differ from its chunks after a writer reused an empty overflow bitmap. Writers now inspect the bits; verification checks the stored exception leaf and accepts missing optional summaries from older writers. Existing indexes need no rebuild for this fault.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.upgrade_check('3.3')` did not list an index with a global order (`order_array_columns`, column format minor 21) as one that 3.3 cannot read: the table of the first release that reads each minor stopped at 20. It is a blocker now, and the check to 3.4 finds it ok.
- Changed: pg_sift: the extension SQL is version 3.5 (`pg_sift--3.5.sql`, `pg_sift--3.4--3.5.sql`, `default_version = '3.5'`): run `ALTER EXTENSION pg_sift UPDATE` in every database. 3.5 adds no SQL object, so the update only moves the version; the scripts of 3.4 are the released ones.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the normal plan of a lookup of one key that owns many rows of the table (a key of the column's most common values, listed as a constant of a CTE) was priced for the mean's rows of the column, which PostgreSQL counts a probe of the key by, so the scan lost the choice for a statement of the heaviest key that it wins by a factor of ten: the normal plan is charged the excess of the key's rows over the mean's, as many times as the statistics give (the key of 1,726 rows of 95,916 in 500 keys: 9 times), on top of the lost-estimate charge of the rows out of the joins. Default settings, 2 workers allowed, a column index, the 148 statements of the heaviest key, medians of three alternated builds against the build without it: 100,000 rows 62.8 ms to 7.5 for a statement of two arms over the key (PostgreSQL's plan 72.9), 12 of the 148 statements 20 to 90% faster (the sum 1,615 ms to 1,490); 300,000 rows 94.7 to 63.7, 90.1 to 60.0, 40.7 to 27.5 (the sum 2,773 ms to 2,710), 5 statements 1.5 to 11 ms slower (51.1 to 62.3).
- Fixed: pg_sift: the keys a statement lists as constants of a CTE were not followed through a second CTE: when a CTE of constant ids was joined to the table in a CTE (`WHERE s.id = k.id`, with or without a `LEFT JOIN`) and a third CTE passed the table's key on, the shared CTE and the arms of the inline plan were priced for the mean's rows of the column, as if the listed key were an average one, so the plan that keeps the shared CTE won where the scans win in time (a statement of the heaviest key through two CTEs). The listed constants are now followed to the CTE that reads the key, and the rows they own (as the statistics give them) lift the CTE's scan as for a CTE of the list itself. Default settings, 2 workers allowed, a column index, 904 statements of 148 shapes, medians of three alternated builds against the build without it: at 100,000 rows 46 plans change, 12 from the normal plan to the scan and none the other way, and the 43 timed statements take 264.4 ms in all against 374.4; at 300,000 rows 53 change and the 53 timed take 959.0 ms against 1,177.0; one statement is slower (2.3 to 6.2 ms).
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the dictionary a worker inflates for a numeric stored as images (35 operators a row, measured with 2,000,000 rows of almost unique values) by the number of values the column has, to the 0.13 power over 2,000,000 (20 operators with 30,000 values over 300,000 rows, 20 ns a row measured): the workers of `sum(numeric)` over such a column were priced above the scan without them and the scan kept one process, though they shorten it by 28 to 40% (300,000 rows, the scan 15 to 17 ms against 22 to 28, with the workers' CPU 43 to 48 ms against 25 to 33). Default settings with `parallel_setup_cost = 1` to bring the break-even to this size, 34 shapes of int, text and numeric keys, `count(DISTINCT)` and wide sums: 7 of the 34 launch workers (3 of them since this change), all 7 are shapes the workers shorten, and none of the shapes they lengthen does.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices what the workers of a scan cost with the measured numbers: a millisecond of the scan is 750 cost units when it weighs the 3.35 ms the workers take to start (it was 500), and the leader's merge costs 25 operators for each group key of each group of each process's table, on top of the 8 for the group (35 ns measured with one key over 30,000 groups, 85 ns with three over 100,000). A scan of 4 to 13 ms no longer launches workers that add 7 to 17 ms of CPU and no wall: 1,000,000 rows, default settings, 2 workers allowed, median of 3, 2 rounds: 9 groups of text and an average 6 ms to 4 (CPU 16 ms to 8), an average over two text keys of 30,000 groups 16 ms to 12 (CPU 36 to 16, instructions 178 to 135 million); the 8 other shapes of the 10 timed keep their plan and their times, including the 8 ms scan over three text keys that the workers shorten from 11 ms.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `VACUUM` of a `sift_table` with a unit of more than 65,000 rows failed with `could not read blocks N..N in file ...: read only 0 of 8192 bytes` once rows were deleted from every such unit, and never finished: the liveness bits of a unit of up to 74,496 rows lie on three pages of the segment, which with the header page are four, and clearing them kept room for three (a page number overwrote the buffer of the first page). 3.3.0, 3.4.0, 3.4.1 and 3.4.2 have it (1,000,000 rows of one integer column, `VACUUM FULL`, `DELETE ... WHERE id % 20 = 5`, `VACUUM`); the rows stay readable and the dead ones unreclaimed. Test `tbl_vacuum_dead_span`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the normal plan of an aggregate that reads a lookup of a few keys was kept at PostgreSQL's price whatever the keys own, and PostgreSQL counts the rows of a key at the mean of the column: a lookup of one id whose rows are a fiftieth of the table (a key of the column's most common values) met a normal plan priced for a fiftieth of that, and the scan lost the choice it wins in time. The normal plan is now charged the excess of the rows the keys own (the share the statistics give the listed constants of the lookup, as the lift of a shared CTE counts them) over the rows PostgreSQL expects out of the joins, as for a lookup of many keys. Default settings, 2 workers allowed, a column index, 904 statements of 148 shapes: at 100,000 rows 14 plans change and take 65.3 ms in all against 20.5 (35.7 ms to 4.7 where PostgreSQL's plan takes 55.4, 14.4 to 3.8 (15.4), 4.2 to 1.4, 3.2 to 1.3); at 300,000 rows 21 change, 320.1 ms against 245.6 (26.5 to 9.5, 29.5 to 16.2, 31.1 to 20.6, 63.5 to 52.1); none is slower by 20% and 1 ms, and the statements within 1.2x (or 5 ms) of the faster of the two plans go from 12 to 14 of the 14 at 100,000 rows and from 14 to 19 of the 21 at 300,000.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the heap check the planner charges the scan for the rows on pages that are not all-visible counted the rows the table's own filters keep, not the rows that reach the groups after the lookup joins have filtered them: a statement whose lookups keep a few hundred of 288,000 rows was charged the pages of all of them. A scan whose arms are priced for the check that the data needs takes the arms it lost: 300,000 rows, 12-key lookups of a table with a column index, 16% of whose pages are not all-visible, default settings, 2 workers allowed: 39.7 ms to 28.6, 38.5 to 28.9, 41.8 to 31.1 (the normal plan takes 4 to 5 ms: the pick of these three statements is still the scan); of the 57 statements timed at 100,000 and 300,000 rows 3 change their plan and none is slower. Test `agg_visibility_refusal`.
- Improved: pg_sift: the scan gives the ids of a text value (a `count(DISTINCT)` of a text column, a group key, the values the leader takes from the workers) by its bytes when the collation is deterministic, hashing and comparing them in place instead of through `hashtext` and `texteq` (each looks up the collation and detoasts both values): 9 to 10% fewer instructions for the scan of `count(DISTINCT author)` over 1,000,000 rows of 245,000 values, 394 to 357 million without workers and 1,009 to 918 with 2 (300,000 rows: 121 to 109 and 303 to 272); the same ids, the same answers; a nondeterministic collation keeps the type's equality.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the ids that the dictionary entries of a text column get for a `count(DISTINCT)` of it (35 operators an entry at 100,000 distinct values, growing with their number: 6 ns a row measured at 10,000 values, 83 at 100,000, 199 at 736,000 over 3,000,000 rows; it was free), and the pairs that the workers send to the leader for it (a distinct text value costs the leader 0.4 to 1.2 us to merge: 250 operators; it was 7 for every row). The scan with 2 workers took 1.9 times as long as the scan without (226 against 119 ms over 1,000,000 rows, 1,051 against 622 ms over 3,000,000) and PostgreSQL's index-only scan 66 and 307 ms, but was priced at 65% of the serial scan. Default settings, 2 workers allowed, median of 5 runs: `count(DISTINCT author)` of 245,000 distinct values over 1,000,000 rows 166 ms to 95, 736,000 values over 3,000,000 rows 680 ms to 327, the plan PostgreSQL's with an index on the column; a column of fewer than 30,000 values keeps the scan.
- Improved: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY k DESC LIMIT n` over a key that grows with the table (a timestamp in insert order) reads the superblocks of each window of the walk from the window's best end, so a window that holds the rows of the page reads one unit and not every unit of the window in table order: `WHERE ts <= x ORDER BY ts DESC LIMIT 10` with x in the middle of a table of 3,000,000 rows (1,000,000, 100,000) ran 2.67 million instructions (1.92, 0.74) and runs 0.65 million (0.74, 0.74), 8 units read (5) become 1 (100,000 rows: 2 before and after). Test `col_topn_reverse_window`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.agg_force` priced every scan path at 0, so the path with workers and the one without tied and the planner kept the one without: a forced scan never got workers, and what was timed under it (`count(DISTINCT uuid)` over 1,000,000 rows, planned with 2 workers by default) was the serial scan. The paths now keep the order of their prices, a millionth of them, so the forced scan launches workers wherever the natural plan does.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the call of a transition function (`min`/`max` of text, `bool_and`, `variance`, `array_agg`, `string_agg`, `corr`, ...) at 1 operator (it was 4) plus the function's cost and the 2 of its argument: the 32 to 88 ns a row the scan measures is 3 to 7 operators in all, and a scan 10 to 50% faster than PostgreSQL's plan lost the choice. Default settings, 2 workers allowed, on top of the pricing of the wide columns: a numeric group key next to an int one 254 ms to 131 (PostgreSQL's plan 211) at 1,000,000 rows, 683 to 356 (474) at 3,000,000, 32 to 19 (37) at 100,000; `bit_and(int)` at 100,000 rows 11 ms to 3; of the 51 aggregates of a set of wide and narrow columns timed against both plans, the choice is the faster one for 50 (39 without it, 29 on the tip); the 4 cells of the matrix (254 shapes at 100,000, 1,000,000 and 3,000,000 rows) that change their plan take 979 ms in all against 510 ms, none is slower than PostgreSQL's plan.
- Fixed: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY k, expr LIMIT n` over a range of the sort key on an index with `order_columns` (`WHERE k >= x ORDER BY k, k * 2, id LIMIT 50`) failed with "sift_col sorted run is corrupted": the search of the run for the first entry of the range read the key through the buffer of the leaf, which holds the whole key of the unit when a sort expression follows it, and overwrote that; it reads the key from the unit's vector.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the scan of a wide column (a numeric without a precision, a uuid, an interval, a bytea) by what the scan does with its dictionary entries: an entry that is only copied (`array_agg`, `string_agg`, `count` of the column, a group key) costs 5 operators and one that a sum or an average of the column is added to in the same scan 25 (it was 60 to 180 for every use), and a group key of a wide column pays for its table of groups by the number of distinct values (45 operators an entry at 30,000 of them, growing with their count to the 0.42 power; it was free). The scan of `array_agg(uuid)` or `array_agg(numeric)` or of a numeric group key, 10 to 40% faster than PostgreSQL's plan and serial by design (the spelling of `1.0` and `1.00` depends on the order of the rows), was priced above it and lost the choice. Default settings, 2 workers allowed: 1,000,000 rows, `array_agg(vnb)` 171 ms to 109 (PostgreSQL's plan 201), `array_agg(vu)` 124 to 78 (141), a numeric key 148 to 107 and 197 to 144; 3,000,000 rows, 613 to 341, 527 to 229, 423 to 306 and 565 to 338; the 11 cells of the matrix (254 aggregate, key and filter shapes at 100,000, 1,000,000 and 3,000,000 rows) that change their plan take 2,824 ms in all against 1,676 ms, none is slower than PostgreSQL's plan.
- Improved: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT n` that reads a sorted run (`order_columns`) over conditions on integer, date and time columns that bound the sort key (`ts >= x`, `ts BETWEEN a AND b`, `ts = x`, an OR of ranges, the page after a key `(ts, id) > (x, y)`) reads a unit from the first entry of the range without running the unit's filters, and tests the conditions on the rows it takes (a condition on another column only where an equality of the key pins it; a unit in which the walk meets many rows that fail goes back to the filters); the search of the range probes where evenly spread keys would put the bound, after the zone of the unit has answered the units that the range holds or misses whole. Instructions a statement over 3,000,000 rows of a scattered timestamp, runs only (1,000,000 rows, 100,000 rows in brackets): `WHERE ts >= x ORDER BY ts LIMIT 10` 18.7 to 8.4 million (6.6 to 3.1, 1.0 to 0.65), the page after a key `LIMIT 50` 63.6 to 10.4 million (24.2 to 4.7, 3.6 to 1.9); EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows `Units Read Without Filters`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the cap of a CTE of a literal list's keys to the constants of its list (3.5) applied to every column the CTE returns, though the list says nothing of the values of a second column (the partner of a listed row, joined to the parents of the rows on that column); it applies to the column the list is of. The scan of an index cut by the key is priced for the keys PostgreSQL expects there again: on a fixture of 400,000 rows the scan costs 876 for the listed column and 1,547 for the partner column, where both cost 876. No plan of the 600 statements measured for the cap changes.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a request for the keys of a literal list (a CTE of ids kept to the keys a sub-select allows) priced the units its keys leave for the rows the keys own in the column's statistics counted as keys, so one key of many rows was priced as thirty and the scan of an index cut by the key (`key_cut`) as a read of a fifth of its units where it opens 3 of 149: the planner kept the normal plan, or chose the scan and started four workers for three units. The keys of such a CTE are no more than the constants of its list. Measured on 1,000,000 rows with the index cut by the key, 600 statements over seven shapes, one to a thousand keys, warm, 4 workers: the pick is within 1.2x of the faster of the normal and the scan plan (or 5 ms of it) for 99.0% of the statements, up from 86.8%; those that lose more than 2x and 50 ms fall from 3 to 1, and the milliseconds lost against the faster plan from 2,304 to 335. Of the 106 statements whose plan changes, 38 are faster by 20% or more and none slower (1,439 -> 517 ms in total; the 36 that go from the normal plan to the scan: 901 -> 184 ms, 6 to 90 ms -> 1.4 to 14 ms). On a plain index 54 plans change (the scan runs without workers: instructions equal, 416 -> 333 ms) and one statement of 1 ms takes the scan (0.7 -> 3.5 ms).
- Changed: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT n` that reads a sorted run (`order_columns`) over a range of the sort key (`ts >= x`, `ts BETWEEN a AND b`, `ts = x`, a row comparison of the page after a key, an OR of ranges) starts at the first entry of the range, found by two binary searches of the run, and no longer walks the entries before it, which fail the filter: `WHERE ts >= x ORDER BY ts LIMIT 10` over 1,000,000 rows of a scattered timestamp takes 6.1 million instructions where the plain index takes 17.8 and the whole run walk 56 (100,000 rows: 1.0 million, 2.2 and 6.0); a key of floating point or text, and a filter on another column, are read as before.
- Fixed: pg_sift: where PostgreSQL's count of a lookup is a default guess (a CTE or a sub-select of ids), the scan path reported to the plan above the groups of all the distinct values of the key, so a request for a few keys met a plan priced for thousands of groups (a hash or merge join over them that the forced scan did not need) and the planner kept the normal plan; it now reports them at the geometric mean of PostgreSQL's count and all of them, as the normal plan's price already takes the keys. Measured on 1,000,000 rows with the index cut by the key (`key_cut`), 600 statements over seven shapes, one to a thousand keys, warm, 4 workers: the pick is within 1.2x of the faster of the normal and the scan plan (or 5 ms of it) for 86.8% of the statements, up from 74.7%; those that lose more than 2x and 50 ms fall from 9 to 3, and the milliseconds lost against the faster plan from 4,853 to 2,304. Of the 73 statements whose plan changes, 52 are faster by 20% or more and none slower (5,551 -> 2,768 ms in total); on an index not cut by the key no pick changes (599 statements at 1,000,000 rows, 28 at 3,000,000), and at 3,000,000 rows on `key_cut` 6 of 28 plans change, all faster (15.0 -> 5.3 ms, 18.2 -> 6.9, 21.0 -> 13.2, 27.7 -> 19.4, 41.9 -> 32.7, 90.1 -> 77.5).
- Improved: pg_sift: a statement with a shared CTE whose verdict is the inline plan no longer plans the plan with the shared CTEs as well to read the size of the request from it: the verdict keeps the size of the inline plan too (the most rows its scans read) under a band of its own, and the hit plans the inline plan alone. On the 156 statements of the corpus over 1,000,000 rows, 84 fresh-backend hit plannings of 28 shapes and classes go from 78 to 72 million instructions on average (the 9 plans of the inline form drop from 94-171 to 81-137 million, 3.4 ms median each at about 3 million instructions a millisecond); the plan chosen is the one planned in full in all 84.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `CREATE INDEX` of a `sift_col` index with `hll_columns` and a leaf that is NULL in nearly every row (a soft-delete stamp, which keeps exception cubes) but no list of elements failed with "segment 1 is corrupted": the offset of the sketches counted a directory of exception element blobs that the segment does not hold; the index is built and the sketches are read (a table of 60,000 rows: `col_verify` passes and `approx_count_distinct` answers from the two units' sketches).
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the state and final function of each group of an aggregate that runs on PostgreSQL's own transition function at 20 operators (it was 150, the price of the sort of an ordered-set group, which keeps it): the 40 to 200 ns a group the scan measures for `min` of text, `bool_and`, `max` of a timestamp, `variance` and `array_agg` over 100,000 to 620,000 groups. A group-by on a column of hundreds of thousands of values with such aggregates kept PostgreSQL's plan where the scan is 2 to 3 times as fast: 1,000,000 rows, `count(*)`, `min(text)`, `max(timestamptz)`, `sum(numeric)` by an integer column of 621,000 values 753 ms to 397 ms, by a rounded float 682 to 221 ms, and by a column of 100,000 values (100,000-row tables) 25 to 7 ms. The 6 cells of the matrix that flip are all faster than PostgreSQL's plan.
- Fixed: pg_sift: with `sift.explain_refusals` on (or in `sift.explain_why`), a `min()` or `max()` that PostgreSQL plans as a `LIMIT 1` sub-select and whose scan arm lost left its arm in a list that outlived the statement, and the next plan of the session read the freed list: the backend crashed (segmentation fault) on the next `EXPLAIN` of the session (`EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT min(v4) FROM t` with `sift.enable_agg` on, then off). The list is dropped with the context it was made in. Test `agg_refusals`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices a row of an aggregate that runs on PostgreSQL's own transition function (`min`/`max` of text, `bool_and`, `variance`, `stddev`, `array_agg`, `string_agg`, `corr`, ...) at 4 operators plus the function's cost (it was 12), the 32 to 88 ns a row the scan measures; it priced the scan at four to five times its cost, and kept PostgreSQL's plan where the scan is 2 to 5 times as fast. Default settings, 1,000,000 rows, 4 groups: `min(vt)` 156 ms to 35 ms, `bool_and` 193 to 22, `variance(vf8)` 174 to 40; the shapes that flip, 41 of the 504 cells of the matrix that ran (254 aggregate, key and filter shapes at 1,000,000 and 100,000 rows, 2 workers allowed), take 1,032 ms in all against 210 ms; none is slower than PostgreSQL's plan.
- Improved: pg_sift: the scan of a numeric without a precision (or an interval, a uuid, a bytea: any wide column) fetches the dictionary entry of a row some rows before it reads it: the dictionary of a segment of millions of almost unique values does not fit the cache, and every row was two misses (the offset, then the image). `GROUP BY` 6 groups, `count`, `sum`, `avg` of 10,000,000 almost unique numbers in one segment, a new backend, no workers: 2,174 to 1,242 ms (8.99 to 5.10 billion cycles; instructions +1.8%); with 4 workers allowed and the scan forced 842 to 501 ms (PostgreSQL's plan 317 to 366 ms), 2,000,000 rows unchanged within the noise (a dictionary of that size is cached).
- Improved: pg_sift: a statement with a lookup table of a first-row child (`LEFT JOIN LATERAL (SELECT .. LIMIT 1)`) that no cache holds no longer reads every page of the table to ask a cache for it: the request for a cached table first looks for an entry of the same query in the backend and the shared memory.
- Added: pg_sift: a `sift_table` reads the rollups of the elements of its declared jsonb lists (`sift.table_set_paths`) that its units already carried and nothing read: a `GROUP BY` of the unnested elements by their leaves of few values with `count`, `sum`, `min` and `max` adds the tuples of each unit up instead of reading its elements, so a `GROUP BY` of an element key over 500,000 rows takes 0.121 ms where it took 22.977. The groups are not written differently, so they grow 0% and the writers cost 0%; `SET sift.agg_rollup_cost = 1000` reads the elements as before. Test `tbl_rollup`.
- Added: pg_sift: the cube of a `sift_table` keeps the minimum and maximum of each integer measure per group, as the cube of an index does by default (`cube_minmax`, column format minor 7): `GROUP BY g` with `min` and `max` of an integer column over 500,000 rows takes 0.140 ms where it took 2.860. The groups grow 0.7% (8 columns) to 3.1% (4 integer columns) and the writers spend 0.0% to 0.2% more instructions; the calendar cells of a date or timestamp column hold none, and a table written before keeps its cubes until a rewrite. `SET sift.col_format_minor = 6` writes none; a 3.2 library cannot read minor 7. Test `tbl_preagg_minmax`.
- Added: pg_sift: a `sift_table` keeps the key list or bitmap of each unit for its int2, int4 and date foreign key and `*_id` columns that are not unique (the default of `key_columns` for an index), and the sorted run and run head of each unit for its `timestamp` and `timestamptz` columns the rows follow (a run of any other takes more than 3% of a unit and is not written): `count(DISTINCT fk)` over 500,000 rows takes 0.053 ms where it took 2.10 (36.2M to 0.69M instructions), and `ORDER BY ts DESC LIMIT 10` 0.038 ms where it took 0.140. The groups grow 0.0% to 1.4% (500,000 rows, 7 columns) and the writers (INSERT, COPY, VACUUM and its merge) spend 0.0% to 1.6% more instructions; groups written before keep their format and are read as before, new and rewritten groups are column format minor 15 (lists) or 18 (run heads, which a 3.3 library cannot read). The top-N does not walk a run that is the rows in their own order (the walk read LIMIT 100 to 1,000 up to 37% more instructions than the unit). No setting: `SET sift.col_format_minor = 12` writes none.
- Fixed: pg_sift: with `sift.enable_agg = off` the planner lifts no more the rows of a CTE scan or of a sub-select scan (a floor made for the choice between the scan and the shared CTE): the normal plan is the plan of the 3.3 library, text and cost, where 3.4.0 planned another one for 92 of the 156 statements of a replay corpus (`Memoize` x3 became x10, `GroupAggregate` x8 became x5 in the largest one); the same 156 statements planned without the column index are identical in both libraries.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the units a lookup's keys leave were priced for the geometric mean of the keys PostgreSQL guesses for a lookup it has no statistics for (a CTE or a sub-select of ids) and the distinct values of the column, so one key was priced as tens of them and an index cut by the key (`key_cut`) kept the normal plan's btree probes where the scan reads a few units: the keys PostgreSQL expects are the keys priced where they leave a handful of units (an index cut by the key, or a key that owns a few units of a clustered table); where they leave more (an index not cut by the key reads tens of units for one key) the geometric mean stays, because the planner cannot tell a key of a few hundred rows from one of thousands and the scan is not worth its planning for the small ones. Measured on 1,000,000 rows, 600 statements over seven shapes, one to a thousand keys, warm, 4 workers: the pick is within 1.2x of the faster of the normal and the scan plan (or 5 ms of it) for 74.7% of the statements on a `key_cut` index, up from 67.2%; those that lose more than 2x and 50 ms fall from 32 to 9, and the milliseconds lost against the faster plan from 8,165 to 4,853.
- Improved: pg_sift: a correlated `LEFT JOIN LATERAL (SELECT .. LIMIT 1)` lookup whose key has no unique index, or whose condition reads the joined row alone, is read as a table once the child has run for an eighth of the rows of the table (or at once when a cache holds it): a key whose rows agree on the columns the query reads is answered from the table and a key whose rows differ runs the child, so the result is that of the child under every plan. `EXPLAIN` shows `First Row Lookups` and `First Row Keys Differ`; test `agg_first_row_dup`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the choice between a shared CTE and its inline form was kept for a statement whatever the size of the request (the constants of a dashboard move the rows its scans read by decades), and the cost guard compared plans of different variants, so a backend that met a size after another one took the plan of the other size: the same statement planned as two plans across fresh backends. A verdict now holds within 1.4 times the rows of the largest scan of an indexed table in the shared plan, the verdict of each size band is kept as well, and a hit that is far from the size of its verdict is planned in full (or from the verdict of its own band); an interleaved run of 44 plannings of eleven sizes gives the plan of each size planned alone, 0 of 44 differ (28 of 44 before).
- Changed: pg_sift: a partitioned table whose partitions are `sift_table`s runs its aggregates as `Custom Scan (SiftAgg)`, as one whose partitions carry a `sift_col` index does (the partitions of one kind, with their columns in the same order, are one source; the partition list, pruning, workers and the top-N work as for indexes): over 200,000 rows in two partitions, `GROUP BY` a 20-value column with `count(*)` and `sum` takes 0.20 ms and 0.6 million instructions instead of 32.0 ms and 350 million, a filtered `count(*)` 0.17 ms instead of 10.6 ms, `count(DISTINCT)` of a column under a filter 2.4 ms instead of 94 ms, and a top-5 by a column 0.17 ms instead of 29 ms; with two workers the first takes 0.21 ms instead of 15.1 ms.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the scan of a numeric without a precision (stored as images) for the workers that read it cold at more operators a row the more distinct values the column has past 2,000,000 (10 more for each million, up to 40 more): the dictionary of a segment outgrows the cache and each row is a miss, so a column of millions of almost unique values keeps PostgreSQL's plan with 4 or 8 workers allowed, where the scan took 1.4 to 2.3 times as long. `GROUP BY` 6 groups, `count`, `sum`, `avg`, default costs, 5,000,000 rows 257 / 266 ms (4 / 8 workers) to 140 / 135 ms, 10,000,000 rows in one segment 801 / 681 ms to 294 / 338 ms. Columns of fewer distinct values (a million rows of 30,000 prices keep the parallel scan: 36 ms against 61), the 2,000,000-row plans and the plans with 0 or 2 workers are unchanged.
- Changed: pg_sift: `count(DISTINCT x)` of a `timestamptz`, `float4`, `float8` or `uuid` column (a leaf with no set of its own) takes its ids from a combo, as the distinct values of a joined column do, and runs in the workers; it was a serial sort of the values (a `GROUP BY` of 8 groups over 1,000,000 rows, 2 workers allowed: `timestamptz` 675 to 201 ms, `float8` 656 to 242 ms, `float4` 605 to 238 ms, `uuid` 1,542 to 582 ms, and 2.5 to 0.9 billion instructions for the `timestamptz` one without workers; the scalar `count(DISTINCT uuid)` 1,472 to 465 ms against PostgreSQL's 388 ms). The counts are exact, as before; the planner prices the new path and keeps PostgreSQL's plan where it is cheaper. Test `agg_distinct_parallel`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a `sift_table` runs the text tests of an aggregate scan (`=`, `<>`, ranges, `IN`, `NOT IN`, `LIKE 'p%'`) on the encoded strings of its groups and uses them to skip groups, as `sift_col` does, with the same code: over a million rows a group-by filtered by `s = 'x'` takes 15 million instructions instead of 44 (`sift_col`: 15), and one filtered by a prefix of a high-cardinality text column 59 instead of 448 (`sift_col`: 504). `sift_col` is unchanged.
- Improved: pg_sift: a statement with a shared CTE whose scans cost less than the work they replace by more than the margin, and whose inline plan is still refused for its total cost, is no longer planned again at ten and a hundred times the rows (that check is for the close calls): two of the eight planner runs of such a statement without a cached verdict, 473 to 261 million instructions for a four-way `min` over categories, the plan the same on 156 statements at three request sizes.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a column scan no longer sends the rest of its rows to the heap check when a promotion of the write buffer (or any other change of the index directory that replaces no segment) runs while it is open: the directory version moved, the scan stopped trusting the visibility map for all its later units, and every selected row of every page was read from the heap. The scan now asks only whether the segments it opened are still in the directory, which a merge answers and a promotion does not. 3,000,000 rows, a wide grouped scan with one promotion in the middle: 155,388 to 22,139 rows and 38,592 to 5,924 heap pages checked (158,337 to 27,971 and 39,549 to 7,574; 192,221 to 33,441 and 48,000 to 9,144).
- Improved: pg_sift: a `LEFT JOIN LATERAL` over the elements of a list that holds a lookup of its own (`LEFT JOIN LATERAL (SELECT .. FROM jsonb_to_recordset(doc -> 'list') AS p(..) LEFT JOIN LATERAL (SELECT .. FROM lookup WHERE .. LIMIT 1) m ON true) e ON true`), the shape of a per-row score that keeps the rows with no element, is answered by the scan, with the placeholder of its columns reading NULL for a row with no element (they kept the normal plan, `from_shape`, which unnests every row): the two-level ranking of 3,000,000 rows (15,000 groups, all of them) takes 0.52 s against 3.2 s with 5 workers (654 ms against 3,022 ms and 4.1 against 14.4 billion instructions in one process; 1,000,000 rows 161 ms against 745 ms, 300,000 rows 90 against 222, 100,000 rows 33 against 99).
- Fixed: pg_sift: a group-by on a text column read through a dictionary stored in blocks looked up the dictionary entry of every row of a chunk, kept or not, so a filter that keeps 82 of the 39,000 rows of the units it reads decompressed the blocks of all the entries of those rows and went on to inflate the whole dictionary; the chunk now looks up the entries of the rows it keeps when it keeps fewer than half. The first statement of a backend that groups 82 rows by a 100,000-entry dictionary takes 1.1 ms where it took 9.5 (warm 0.7 ms, unchanged); the result and the plan are the same.
- Improved: pg_sift: opening the head of a stored unit validates the bitmap of each sparse page 8 bytes at a time, where it counted the bits of each byte: a unit of a table that churned (gaps in its pages) costs 40,000 instead of 86,000 instructions to open, and the grouped sum of 1,000,000 orders after churn (store question 3, 196 units, 57 of them dirty) runs 10.9 million instructions instead of 13.5 million (-19%), with `sift.agg_dead_delta` on or off; no format change, no setting (scripts/instr-budget.sh holds the number: 908,000 to 435,000 instructions for 12 units)
- Changed: pg_sift: a CTE of the keys a request names (a literal list kept to the keys a sub-select allows, `WHERE id IN (7, 12) AND id IN (SELECT ..)`) is estimated at the keys its list is worth in the statistics of the column of the indexed table it is joined to, where PostgreSQL multiplied the two selectivities and priced the plan that keeps the CTE for one key: a request for one key that owns 6% of 3,000,000 rows planned the normal plan at 3,400 ms where the scan took 363 ms, and one for 100 keys 3,159 ms where it took 397 ms (warm medians of 3 rounds); a key of the mean size stays at one, so the requests that the normal plan answers in a few milliseconds keep it.
- Changed: pg_sift: a scan that reads a numeric without a precision (or any other wide column: interval, uuid) goes to the workers of a parallel plan unless a group key shows it: the counts, the sums of an expression, `stddev` and `variance` of a bare `numeric`, and a `uuid` key, took one process (a group key of the numeric, `min`, `max` and the lists of it still do: they show which of 1.0 and 1.00 came first). 1,000,000 rows, 8 workers and `seq_page_cost = 100`: `count(n) GROUP BY g` 100 to 44 ms, `stddev(n)` 102 to 36 ms, `sum(n * 2)` 112 to 46 ms, `GROUP BY uuid_col` 41 to 17 ms (the normal plan: 39 to 52 ms). `EXPLAIN (VERBOSE)` says `Serial Because` for the reasons the planner has (a function that is not parallel safe, a group key of equal numerics, a generic aggregate: DISTINCT, ORDER BY, no combine function) as it did for the state of the aggregate. Test `agg_wide_sum_dict`; the diff fuzz family `widew`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a `CREATE INDEX` (also `CONCURRENTLY`) or `REINDEX` of a `sift_col` index with `key_cut` builds a table of any size with the memory it has: when the runs fill the 121 segments of the directory the build merges the oldest of the lowest level into one (the streaming merge of the key-cut segments, a row written once for each level it reaches) and gives the pages of the inputs back, instead of stopping before the table is read; the check that counted the runs and the automatic cut that was not chosen for it are gone (6,000,000 rows of 480 bytes at 64 MB with 1 worker: 114 segments, 10 s, 383 MB of segments against 381 MB for one run built with 12 GB, 54 MB of private RSS a process; the file keeps about 8% of free pages that a REINDEX with more memory does not)
- Improved: pg_sift: the scan's start is priced from what it does: a top-N that reads the order array opens no unit (the executor starts, the array of each segment is opened and its first chunk decoded: 25 to 35 us measured), and an aggregate with no groups and no search starts at 0.1 ms, not 0.25 ms. The scan now wins over the normal plan's Sort or Aggregate on 100,000 to 300,000 rows: `WHERE grp = x ORDER BY ts DESC LIMIT 10` 0.23 to 0.10 ms, with a range of `ts` 0.23 to 0.09 ms, keyset page 0.28 to 0.10 ms, `max(ts) WHERE grp = x` 0.36 to 0.10 ms (100,000 rows), `max(ts) WHERE grp = x AND ts < t` 0.69 to 0.13 ms (300,000 rows); where the scan has no unit to skip (`count(*) WHERE k = x` on a column of 100,000 distinct values) the normal plan keeps winning. No setting, no format change.
- Added: docs: [docs/COL-TAM.md](docs/COL-TAM.md) section 13 lists the features of `sift_col` and what `sift_table` offers of each (works, partial, missing, n/a with the reason).
- Improved: pg_sift: an ungrouped aggregate that the pre-aggregates do not answer (a filter on a column the units do not summarize, a unit with dead rows) counts and sums the rows of a chunk in one pass over its selection, where it scattered each row through an array of group ids that are all 0: `SELECT count(*), sum(v), sum(m)` over 1,000,000 rows (`sum(v)` an integer, `sum(m)` a `numeric(12,2)`, `sift.agg_preagg = off`) runs 54.9 million instructions instead of 90.9 million (-40%; 3.3.0: 86.1 million); a grouped aggregate is unchanged; no format change, no setting (scripts/instr-budget-ungrouped.sh holds the number: 18,136,000 to 10,987,000 instructions for 200,000 rows)
- Improved: pg_sift: an ungrouped `sum` of a `numeric` expression that the pre-aggregates do not answer adds the sum of each block of 512 values with one kernel (`sum_i64`, written once for AVX2, AVX-512 and the scalar level) instead of scattering each value through the group ids: the ungrouped `SELECT count(*), sum(v), sum(m)` over 200,000 rows (`sum(m)` a `numeric(12,2)`, `sift.agg_preagg = off`) runs 9.99 million instructions instead of 10.99 million (-9%); a grouped sum is unchanged; no format change, no setting (scripts/instr-budget-ungrouped.sh holds the number)

## 3.4.2 - 2026-10-26

- Fixed: pg_sift: the extension builds against PostgreSQL 19 again: the planner's read of a lookup's plan down to its inputs named `UpperUniquePath`, which 19 folded into `UniquePath`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the key-cut test of the few-keys rule (an index cut by the key leaves a few-keys lookup to the scan) counts the keys PostgreSQL expects of the lookup, as the rule itself does, and no more the mean between those and the column's distinct values that prices the scan: a request for one key as a CTE or sub-select of ids (estimated at one row, priced as several) kept the normal plan's price on an index cut by the key, and a statement with a shared CTE took a plan split into one scan per arm, 1.3 to 5 times slower, or a lookup by the index of the table.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a lookup of a few keys no longer keeps the normal plan's price when the index is cut by the key (`key_cut`): the keys leave a handful of units there, so the scan reads them in a millisecond or two while the probes of the btree cost the time of the rows of the largest keys. An index not cut by the key keeps the choice it had.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a CTE of the keys a request names (a literal list kept to the keys a sub-select allows, `WHERE id IN (7, 12) AND id IN (SELECT ..)`) is estimated at the keys its list is worth in the statistics of the column of the indexed table it is joined to, where PostgreSQL multiplied the two selectivities and priced every plan that keeps the CTE for one key: a request for one key that owns 6% of 3,000,000 rows planned the normal plan at 3,400 ms where the scan took 363 ms, and one for 100 keys 3,159 ms where it took 397 ms (warm medians of 3 rounds); a key of the mean size stays at one, so the requests that the normal plan answers in a few milliseconds keep it (`agg_cte_key_list`).
- Fixed: pg_sift: the few-keys rule no longer keeps the normal plan's price for a lookup whose keys PostgreSQL estimates at a fraction of the smaller list a join of its plan reads (ten ids semi-joined to forty: one, or two): the plan of the lookup is read down to its inputs, a CTE as its query, so a request for ten ids takes the scan instead of an index nested loop that reads the rows of the largest id.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the scan of a numeric without a precision (stored as images) for the workers that read it cold: a worker inflates and indexes the dictionary of images of every unit it takes, where a backend that repeats the scan finds them decoded, so the parallel scan of a column of almost unique values scaled to 1.7x with 3 processes while PostgreSQL's plan scales to 3.4x. `GROUP BY` 6 groups, `count`, `sum`, `avg` of 2,000,000 almost unique values (2 / 4 / 8 workers allowed, default costs): the planner took the scan at 103 / 91 / 86 ms against PostgreSQL's 96 / 70 / 70 ms (up to 31% slower), it now keeps PostgreSQL's plan (79 / 62 / 62 ms); 500,000 rows 27.7 against 24 ms, now 24 to 26 ms. Without workers the scan is still taken (135 against 213 ms). No effect when `parallel_setup_cost` is 0.
- Fixed: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT n` that reads a sorted run (`order_columns`) over a filter that few rows pass (`WHERE ts >= x ORDER BY ts LIMIT 10` on 1,000,000 rows of a scattered timestamp) read the sort key of every entry it walked by position, 83 instructions each, where 3.3.0 decoded the unit's column; it reads the keys of the rows that pass only: 113 million instructions a statement (3.4.0) become 56 million, 3.3.0's 59 million.
- Fixed: pg_sift: an `UPDATE` of a row of a `sift_table` that is the referencing table of a foreign key failed with "don't have transaction information for this type of tuple" in 3.3.0, 3.4.0 and 3.4.1, whatever column it changed: the integrity trigger asks the slot of the old row whether the current transaction made it, and the table's slot answered with the error of a virtual slot. The slot now answers from the xmin of the row, as it already did for `xmin`. `INSERT`, `DELETE`, and every statement on the referenced table were not affected; `tbl_foreign_keys` runs them all, with the three actions, on heap and `sift_table` pairs.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `CREATE INDEX` of a `sift_col` index with `hll_columns` and a leaf that is NULL in nearly every row (a soft-delete stamp, which keeps exception cubes) but no list of elements failed with "segment 1 is corrupted", and the same index created before its rows could be written by an `INSERT` and then refuse `VACUUM` and every scan of the segment: the offset of the sketches counted a directory of exception element blobs that the segment does not hold. The index is built and the sketches are read (a table of 60,000 rows: `col_verify` passes and `approx_count_distinct` answers from the two units' sketches); an index already built with the fault needs a `REINDEX`.
- Improved: pg_sift: the heap check of the rows of an index that no VACUUM covers (a table loaded and not vacuumed, or one an old transaction holds back: no page is all-visible) tests a row without a call through a test of its TID and without the call of `TransactionIdPrecedes`: the grouped sum of 1,000,000 rows with 1% dead rows and no VACUUM (`SELECT k, sum(v), count(*) .. GROUP BY k`, PostgreSQL 18, user instructions of the backend) runs 164.5 million instructions instead of 204.4 million (-19%; 3.3.0: 180.2 million, 3.4.1: 206.3 million), `SELECT count(*), sum(v), sum(m)` 197.1 million instead of 237.0 million (-17%); a table with the pages all-visible does not check its rows and is unchanged; no format change, no setting (scripts/instr-budget-vet.sh holds the number: 20,024,000 to 12,030,000 instructions for 200,000 rows)
- Fixed: pg_sift: a statement with workers failed with `ExtensibleNodeMethods "SiftAggRows" was not registered` ("parallel worker") when a scan with workers whose filter starts a sub-plan (`x IN (SELECT ..) OR ..`) sat beside another sub-plan of the statement that holds a `Custom Scan (SiftAggRows)` node, such as a CTE of per-row aggregates grouped with `count(DISTINCT ..)`: the workers registered the `SiftAgg` node and not the `SiftAggRows` one when they read the sub-plans of the statement; both are registered now (test agg_rows)

## 3.4.1 - 2026-10-26

- Fixed: pg_sift: a request for one or a few keys of a lookup (a CTE of ids joined to the table) took the scan at a price far above the normal plan's, and lost: the 3.4.0 charge on the normal plan of an aggregate over the elements of a jsonb array (the excess of the table's rows over the rows PostgreSQL expects, and of the keys over its count) read the rows the keys pick as a lost estimate, hundreds of times the plan's price for a single key, so the scan won whatever it cost. A lookup of up to four keys, as PostgreSQL counts them, keeps its btree probes at the price they are planned for; longer lists keep the charge. Measured (1,000,000 rows, one key of about 200 rows): the normal grouped aggregate was charged 64,555.85 where its plan is priced 48.29, and lost to the scan priced 3,507.02; with 3,000,000 rows the grouped aggregate over a jsonb map for one small or one medium key took 8.8 and 12.4 ms in the scan and takes 0.9 and 6.0 ms now (the normal plan takes 1.1 and 7.0 ms), and a workload of single-key lookups that took 340 ms each takes between 2 and 6 ms. Limits: one key of a very large entity takes the normal plan, as in 3.3 (3,000,000 rows: 13.9 ms in the scan, 20.9 ms in the plan), and lookups of 10 keys or more keep the charge, so the scan still wins there (22 ms for 10 keys where the normal plan takes 76 ms); a choice made at run time is 3.5 work (`agg_few_keys_pick`).
- Fixed: pg_sift: a `CREATE INDEX` (also `CONCURRENTLY`) or `REINDEX` of a `sift_col` index with `key_cut` on a table whose rows made more runs than the 121 segments of the directory with `maintenance_work_mem` failed after reading as many rows as the runs held ("needs more than 60 segments", 136,750,000 rows at 64 MB with 1 worker): the build now holds the rows of up to 32 superblocks before it reads the table, counts the runs, uses fewer parallel workers when that makes them fit (6,000,000 rows of 580 bytes at 64 MB: 2 participants failed at 60 segments each, serial builds in 52 runs, 28 s), and otherwise stops at once with the `maintenance_work_mem` it needs; the automatic decision gives up a cut that does not fit with a `NOTICE` (`col_key_cut`, `col_key_cut_auto`). Limits: an explicit `key_cut` build of a table of wide rows still needs `maintenance_work_mem` of about (workers + 1) x 2 or 3 GB (the message names it), and a table that needs more runs than the directory holds at the memory given cannot be built with `key_cut` until the runs are merged inside the build, which comes in 3.5.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a session that used the column aggregate pushdown, a partitioned table, a shared CTE or a `sift_table` took up to four of the ten relcache callback slots PostgreSQL gives a backend, one for each cache, and the session ended with `FATAL: out of relcache_callback_list slots` when the libraries loaded next to pg_sift (a distributed-table or time-series extension, a scheduler, ...) had taken the rest. pg_sift takes none now: the partition bounds and facts follow the rows of `pg_class` through syscache callbacks (sixty-four slots of their own), a `sift_table` notices the invalidation of its relation by the `rd_amcache` mark the relcache frees with it, and the CTE verdicts, whose keys carry the size, file and indexes of each table, are no longer dropped by an invalidation. Measured on PostgreSQL 18: the server takes 2 of the ten slots, `citus.so` 1 at load and 4 more on the first query when the `citus` extension is created in the database, `pg_cron` 1 and `timescaledb` 1, so the second cache of pg_sift was the eleventh registration; the test `relcache_slots` finds 8 free slots without pg_sift and 4 with 3.4.0, 8 and 8 now. The ten slots are shared by every library, so another library can still use them up: with the `citus` extension created, `timescaledb`, `pg_cron` and the server leave one spare.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a statement whose filter is a set of ranges on the zones of a `sift_col` index (the index with `key_cut`) no longer starts the setup of a scan for each morsel: the setup is made once for the scan, and an index with many units no longer pays about 0.3 s for each statement (a typical scan of the lab went from 8.3 ms down to 1.6 ms; an index without `key_cut` gains in proportion to its units, and `scripts/instr-budget-zone.sh` bounds the pruning of the case).
- Fixed: pg_sift: a filter on a text column (`LIKE`, a function of the column) is evaluated once for each entry of the dictionary of the column before the scan reads a row, and that loop could not be interrupted: `statement_timeout` and a cancel waited for the whole loop (about 200 ms over 2,800,000 rows, seconds with a dictionary of a hundred thousand long names and an expression that costs microseconds an entry; the test `interrupt_dict_filter` ends a 3,192 ms statement in under a second). The evaluation checks for interrupts now.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the first N rows of each group asked with a window function (`row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY k ORDER BY o)` and `rn <= N`, N > 1) ran in the scan with the groups' arrays when its plan priced less than the window's, which left out the work above the scan: the executor spends 1.4 to 2 us on each group to unnest its rows (PostgreSQL prices the rescan of a function scan at the rows it returns, 0.11), so 6,263 groups of the top 3 rows cost 12.3 ms there, priced 4.5. The plan of the arrays now pays 0.9 for each group it unnests (360 operator costs). 1,000,000 rows, 31,675 of them in range, 6,263 groups: the window plan 26.2 ms, the arrays 32.8 ms (was chosen, priced 17,693 against 18,857); with 30 or 40 groups the scan stays (22.4 ms against 33.2); 100,000 rows unchanged (2.3 ms). Test `agg_topn_rownumber`.

## 3.4.0 - 2026-10-25

- Changed: pg_sift: the byte budget of the cube of a `sift_col` unit counts what the cube holds (every measure of a kept dimension, with its minimum and maximum) and is 40% of the unit's encoded bytes, at least 8 kB, where it was 2% of the first three measures, so an index of sixteen columns whose dimensions have 50 to 200 values keeps the dimensions in every unit and answers a `GROUP BY` of any of them from the pre-aggregates: the last of eight dimensions of 50 values over 500,000 rows goes from 2.3 ms to 0.28 ms (the normal plan takes 63 ms), the index grows by 8.9% for the cube instead of 6.7% at 1,000,000 rows (at most 41% for 32 columns of 200 values) and the index of the store table is the same file. Dimensions of alike size keep the order of the columns, so units do not keep other ones each, the calendar cells of the dates share 10% as built, and a column the cube holds in every unit no longer keeps a key list (`key_columns`). Segments written afterwards; `REINDEX` or `sift.col_refresh(index)` rewrite the older ones. No format change.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `count(DISTINCT d)` of a `date` column that is not among the sixteen dimensions of the cube, but whose calendar cells the units keep, counted the months (or years) of the cells instead of the days: 14 for a column of 400 days over 14 months, with `Units From Pre-aggregates` of every unit. The count reads the histogram of the column itself, and the units of a column without one are scanned.
- Changed: pg_sift: the sum and average of a `numeric` column without a precision (read through a dictionary of the unit's values) run in the workers of a parallel scan, where they kept the scan in one process: the scan was then priced 10,086 against 16,063 for PostgreSQL's plan, which runs with the default 2 workers, and was slower than it (1,000,000 rows, 16 groups: 70 ms against 52 ms). A sum that leaves the 38-digit integer form in a worker (NaN, Infinity, past 38 digits or 128 bits) crosses to the leader as a numeric and is added there with the same arithmetic; the sums of an expression of a nullable input take the same route. 1,000,000 rows: 33 ms against 55 ms for the normal plan at the default 2 workers (leader instructions 743M to 293M), 30 ms against 50 ms with 8 workers and the page costs of a large database; 100,000 rows unchanged (7.5 ms). Test `agg_wide_sum_dict`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a top-N scan for each key an enclosing query sets (`CROSS JOIN LATERAL (.. WHERE device = d.device ORDER BY ts DESC LIMIT 3)`, a correlated scalar sub-select with `ORDER BY .. LIMIT`) is the `SiftAgg` top-N of the rows of the key, started over for each value, and no longer a read of the whole table for each: 20 devices over 1,000,000 readings with no btree on the key take 1.1 ms where the normal plan takes 739 ms; a top-N below a few superblocks reads its units for each value (100,000 rows: 2.4 ms for 20 devices, 60 ms before). The scan has no joins, partitions or window functions then, and a text key keeps the normal plan. No format change.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `date_trunc(unit, tstz, 'UTC')` and `tstz AT TIME ZONE 'UTC'` (and `GMT`) grouped by days of the UTC clock in a session whose time zone defines that name itself (the POSIX zone `UTC+3` has the abbreviation `UTC` at -03, which PostgreSQL reads first on 18 and later), so the scan and the calendar cells gave other groups than the normal plan there; the planner and the executor now check the name against the session zone (a plan made in another zone included) and evaluate the key for each value where it is not the UTC. The names with a slash (`Etc/UTC`) and every other session are unchanged.
- Changed: pg_sift: a `sift_table` reads only the rows its filters kept of a unit that keeps few (`read_sel`), and goes on with those rows alone when they are fewer than an eighth of it (`narrow`), as `sift_col` does: over a million rows the single-value equality that kept one row in a thousand takes 5.5 million instructions, from 13.4 (`sift_col`: 6.5). `sift_col` is unchanged.
- Changed: pg_sift: a `sift_table` decodes the columns of a unit straight into the executor's buffers in their own vector type, with the code `sift_col` uses (`sift_aggsel_read_lane`), instead of building a vector of keys, converting it and copying it: over a million rows an unfiltered group-by goes from 91 to 72 million instructions (`sift_col`: 73), and a filtered one with three conjuncts from 72.5 to 53 (`sift_col`: 54). `sift_col` is unchanged.
- Changed: pg_sift: the dictionary of a text column that a segment stores in blocks (column format minor 14, dictionaries of 1,024 strings or more that are not stored whole) is not inflated whole by the first statement of a backend that groups by the column: the blocks of the strings of the groups the request keeps are decompressed one at a time, and the dictionary is loaded whole, and kept for the backend, once those blocks add up to an eighth of the dictionary's (`count(DISTINCT text)`, the key of a group by or a lookup of the entries are read this way; a predicate on the text, a top-N, `ROLLUP` or any use that reads the dictionary whole load it when it reads). On a fixture of 1,000,000 rows with two text dictionaries of about 97,000 strings of 25 bytes each, a request over 82 rows grouped by one of them, forced scan, new backend: the first statement runs 1.0 ms where it ran 5.6 ms with the default `dict_stored` and 2.6 ms with `dict_stored = 17` (51.3M instructions to 7.1M, and 9.7M to 6.5M), so the stored form no longer pays on it. Test `agg_dict_blocks_lazy`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a `uuid` column of a `sift_table` is stored as a dictionary of its values, as in a `sift_col` index, and its top-N (`ORDER BY <uuid column> LIMIT n`) reads the codes of the stored dictionary: over 200,000 rows 8.5 ms become 2.7 ms (1,024 distinct values: 6.9 ms become 0.9 ms); the table grows by 0.2% with unique values and shrinks by a third with 1,024 random ones, and writing the groups costs 1% to 3% more. Groups written before stay readable and become dictionaries when rewritten.
- Changed: pg_sift: `p ->> 'k' IS [NOT] NULL` and `count(p ->> 'k')` of a member of the elements of `jsonb_array_elements(doc -> 'list')` (the way `jsonb_to_recordset` was already read), declared typed by the index (`list[]{k float8}`), are the null test and the count of the typed leaf of the member, as the null test of a member of the document is: `->>` is NULL for a missing key and a JSON null, the leaf too. A member that holds a value the leaf cannot store keeps the normal plan, as every read of it does. 1,000,000 events with `count(p ->> 'score')`, `count(*)` and `avg((p ->> 'score')::float8)` of the parts that have a score take 11.3 ms where PostgreSQL's plan takes 583 ms (`elem_member_null`); 200,000 documents of 1 to 9 elements, `WHERE p ->> 'score' IS NOT NULL` 3.6 ms against 404 ms, `count(p ->> 'score')` 0.5 ms against 381 ms. No format change. Test `agg_elem_member_null`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the aggregate scan reads a leaf for the surviving rows only when fewer than a thirty-second of the unit's rows still count (it was an eighth): over a million rows, with a filter that keeps 6% of the rows, a group-by takes 18.5 million instructions instead of 30.6, and one that keeps 0.1% still takes 6.4; between 3% and 12% the gather cost more than it saved.
- Improved: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT n` scan with n above 32 over an integer, date or timestamp column of an index with `order_columns` answers a superblock (and a unit) from the head of its sorted run once the heap holds n rows and the last entry of the head is after the heap's bar: no row after the head can enter the result, so the unit is not read. `ORDER BY ts DESC LIMIT 1000` over 3,000,000 rows with `ts` in no order takes 2.6 ms, not 4.2 ms (3.0 ms, not 3.9 ms when the rows are written by `(tenant, ts)`), and the cost of the units read is no longer linear in the rows (slope 0.30, was 0.42); the index and the answers do not change.
- Changed: pg_sift: `date_trunc`, `extract` and `date()` (`::date`) of a `timestamptz` in the session's time zone are grouped by the step of the zone's clock (a second, minute, hour or day) the scan numbers from the rows, as it does for a `timestamp`, and the expression is evaluated once per step and not per row: the offsets of the zone over the span of the data are read when the executor starts (PostgreSQL's own zone rules, so daylight saving time, 30 and 45-minute offsets and the offsets of local mean time give the rows of the normal plan, and a cached plan follows `SET TIME ZONE`); `date_trunc` to a second, minute or hour keeps the offset of the instant, so the repeated hour of a daylight saving change gives two groups as in PostgreSQL. A day of a zone with daylight saving time over 3,000,000 rows takes 20.8 ms and 284 million instructions (95 instructions a row) where the scan evaluated the key per row, 478 ms and 5.8 billion instructions, and PostgreSQL's plan 533 ms (1,000,000 rows: 8.0 ms, 164 ms, 175 ms). A call or unit that is not a step of the clock (`epoch`, `second`, `julian`, a zone named in the call) keeps the normal evaluation. No setting, no format change.
- Improved: pg_sift: on arm64 the chunk codec runs the fused range filter over bit-packed values (`unpack_range32`), the expansion of runs and the float4 keys of a decimal-scaled chunk with NEON kernels, bit-identical to the scalar ones: 4096 values on an arm64 core take 1750 to 313 ns (the range filter at 3 bits a value), 1125 to 273 ns (run expansion) and 1375 to 719 ns (float4 keys); the kernels that did not beat the scalar loop on that CPU (`max32`, the prefix sums, `scale32/64`, the float8 and float4 conversions) stay scalar.
- Added: pg_sift: `CREATE INDEX` on a `sift_col` index says in a NOTICE which foreign key columns of the table are not columns of the index: a join on them keeps the normal plan (`join_condition`), and the scan cannot filter the table by the keys of the rows another table's filters keep. The TPC-H-like cases declare their foreign keys and index the join keys, and `tpch_q3`, `tpch_q10` and `tpch_q12` then run on the scan (1,000,000 lineitem rows: 69, 115 and 64 ms for PostgreSQL's plan, 18, 23 and 13 ms with the lookup tables kept from an earlier query); the first query after a write loads those tables, and `tpch_q3_cold`, `tpch_q10_cold` and `tpch_q12_cold` time that run (31, 23 and 38 ms against 73, 112 and 60 ms: the planner prices the load, and the scan takes the cheaper table as the scanned one). Test `col_key_columns_auto`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the ordered output of an aggregate scan (the groups emitted in the order the parent sorts by) kept its description of the order in an array of the planner's stack that was gone when the plan was made, so a scan with a semi or anti join under an `ORDER BY` or a window read garbage ("operator 0 is not a valid ordering operator", a floating-point exception, AddressSanitizer stack-use-after-return); the array lives as long as the paths. It also builds on PostgreSQL 17, which has no `SortGroupClause.reverse_sort` (the direction is the sort operator's there).
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the test of an IN list by the structure that makes it: a hash table of values spread over a wide key costs a row 5 to 10 ns (a byte table in the cache 1, a bit table 2, a byte table wider than the cache 10), where every list cost the same fraction of a nanosecond. A list of 300 to 1,000 values spread over a key of 1,000,000 rows took the scan, which ran in 7.6 to 11.3 ms (51M to 63M instructions) where the btree plan runs in 0.3 to 1.0 ms; it now keeps the btree plan. Test `agg_in_list_cost`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the dictionary of a text column that a segment stores in blocks (column format minor 14, dictionaries of 1,024 strings or more that are not stored whole) is not inflated whole by the first statement of a backend that groups by the column: the blocks of the strings of the groups the request keeps are decompressed one at a time, and the dictionary is loaded whole, and kept for the backend, once those blocks add up to an eighth of the dictionary's (`count(DISTINCT text)`, the key of a group by or a lookup of the entries are read this way; a predicate on the text, a top-N, `ROLLUP` or any use that reads the dictionary whole load it when it reads). On a fixture of 1,000,000 rows with two text dictionaries of about 97,000 strings of 25 bytes each, a request over 82 rows grouped by one of them, forced scan, new backend: the first statement runs 1.0 ms where it ran 5.6 ms (51.3M instructions to 7.1M). Test `agg_dict_blocks_lazy`.
- Changed: pg_sift: `sift.table_set_paths` says, in a NOTICE, when the column groups of the table written before the declaration may keep the column as one leaf; its paths are used once every group has their leaves (`VACUUM FULL` rewrites them), and until then the plan stays the normal one.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a sum or average of numerics that came to exactly 2^127 - 1 (170141183460469231731687303715884105727, at any scale) was returned as NaN: the integer of a sum, 128 bits wide, was read as the NaN the numeric programs mark with that value. A `numeric(38,0)` column with two values of 99999999999999999999999999999999999999 and 70141183460469231731687303715884105728, a `numeric` without a precision holding the value, and a sum of products that ends there gave NaN where PostgreSQL gives the number; the sums convert their integer without the NaN reading now (test `agg_sum_int128_max`). No format change.
- Changed: pg_sift: the fixed cost of opening a scan in the planner's price is 100,000 operator costs (0.25 ms), where it was 210,000: a lookup of 500 to 700 rows in a table of 20,000 to 1,000,000 was left to the btree plan (a scan of 50 to 100 us against 190 to 370 us) because the scan was charged 0.5 ms before it read a row. The cost bands of the plan-choice gate are re-recorded for the shapes whose scan price falls.
- Improved: pg_sift: a comparison of two columns of one row (`l_shipdate < l_commitdate`, `end_at > start_at`: int2, int4, int8, date, timestamp, float4 and float8 columns of one type) is a predicate of the scan, evaluated on the vectors of both columns by one kernel per SIMD level (AVX2, AVX-512, NEON) with PostgreSQL's float order and the NULL rule, not one expression resolved for each tuple: the five filters of the TPC-H-like Q12 on `lineitem` (`tpch_q12`, 1,000,000 rows, scan forced) 220 to 4.5 ms and 832 to 40 instructions a row, a single column comparison 64 to 4.2 ms (425 to 31 instructions a row; 76 with the scalar kernel); 4.9 ms on an arm64 core.
