- Changed: pg_sift: the lookup table of a join (the rows of a joined table that the scan keeps in a hash by key) is loaded from the leaves of a `sift_col` index of the table when one holds the columns the join reads (the keys, the columns of the expressions, plain integer, date, timestamp, text and numeric(p <= 18) columns), where it was read from the heap: the units are decoded a unit at a time, the rows the index says are visible get a tuple of the columns, and the table's own conditions of the form `column <op> constant` are tested on the decoded leaves before a tuple is made, which drops a condition that tests nothing more. A table with no such index, a unit holding a value the leaf cannot store (a NaN in a numeric) or a load by an index scan keeps the heap. `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` shows `Lookup Column Loads`; `sift.agg_lookup_columns = off` turns it off. `lookup_columns` (1,000,000 orders joined to a table of 200,000 rows that the query loads) takes 23.3 ms where the heap load took 33.5 ms, against 152 ms for PostgreSQL's plan; the load alone of the fact-first TPC-H-like Q3 and Q10 joins (400,000 lineitem rows) is 102 M to 41 M instructions.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.explain_why` for `ORDER BY .. LIMIT` told that the top-N scan was used when the plan was the b-tree's: the arm compared its path with the whole cost of the ordered paths, before the LIMIT was costed (an index that gives the order reads only the rows asked for). The row now waits for the final paths and, when the b-tree wins, names the reason `cost` with both costs (`the scan costs 3124.66, the normal plan with the LIMIT 19.11`).
- Changed: pg_sift: the build, promotion and forced rebuild of an index with `key_cut` sort the rows by the key copied next to each reference with an inlined comparison, and cut the units from the sorted references without copying the rows into a window first (two copies of every row less): `CREATE INDEX .. WITH (key_cut = 'ts')` of 200,000 rows with a scattered `ts` takes 1.53 billion instructions, not 2.18 billion (a page-cut index: 1.23 billion), and the build CPU is 1.4 to 2.0 times a page-cut index's at 800,000 rows, where it was 1.6 to 2.9. A merge of segments and the promotion of a write buffer copy a row without arrays leaf by leaf, not through a decoded row: a forced `sift.col_merge` after churn of 200,000 rows takes 0.58 billion instructions on a page-cut index, not 0.70, and 0.86 billion with `key_cut`, not 1.22. No format change.
- Changed: pg_sift: the plan in full that checks a kept choice between a shared CTE and its inline plan (or between the flattened form and the one as written) comes every 8th hit of an entry the first time and, each time it confirms the choice, after twice as many hits (8, 16, 32, 64), where it came every 8th hit always; a plan in full that changes the choice starts at 8 again. A statement of 27 KB repeated in one backend: the planning of 70 runs averaged 6.1 to 6.7 ms (median 5.6) where it averaged 8.3 to 9.2 ms (median 6.1 to 7.1), and the plans in full over 20 ms went from 8 (184 to 231 ms in all) to 1 to 3 (26 to 75 ms). The name of the shared table of the choices changed (`sift_agg_verdicts_2`).
- Changed: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT n` scan over a `float4` or `float8` key hands the rows of a unit to the heap through the min/max and compare kernels as it does for an integer key (a float key was tested and pushed row by row): `ORDER BY value DESC LIMIT 10` over 1,000,000 readings no longer reads every row of the units whose best value cannot pass the n-th; the order is PostgreSQL's (`-0` equals `0`, `NaN` is the largest, the infinities), no option, no format change.
- Changed: pg_sift: the sum and average of a product of two decimal expressions whose digits together are 39 to 48 (`price * (1 - disc) * (1 + tax)` of three `decimal(15,2)` is 47) is added by the scan, as the sums of up to 38 digits are: each pair is multiplied in 256 bits and added to the 128-bit sum with the number of times it wrapped, where the product went through `numeric_mul` for every row (`sum_product_47`, 1,000,000 rows, six aggregates of a pricing summary: 466 ms in PostgreSQL's plan, 40 ms now; a scan that evaluated the product per row took 830 ms against 723). A product of more digits, a `DISTINCT` sum and a `min`, `max` or `count(DISTINCT)` of it keep the normal plan; a sum whose carries pass 2^63 (2^31 rows of the largest value of the 48 digits) raises an error. Test `agg_sum_product_exact`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the merge of segments of an index with `key_cut` copies each row once, from the unit it was decoded into to the unit it is written to, where it copied it three times; a forced merge of six segments of a 600,000-row table is 20% shorter (1.82 to 1.48 s and 2.49 to 1.96 s for the same table at two sizes). The build and the promotion use the same cut and are unchanged.
- Changed: pg_sift: a `sift_table` applies the integer filters of an aggregate scan (comparisons, ranges, `IN`, `NOT IN`, not null) on the encoded chunks of its groups, as `sift_col` does, with the same code (`agg/chunkfilter.c`): a filtered group-by over a million rows drops from 42-101 to 33-85 million instructions, and `sift_col` is unchanged.
- Changed: pg_sift: the aggregate scan of an inner join of three or more tables, or of two when the plan without it costs 1,000 or more, is priced with each table in turn as the scanned one and the others as its lookup tables, and the cheapest is planned, where the first table of the FROM list was the only one tried; a lookup table read whole is priced at the rows it loads (20 operators a row from the heap with the pages it reads, 8 from the leaves of a column index that holds its columns: `sift.agg_lookup_columns`), and a test between two columns of the scanned table is priced at the rows that survive the zones (it is evaluated once for each row). A join such as TPC-H Q3, Q10 or Q12 is answered by the scan of the fact table whichever way the FROM list is written, also cold, when the lookup tables are read from column indexes; with no covering index a lookup table that the heap makes dearer than the normal plan keeps the normal plan. No setting: `sift.enable_agg = off` turns the scan off.
- Changed: pg_sift: the small-set summaries of a unit (the key lists a scan prunes units with) are checked in one loop for each width of the list, where each key was a call, and a dictionary written uncompressed or in blocks is read into its own arrays, where it was read into a buffer and copied. On a fixture of one million rows (434 units, a text column of 100,000 values) a request over about 80 groups runs 0.5 ms warm where it ran 2.4 ms, a request without a text column 0.7 ms where it ran 2.5 ms, and the first statement of a new backend 2.9 ms where it ran 5.6 ms (17.2M to 11.7M instructions); the planning of a cold session is unchanged (38 ms and 6 ms). Test `col_set_summary`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a `sift_table` hands the byte order of a text dictionary (`sift.table_dict_lookup`) to the aggregate scan, as `sift_col` does: the top-N of a text column orders its units by exact codes instead of reading the strings.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a `sift_table` is read by the rewrites that only looked for a table with a `sift_col` index: the decorrelation of a `LATERAL` sub-select that aggregates and of `EXISTS` / `NOT EXISTS`, the inline plan of a shared CTE and the cost of a statement that reads one. Such a statement on a table of this access method ran as the normal plan with a sub-plan for each outer row (test `tbl_agg_rewrites`).
- Changed: pg_sift: `sift.enable_col_scan` is on by default: a `sift_col` index with the `sift.col_*_ops` classes is a planner path (bitmap scan, Index Scan) priced against the b-trees and the sequential scan, so a table whose b-trees were dropped reads its point, range and `IN` lookups from the index and not by a sequential scan (1,000,000 rows: `id = x` 29.5 to 0.21 ms, `id IN (100 ids)` 43.4 to 0.62 ms, a range of 100 rows 32.8 to 0.31 ms, a range of 1,000 rows 40.6 to 2.9 ms); with the b-trees present the plans are the b-tree's (24 of 26 shapes identical at 100,000 and 1,000,000 rows), and a few `count(*)` shapes that SiftAgg answered take the bitmap scan by cost, up to 0.16 ms slower. `SET sift.enable_col_scan = off` restores the earlier planner. Plan gate lanes unchanged.
- Added: pg_sift: the reloption `order_array_columns` of a `sift_col` index (a subset of `order_columns`: ordered runs, optionally with a global order array for any LIMIT) keeps, for each integer, date or timestamp column it names and each segment written afterwards, the sorted array of every row's key and heap address. An `ORDER BY column LIMIT n` over one such key, with no filter, join or aggregate, merges the arrays of the segments and of the write buffer and fetches `n` heap rows in the order of the key, whatever the number of units, where the scan opened every unit; the heap confirms each entry as it does a b-tree's (a dead row or an address another row took is skipped); EXPLAIN shows `Global Order`. A segment without the array keeps the scan of the units; a 3.3 library refuses a minor 21 segment (`ALTER INDEX .. RESET (order_array_columns)` and `REINDEX` first); `sift.col_verify` checks the arrays.
- Added: pg_sift: the reloption `order_array_columns` of a `sift_col` index (a subset of `order_columns`: ordered runs, optionally with a global order array for any LIMIT) keeps, for each integer, date or timestamp column it names and each segment written afterwards, the sorted array of every row's key and heap address. An `ORDER BY column LIMIT n` over one such key, with no join or aggregate and no filter but comparisons of the key with constants (a range, `ts >= x`: the scan searches each segment's array for the first entry of the range and ends at its other end), merges the arrays of the segments and of the write buffer and fetches `n` heap rows in the order of the key, whatever the number of units, where the scan opened every unit; the heap confirms each entry as it does a b-tree's (a dead row or an address another row took is skipped); EXPLAIN shows `Global Order`. A segment without the array keeps the scan of the units; a 3.3 library refuses a minor 21 segment (`ALTER INDEX .. RESET (order_array_columns)` and `REINDEX` first); `sift.col_verify` checks the arrays.
- Added: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY` of a key with a global order array (`order_array_columns`) and more integer, date or timestamp keys (`ORDER BY created_at, id LIMIT 50`), with or without the page after a key (`WHERE (created_at, id) > (x, y)`), reads the array from the key of the page: the scan takes the whole groups of rows that share a value of the first key, puts each in order by the heap row's other keys, and stops at the group that makes the rows wanted, where the units were read (1,000,000 and 3,000,000 rows, scattered timestamp, no b-tree: the page of 50 rows 17.1 and 17.3 ms for the units, 0.29 ms; descending 14.4 and 53.4 ms, 0.34 and 0.32 ms; a b-tree 0.13 to 0.21 ms); a first key that many rows share (more than 4,096 entries read, or 64 for each row wanted) reads the units. Test `col_topn_gorder`, fuzz family `gorder`.
- Added: pg_sift: the reloption `order_array_group` of a `sift_col` index (one integer, date or timestamp column) sorts the arrays of `order_array_columns` by that column and then the key, so `WHERE grp = x ORDER BY ts DESC LIMIT 10` (also with a range of the key or the page after a key) reads the entries of the group x in each segment and `n` heap rows, whatever the rows of the table, where the units read every unit of the group (1,024 groups, scattered timestamp, no b-tree, 100,000 / 1,000,000 / 3,000,000 rows: 0.25 / 0.32 / 0.22 ms against 0.46 / 1.6 / 3.7 ms for the units; a b-tree on (grp, ts) 0.08 / 0.10 / 0.05 ms); opt-in: about 6 bytes a row for each array column (+74% of a 4-column index with one), CREATE INDEX +65% over the runs; a query that does not pin the group reads the units; column format minor 21. Test `col_gorder_group`, fuzz family `gorder`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a parallel worker of an aggregate scan that starts a sub-plan or a child plan did not make the snapshot of the statement its active snapshot, so a build with assertions stopped the worker (`GetActiveSnapshot() == queryDesc->snapshot`) and the sub-plan ran with the restored copy of the leader's active snapshot instead of the one it was started with; the worker now pushes it for the life of the executor states. No plan changes.
- Changed: pg_sift: a column chunk of plain 3, 5, 6 or 7-byte values (a timestamp with microseconds spread over years) is decoded with one 8-byte load per value and not a library copy per value. No format change.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a `VACUUM` that vacuumed the other indexes of the table with parallel workers failed with "cannot assign transaction IDs during a parallel operation" when a merge of a `sift_col` index in its leader decided `key_columns = 'auto'` again (the write of the option); the merge leaves the decision to the next one outside parallel mode (`col_vacuum_parallel_keys`).
- Fixed: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY` a text column `LIMIT n` over a `sift_table` whose column was added with `ALTER TABLE .. ADD COLUMN` after its column groups were written ended the backend with a segmentation fault: the column of those groups, all NULL, had no dictionary (test `tbl_topn_absent`).
- Improved: pg_sift: a group by of one integer column with millions of groups takes fewer instructions a row and its time grows with the rows, not faster: the groups of one key stay in the arrays past a million (they were rehashed into the hash table at 1,048,576, which cost 2.4 times the instructions for twice the rows), a key that outgrows its arrays no longer renumbers the groups (the arrays are copied whole), and the slots of the ids and groups of the next 16 rows are fetched ahead. At `work_mem = 1GB` `count(*), sum(v)` of 3,000,000 rows takes 583 to 325 ms with 1,500,000 groups (the normal plan 1,151 ms); the case `group_many_groups`, with the default workers, takes 246 ms against 1,047, and of 4,000,000 rows 893 to 325 ms with 4,000,000 groups and 349 to 232 ms with 1,000,000 (10,000 groups: 23 to 22 ms, 100,000: 78 to 74 ms); with about as many groups as rows, 1,000,000, 2,000,000 and 4,000,000 rows take 128 to 93, 450 to 165 and 920 to 340 ms. On an arm64 core the same query takes 191 to 114 ms (1,500,000 groups), 155 to 126 ms (1,000,000) and 360 to 181 ms (4,000,000). The memory limit that caps the arrays is the same; two or more keys keep the limit of 1,048,576 slots.
- Changed: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT n` scan over an `int2`, `int4`, `int8`, date or timestamp key hands the rows of a unit to the heap through the range and min/max kernels, 256 rows at a time and the segments by the best value they hold, where it tested and pushed them one by one: a descending scan of a column that follows the table (the latest rows) no longer pushes every row of the unit (15.1M instructions at 1,000,000 rows, 8x the ascending scan, now 0.7M: 1.2 ms to 0.07 ms, flat to 3,000,000 rows) and a random key reads a unit with fewer instructions; no option, no format change; units under 512 rows and filters that leave under a sixteenth of a unit's rows keep the row loop; the kernels are the existing ones of x86-64 (AVX2, AVX-512) and arm64 (NEON).
- Improved: pg_sift: the slots of the (group, value) pairs of a joined `count(DISTINCT)` are fetched ahead of the adds, and so are those of the merge of the workers' sets: with a set past the caches (6,000,000 rows, 600,000 groups, two columns of a lookup table) the scan takes 661 to 428 ms (the normal plan 1,318 ms), the case `count_distinct_lookup_pairs`; a set that fits the caches does not change (1,000,000 rows, 190,000 groups: 60 and 71 ms, within noise).
- Improved: pg_sift: a short IN list of integers that has runs of consecutive members (`id IN (7, 8, 9)`) is one range test of the column engine, not one pass over the chunk a member, and the AVX2 range kernel compares in registers (`filter_few_rows`, 1,000,000 rows: 24.4 to 15.0 million instructions a query, 2.07 to 1.66 ms).
- Improved: pg_sift: a unit of a `sift_col` index with dead rows (a VACUUM cleared their liveness bits) is answered from its stored counts and integer and numeric sums minus its dead rows (`sift.agg_dead_delta`, default 15, 0 off) when every page it has rows on is all-visible and at most that percent of its rows are dead, where it was read by its rows: after 60 days of churn at 20% a month the grouped sum of 3,000,000 rows takes 84 to 721 of its 721 units from the pre-aggregates and runs 21.6 to 15.0 ms (336 to 160 million instructions), and of 10,000,000 rows 104 to 80 ms (1,214 to 821 million); no stored byte, no write, no format change; a minimum, a maximum, count(DISTINCT), a float sum and the write buffer still scan
- Improved: pg_sift: the overlays that churn leaves in a `sift_col` index are cut by TID range into units of `sift.col_unit_floor` rows (default 2,048) when a promotion reaches 16 superblocks or more with under half a unit's rows in each, and the tier merges of such overlays stay cut that way, so a table that has churned keeps hundreds of units where it had tens of thousands (3,000,000 rows, 20% of the rows changed a month: 10,552 to 721 units, a grouped sum 64.5 to 24.6 ms, a key lookup 35.0 to 7.5 ms; 10,000,000 rows: 35,107 to 2,415 units, 231 to 97 ms, 142 to 40 ms) with a smaller index while dirty, the same size after a merge and less WAL; the newest rows by `ORDER BY .. LIMIT` cost 0.1 to 0.4 ms more; a segment cut by TID range is column format minor 17 and `sift.col_unit_floor = 0` writes the superblock cut as before
- Added: pg_sift: `ORDER BY a, b LIMIT n` where only the first keys are columns of the `sift_col` index (an integer, date or timestamp column, then a column the index does not hold or an expression) is a top-N of the first keys under PostgreSQL's `Incremental Sort` of the whole `ORDER BY`: the scan gives the rows up to the last one that ties with the `n`-th on the first keys, starting again with a heap 16 times larger (twice, then every row) when the statistics did not know a large tie group, and the sort puts the ties in order (up to 8192 rows sharing a value by the statistics; 1,000,000-row table, `ORDER BY event_time, watch_id LIMIT 10`: 108 ms to 0.4 ms; 10,000,000 rows: 940 ms to 0.5 ms, the cost of the one-key top-N plus about 0.03 ms). No format change, no setting; `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` shows `Top-N Candidates` and `Top-N Restarts`. Test `col_topn_trail`, fuzz family `trail`, plan-gate shapes `b07` to `b09`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: an aggregate scan of a key-cut index (`key_cut`) vetted every row of a unit with dead rows through the heap even when every page of its block range was all-visible (a table VACUUMed after its updates): such a unit is now counted from its liveness alone and the scan fetches no heap page (a group-by over a table with 9% of its rows dead in the oldest units fetched all 29,628 pages of the heap).
- Changed: pg_sift: the automatic `key_cut` also cuts by a date or timestamp column when no key qualifies: a range holding the rows of one unit reads at least 8 times the units those rows fill and at least 64 units (a scattered `ts`: a day 9.05 to 0.23 ms and 30 days 11.1 to 0.38 ms at 3,000,000 rows, the rows of one key 12.6 to 28.4 ms), under the same guards and the 3% growth, and never advised (a time leaf is cut by or left alone); the decision ends at the first look of 8 superblocks when a leaf follows the heap order or a time cut would grow the index by more than 7%, and its sample is a quarter of the heap at most; the `NOTICE` and `sift.key_cut_decision` say "a range" for it (`col_key_cut_auto`).
- Improved: pg_sift: sums and averages of a decimal expression run on int64 vectors in blocks of 512 tuples when the data proves it safe (every multiplied operand below 2^31, no value near 2^62), with the vector kernels of each CPU (AVX2, NEON) and the int128 interpreter for the other blocks: `tpch_q1` (1,000,000 rows, scan forced, one worker) 44 to 27 ms and 609 to 347 instructions a row.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the build of a `key_cut` index on a table that was never analyzed aimed its units at 1024 rows, where a superblock held 7,000: 1,400,000 rows made 654 units a segment instead of 93, the pre-aggregates grew 1.1 to 6.9 MB and the index 25.1 to 33.0 MB (+32%, against +6% analyzed). A unit now aims at the rows a superblock holds in the run, and the automatic decision reads them from the middle superblock of the heap (`col_key_cut_auto`).
- Fixed: pg_sift: the size the automatic `key_cut` predicts counts the row directories as the index writes them, from the addresses of the sampled rows, not 0.65 bytes a row: a scattered key's addresses take 1.2 to 1.5, so an index of 10 columns on 700,000 rows that grew 3.3% was predicted at +0.2% and is now predicted at +3.9% (and advised), and 1,400,000 rows +3.1% measured against +0.7% before and +5.6% now (`col_key_cut_auto`).
- Changed: pg_sift: `CREATE INDEX` and `REINDEX` of a `sift_col` index cut its units by a key (`key_cut`, column format minor 17) by default when a sample of the heap shows that zones cannot skip the units of the key: the first key-shaped column whose equality reads at least 8 times the units its rows fill and at least 64 units, when no other leaf follows the heap order, the index has no `order_columns` and its predicted size grows by at most 3%; otherwise the `NOTICE` gives the units read, the predicted growth and the command; `sift.key_cut_decision(index)` says what a `REINDEX` would decide, `key_cut = 'off'` switches it, indexes built before keep their units (`col_key_cut_auto`). The decision first reads 8 superblocks and stops there for a table whose keys sit in few units (11 to 16 ms on a 353,029-row table, where the full sample took 225 ms of a 0.5 s build).
- Improved: sift_table: COPY, CREATE TABLE AS and REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW into a `sift_table` spend 12% fewer instructions (`bulk_copy_wal` 1,583 to 1,377 ms against 853 ms for a heap table, 1,000,000 rows): a numeric(p <= 18, s) value becomes its integer key from the digits of the numeric (70 instructions instead of 1,400 through the multiply, round and int8 conversions) and a row goes from the slot to the column batch without being formed and deformed as a tuple.
- Changed: pg_sift: the conditions of the outer aggregates of a two-level scan read the value of each cell once: the inner average, sum or count of a cell is asked by the first outer aggregate that needs it, not by every condition and every value (twenty times a cell on a score distribution over customer-and-month cells), and the comparison of a cell's float value with a constant is inline (NaN above every number, equal to itself, as `float8_cmp_internal`). On the statements with two-level cell conditions (eleven outer aggregates, five of them filtered on the cell's average) 5.0% to 7.2% fewer instructions (3.10G to 2.92G for the largest, 2.37G to 2.25G for a monthly one); over a set of 156 statements of a reporting workload -2.7% in all, none more than 2% above. Tests `agg_cell_conditions`.
- Improved: pg_sift: a grouped scan whose group table spills (`work_mem` below the groups) is 33% faster (`count_distinct_spill`, 193,000 groups at `work_mem` 1MB: 423 to 300 ms, the normal plan 306 ms): the groups it returns are no longer kept for a rescan the plan never asks for (and a plan that does ask appends them without reading each back, 170,000 write and 170,000 read system calls less), the first spill counts the groups its table holds, not the room it has, to choose the partitions (8 became 16 here: a partition that did not fit hash memory was spilled a second time), and the key tables of a table after a spill start with the room of the one written.
- Changed: pg_sift: planning a statement on a table with a column index no longer reads the index's statistics for every plan (they are kept by the backend while the index, its table and the catalog are unchanged) and no longer serializes the spec of a path that loses, which most are; plans are unchanged.
- Added: bench: the generic suites of the cases, generated locally and measured against PostgreSQL with the losses in the tables: TPC-H-like (`tpch_q1`, `tpch_q6`, `tpch_q3`, `tpch_q10`, `tpch_q12`: customer, orders and lineitem with the columns and key relations of TPC-H, not its distributions or its results), ClickBench-like (`hits_count`, `hits_filter_count`, `hits_sum_avg`, `hits_distinct_users`, `hits_distinct_phrases`, `hits_region_users`, `hits_top_phrases`, `hits_like_count`, `hits_like_group`, `hits_order_time`: a generated wide table, not ClickBench's data), time series (`ts_range_by_day`, `ts_hour_metric`, `ts_top_devices`, `ts_latest_device`) and the write overhead of a `sift_col` index (`oltp_insert_index`, `oltp_update_index`).
- Changed: pg_sift: the inline plan of a shared CTE no longer repeats an aggregating `IN` sub-select of the CTE body in every arm: it is a `MATERIALIZED` CTE of the same `WITH` that the arms read, so the ranking runs once and the plan is smaller to build; the plans of the load-replay statements are unchanged (numbers in docs/COL-PLAN.md).
- Improved: sift_table: UPDATEs of keys that are updated many times in one transaction are 3x faster (`tbl_update_keys` 3,076 to 950 ms against 705 ms for a heap table): the uniqueness check and the scan skip the old versions the transaction deleted without reading their rows, and the fetch scan keeps the TIDs of up to 32 superblocks (8 MB per table at most) instead of 4.
- Changed: pg_sift: a lookup table that a later inner or semi join keys by one of its columns alone (the ids of a request, a CTE or sub-select of them, joined to the table of their rows), or that a CTE of ids joins on the same leaf of the scan, is loaded by a btree probe for each of those keys while the descents and the pages of the rows they find cost less than the pages of the table at the planner's page cost, where the whole table was read for every request (`Lookup Index Scans`, `Lookup Rows Read`); the plan price follows the same rule, and `sift.agg_lookup_pushdown = off` reads the table whole (`agg_lookup_keyed`).
- Changed: pg_sift: a range on a leaf with a sorted run (`order_columns`), or an equality on it, in a unit of 1,024 rows or more is answered from the run by binary search when it holds at most an eighth of the unit, and the chunk is decoded only at those rows, where every row was tested; an aggregate scan tests such a range before its other filters and EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows `Units Filtered By Sorted Run`. On a timestamp with microseconds the time falls 1.1x to 1.5x (1,000,000 to 10,000,000 rows), on whole-second keys only the instructions of the range test fall and the time changes by -12% to +13%. No format change and no option; an index without `order_columns`, a float or text leaf, a chunk with overflowed rows and a range with one bound are tested as before.
- Added: pg_sift: every option, setting and automatic behavior of the options record (`auto(name)` rows for the heuristics that have no setting) states what it does, its measured gain, its impact (index size, build, merge and write cost), when it applies, why its default is what it is and how to turn it off; `docs/OPTIONS.md` lists the NOTICE texts the library raises.
- Changed: pg_sift: a scan whose keys or conditions hold a hashed sub-plan (`x IN (SELECT ..)`) can plan workers: each worker starts the sub-plan in an executor state of the statement and hashes its table once. A sub-plan that holds a function unsafe in a worker and the output of an ARRAY sub-select keep one process.
- Changed: pg_sift: a scan with a spine (the rows of a plan the leader runs, joined to the cells of the scan) and a scan whose join is a correlated sub-select (run for each distinct combination of the leaves it reads) can plan workers: each worker reads the spine from the shared memory and starts the sub-select's plan itself, and the rows the sub-select returned for a set of parameter values are kept in the shared memory, so that the processes of the scan run it once for each set between them, not once each.
- Changed: pg_sift: a grouped scan under an `ORDER BY` or a window on its group keys (also over a sub-select or an inlined CTE that the window reads, whose columns may be constants or `COALESCE(key, constant)`) emits its groups in that order, and no Sort is put above it (EXPLAIN shows `Group Order Keys`): the distinct values of each key are sorted once with the comparator of the pathkey and the groups by their ranks. The rows and their order are those of the normal plan; a grouping set, `DISTINCT ON`, a scan that spills and an `ORDER BY` an aggregate keep the Sort. No setting, no format change. Replay statement with a window over 40,586 groups at 1,000,000 rows: 514 ms against 645 ms with 4 workers and 705 against 800 ms in one process (the Sort above the scan was 117 ms of it).
- Changed: pg_sift: a parallel scan with float sums keeps the sums of each lane a process scanned apart in the table it scans into and sends one table for all its lanes, where it started a table, a blob and a merge for each lane; the leader adds the lanes in order. The float sums of every scan that could have workers before are the same bits as before, with or without workers. The scans that gained workers (a spine, a correlated sub-select) add in the order of the rows when their plan has no workers, as they did, and in lane order when it has, so with workers their last digits can differ from the earlier release and from the plan without (at most 2 (n - 1) 2^-53 of the sum of the absolute values, docs/COL-PLAN.md; 4e-13 relative to the result on the replay statements at 1,000,000 rows); integers, numeric, counts, min, max and distinct are exact in any plan. `max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 0` or the table's `parallel_workers = 0` keeps their earlier sums.
- Changed: pg_sift: the ids of a dictionary's entries (the key of a group by a text column, the values of `count(DISTINCT text)`) are made when a tuple first reads the entry, where the statement made them for every entry of the dictionary at its start (the hash of each string): a request over 82 rows with a 100,000-entry category dictionary ran 9.7 ms warm, against 1.8 ms with the entries made on demand (the control without a text leaf 1.8 ms); the groups of a statement are numbered in the order the scan meets them. Tests `agg_dict_lazy_map`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a parallel scan with lanes (a float sum or average, added in the order of the scan) that reads a column of a lookup in an expression (`avg(x) FILTER (WHERE d.tag IS NOT NULL)` after a `LEFT JOIN`) could crash the backend with a segmentation fault from the second lane on: the lookup's output rows start again with each lane, and the ids that an expression combo had given to the values of those rows did not, so a row that matched nothing was read as one of the earlier lane's rows. Test `agg_lookup_lanes`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a `COPY` into a `sift_table` next to other writers that keep its directory at the limit (`sift.table_max_segments`) settles the directory again, up to 8 times, before it fails with `has N segments`: the room one settle made was taken by the writers waiting for it before the load held the lock. It still fails when the directory stays full (rows of running transactions in the way). 3 of 30 runs of 8 writers and 2 `COPY`s of 30,000 rows of 1 kB at a limit of 16 failed; 0 of 60 do.
- Changed: pg_sift: a scan whose lookup joins read a CTE, a sub-select or a function (rows the leader loads and shares with the workers) can plan workers when the functions of the expressions the scan evaluates are parallel safe; the relations of the joins no longer have to be, as PostgreSQL asks of a parallel scan, so a query with a CTE scan in it no longer keeps one process. The workers are priced as for any other scan (`parallel_setup_cost` and the rows of the lookup tables shared), so a small scan stays in one process. A request that reads every row of a 1,000,000-row index through a CTE of 12,000 keys takes 42 ms in the aggregate node with 4 workers where it took 80 ms with one process (best of 3).
- Fixed: pg_sift: a parallel scan with float sums (merged in lane order) and a lookup keyed by a column of an earlier lookup (a second join on a column that the first one outputs) kept, for the second lane that a worker scanned, the ids that the lookup had given to the values of the lane before: the tuples of the lane were counted in the groups of another lane's rows (totals kept, the split between groups wrong). The ids start again with each lane.
- Changed: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT n` scan whose output columns are all integer, date or timestamp columns of the index takes the rows of the result from the units that gave them, where it fetched each from the heap (`Heap Fetches: 0` in EXPLAIN): a sort key is taken from the entry, the other columns are decoded from the unit when the first key follows the heap order (`pg_stats` correlation of 0.9 or more) and the unit gives at least half of the rows the scan keeps. Rows from the write buffer and from units that give fewer are still fetched. No format change; a column outside the index, a text column or a numeric one keeps the heap fetch.
- Changed: pg_sift: an aggregate scan reads the point directory of a leaf (`point_columns`, `point_tid_columns`) for an `IN` list or an equality only while the keys are expected to have fewer rows than 4,096 or a 64th of the segment's rows (the keys of the leaf have the segment's rows divided by its distinct keys, each): a list of keys with many rows each lists a row in most units and costs more to read than the units cost to scan, so the zones and summaries answer it; the plan price follows the same rule. A scan that hands addresses to the heap is as before (`col_point_dense`).
- Changed: pg_sift: `count(DISTINCT x)` of a `bigint` column is kept as the distinct values of a decimal expression (the path `count(DISTINCT x + 1)` already took), not as the combinations of a generic aggregate: the number is the same, the set is exact, and the aggregate runs in the workers of a parallel scan (the leader merges their sets) where it ran in the leader alone.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the scan of a grouped `count(DISTINCT x)` of an integer column is no longer priced for bitmap chunks its sets do not allocate: a group of few rows (under 16 chunks of the range of the column, and the directory) starts as a hash set, so 100,000 groups of 10 rows over 1,000,000 rows stay on the scan (69 ms; the normal plan picked at a 4GB `work_mem` took 493 ms) as the 30,000 groups before them did (bench case `count_distinct_highcard`: a hundred thousand groups of a few rows).
- Fixed: pg_sift: the heap check of the aggregate scan (the pages of the kept units that are not all-visible) is never priced above the random fetch of the same pages: with `seq_page_cost` above `random_page_cost` (100 and 4, the tuned profile of `scripts/plangate.sh --lane tuned`) the check cost 100 a page, which was most of the price of a scan of a table of 15 million pages, and the scan lost to a normal plan that runs many times slower for 105 of the 237 statements of the corpus planned at that size. Configurations with `seq_page_cost` at most `random_page_cost` are priced as before.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the load of a lookup table that the scan reads whole (no index usable, no plan of its own) is priced with the pages PostgreSQL's own sequential scan of it reads, besides its rows: a join to a table of 1.5 million rows cost the scan about half of what the normal plan pays to read the same table, and the scan was taken for outer sides of up to 200,000 rows where the normal plan ran faster.
- Changed: pg_sift: `count(DISTINCT x)` of a column of a joined table (a lookup join, the nullable side of a join from the left) runs in parallel: a worker sends the pairs (group, value) of its set with the values, the leader interns them and a value two workers saw counts once, `count(x)` of such a column adds up. The start of workers is priced at 3 ms and 0.35 ms each in estimates of 500 units a millisecond (it was 2,750, which charged the median scan of a replay of 156 statements 215 ms and kept the workers off every one of them), a set of a joined column is charged its pairs (at most the distinct values of the argument for each group), so a scan of 20-valued names takes workers from about 800,000 rows (28.6 to 16.7 ms; 94.5 to 38.3 ms at 3,200,000) and one of almost one value a row stays serial at 800,000 rows. Of the replayed statements 60 scans that kept one process for this reason are allowed workers, 4 plan them (the sum of the 156 at two workers 17.20 s against 17.16 s serial), and the rows are the same at 0 and 4 workers.
- Changed: pg_sift: a scan whose query joins a table from the left to what it scans (a calendar `LEFT JOIN` the facts) runs in parallel: the preserved side is shared read-only, each participant marks the rows its tuples matched, a worker sends the marks with its tables and the leader adds the rows nobody matched once every worker has told, and the counts and float sums of the nullable side add up in lane order. Aggregates that still keep the scan in one process: `min`, `max` and numeric sums of the nullable side, arms and a spine.
- Added: pg_sift: `docs/OPTIONS.md` has a table "Upgrading from 3.3": the options that are new, have another default or write a newer column format minor than 3.3 reads, and those whose behavior this release changes, with what a 3.3 user gets after `ALTER EXTENSION pg_sift UPDATE` and whether it needs a `REINDEX`. No default changed since 3.3.
- `col_index_advice`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a lookup join on one column of the scanned table (a text, integer, date, int8 or float8 column) resolves its rows in a loop of the column's kind with the cache of matches probed inline, and reads a row's value once for all of its elements; with the key ids shared by grouping sets, reports with grouping sets over such joins run in 9.5% fewer cycles (10.3% fewer instructions; x0.1 replay statements, one backend).
- Changed: pg_sift: under `GROUPING SETS` whose groups are kept in a hash table (keys of many values), the aggregate scan prepares the key ids of a chunk once for all the sets that read its rows, where it prepared every key again for each set.
- Changed: pg_sift: the plan-choice gate keeps the expected plan and cost band of each shape in the `--lane tuned` and `--table` lanes (`test/plan/bands.tsv`; `scripts/plangate.sh --update` in a lane rewrites its rows), as the planner costs of those lanes differ from the stock lane's by a regime (the tuned profile prices a sequential page at 100) and not by a drift: a plan or ratio that leaves the lane's own record now fails on PostgreSQL 18 (a warning on another major), and the CI tiers run the table lane with the tuned profile too (`plan-gate-table-tuned`).
- Added: pg_sift: `scripts/plangate.sh --lane tuned` runs the plan-choice gate with the planner settings of a tuned profile (`seq_page_cost` 100, `random_page_cost` 4, 8 workers per gather, `work_mem` 4GB): the results must agree and a lose fails, the expected plans and cost bands stay those of the stock lane (a different plan is printed, a band drift is a warning); the CI tiers run it as `plan-gate-tuned`.
- Added: pg_sift: `scripts/plangate.sh --table` runs the 96 plan-choice shapes on the tables as `sift_table` (`test/plan/table.sql`): the results agree with the normal plan on every shape; the plan and cost band are those of the heap with a `sift_col` index (a different plan is printed, a drift is a warning); the CI tiers run it as `plan-gate-table`.
- Changed: pg_sift: an aggregate's argument or `FILTER` over a column of a query joined from the left (a spine `LEFT JOIN` the rows of a table with a `sift_col` index, under grouping sets: PostgreSQL hands the column as a placeholder) is read as the expression it stands for when that is a strict expression of the scanned table's columns, which is NULL where the join finds no row: the cast of a map value is the float leaf, so `avg((value #>> '{}')::float8)` is the leaf aggregate and `FILTER (WHERE value >= 8)` compares the decoded values, where both went through the lookup cache once per element and per aggregate. A constant, a non-strict expression (`coalesce`, `CASE`) or a volatile one keeps the placeholder. Reports of that shape run in 1.5-2.1x fewer cycles (2.4x fewer instructions in the scan, x0.1 replay statements, one backend).
- Changed: pg_sift: a merge of every segment of an index whose `key_columns` is `auto` decides the key lists again from the segments it rewrites: after churn, a key-shaped column that the cube holds in under 40% of the units gets its key list at the merge, as a `REINDEX` would have chosen (the option reads `auto: set_id`; the report after the merge drops what does not fit or what the cube holds in half of the units, so the choice does not alternate); an explicit `key_columns`, a partial merge and `sift.col_format_minor` under 15 are unchanged, and a merge that cannot take the table's maintenance lock at once (a VACUUM holds it) leaves the choice to the next merge instead of waiting for it with the index metapage held, which deadlocked with the VACUUM (`col_auto_merge_concurrent`).
- Added: pg_sift: `CREATE INDEX` on a `sift_col` index names, in one NOTICE, the covered columns that look rewritten often (a name ending in `updated_at`, `modified_at`, `_count`, `_version`, or `likes`, `views`, `version`): an `UPDATE` of them is not a HOT update (0% HOT updates against 28% on the 13-column index of the web API, measured). The column stays covered.
- The figures for `compression`, `table_compression`, `key_columns` and `table_dict_lookup` that had no source are replaced by measured ones, and the GPU section of `docs/GPU.md` reads the stored table (the device ties the CPU).
- Changed: pg_sift: the refusal `lateral_limit` of a `LATERAL (.. LIMIT 1)` child names the columns of its key and ORDER BY that no unique index holds and says what makes the key unique: end the ORDER BY with the primary key, or create a unique index on them (a partial one, `WHERE col IS NOT NULL`, for a sub-select with that condition; an index on an expression does not count), and that `CLUSTER` on it and `VACUUM` keep the lookups on adjacent pages.
- Changed: pg_sift: a lookup of a key or an IN list of the point directory of heap addresses (`point_tid_columns`) on a query the index covers is planned on the addresses and the heap, as it is on a query it does not cover, instead of through the index-only scan that reads every kept unit; and a key with a range on another column hands the heap only the rows of the range when a unit lists many of the key's addresses (the unit is read and filtered), where it handed all of them to be rechecked away.
- Added: pg_sift: `CREATE INDEX` on a `sift_col` index says in a NOTICE that the primary key is not a column of the index when a foreign key of another table references it, since a join of two sift tables on a key that is not a column of both indexes keeps the normal plan (`join_condition`). The key is not added by default: it grows an index of the web API by 19.6% to 131% (about 3 bytes a row for ids with gaps; nothing for consecutive ids), more than the 3% a default may cost.
- Changed: pg_sift: `EXISTS (SELECT .. FROM f WHERE f.k = x.k AND ..)` and `NOT EXISTS` where PostgreSQL leaves a sub-plan for each outer row (the select list, under an `OR`, in a `CASE`) are `f` grouped by `k` left joined to `x`, EXISTS being a group that is there: kept when the planner costs it below the lookups of the plain plan (`found IS NOT NULL`: never NULL, a NULL key has no group, an empty table none, a repeated key answers each time). An EXISTS the WHERE ANDs stays the semi join (a NOT EXISTS the anti join) PostgreSQL makes of it; `IN`, `NOT IN` (their NULLs differ), a condition other than equalities of the table's column and an outer one, a `LIMIT`, a join, a sub-select inside and an outer query that groups leave it as written.
- Changed: pg_sift: `sift.index_advice` names `preagg = 'auto'` when more dimensions are asked for than the cube's 16, and `rollup_dims = 'auto'` with the advice of a pair (`col_index_advice`).
- Changed: pg_sift: the page mask and the run head of a unit that a top-N scan reads for the head bypass are kept by the backend for the next query (in the cache of `dict_cache_kb`), so the superblocks of an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT` read them from memory.
- Added: pg_sift: `docs/OPTIONS.md` records the case of every option and setting: its class (default, opt-in, diagnostic, safety, deprecated), for an opt-in the limit of the default rule it failed, the measured gain, index growth and write cost with their conditions, the pull request, the benchmark case and the advice line. README.md and the tables of `docs/USAGE.md` gain a class column.
- Changed: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT` over a `sift_col` index probes the zones of six superblocks from the segment headers before the walk, and no longer reads a unit of each.
- Changed: pg_sift: the largest value of a float key of each row averaged by group is one two-level scan: `max` and `min` of a `float8` column or of a float8 key of a jsonb document are inner aggregates of the arms, so `SELECT .., avg(x) FROM (SELECT id, .., max((doc ->> 'k')::float8) AS x .. GROUP BY id, ..) GROUP BY ..` (the per-row scores of a table, averaged by group) no longer returns a row per inner group to a PostgreSQL aggregate. A cell with no value gives NULL, which the outer avg and sum skip; a NaN is the largest value. The answers are those of the normal plan (differential regress `agg_two_level_inner_minmax`: NULL cells, a NaN and infinities, a WHERE and a HAVING on the inner aggregate, two arms). No format change.
- Changed: pg_sift: the `sum` and `avg` of a decimal expression of up to 38 digits (a price times one minus a discount times one plus a tax, `tpch_q1`) are computed by the scan: the sum is kept in 128 bits and the number of times it wrapped, so it is exact for any number of rows, where an argument of more than 26 digits was left to PostgreSQL's own numeric arithmetic row by row (the pre-aggregates still take only the 26-digit programs); the one aggregate was 92% of the time of the query, which now takes 19 ms where it took 200 ms at 400,000 rows.
- Changed: pg_sift: a `numeric` column without a precision (or past 38 digits) is read through a dictionary of the unit's values whose entries are built from the digits of the stored image, not from its text through `numeric_in` (the numerics are the same bit for bit), and a `sum` or `avg` of such a column adds the entries as an integer and a scale decoded once for each entry of a dictionary, where the combo made a datum of every entry and `numeric_add` added it: 1,000,000 rows of 100,000 distinct values in 6 groups take 68 ms where PostgreSQL's plan takes 155 ms (the scan took 530 ms before, a 3x loss that the planner kept out of). The sum stays on one process, so the planner keeps a parallel plan of PostgreSQL where that is cheaper (a table of 1,000,000 rows read by 2 workers: 61 ms against 93). The sum of a `numeric(30,16)` column of 100,000 rows takes 7.3 ms against 16.8 ms. Test `agg_wide_sum_dict`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the groups that cannot be among the first n are dropped before they become tuples: an `ORDER BY <aggregate> [DESC] LIMIT n [OFFSET m]` over a plain `GROUP BY` (the first sort key a `count`, `sum` of an integer, decimal or float8, or `avg` of a float8 column; no HAVING, window, DISTINCT or grouping sets) ranks the groups by the raw value of the aggregate and emits only those that tie with or beat the (n + m)-th, where PostgreSQL sorted every group; the sort and the limit above stay in the plan and rank the ties by the other keys (EXPLAIN shows `Groups Dropped By Top-N`). `GROUP BY id ORDER BY sum(amount * quantity) DESC LIMIT 10` over 400,000 groups takes 14.6 ms against 96.6 ms (over 1,000,000 orders 61 ms against 174 ms, normal plan 504 ms), the top 10 customers by `sum` 15.1 ms against 32.2 ms at 77,000 groups, by `count` 14.4 ms against 20.0 ms; at 100,000 groups 4.4 ms against 23.2 ms. The rows are those of the normal plan, ties at the n-th aggregate value included (differential regress `agg_group_topn`: ASC and DESC, NULLS FIRST and LAST, an OFFSET, NaN and infinities, NULL sums, a second sort key, ties, parallel workers). No format change.
- Changed: pg_sift: the groups that cannot be among the first n are dropped before they become tuples: an `ORDER BY <aggregate> [DESC] LIMIT n [OFFSET m]` over a plain `GROUP BY` (the first sort key a `count`, `sum` of an integer, decimal or float8, or `avg` of a float8 column; no HAVING, window, DISTINCT or grouping sets) ranks the groups by the raw value of the aggregate and emits only those that tie with or beat the (n + m)-th, where PostgreSQL sorted every group; the sort and the limit above stay in the plan and rank the ties by the other keys (EXPLAIN shows `Groups Dropped By Top-N`). `GROUP BY id ORDER BY sum(amount * quantity) DESC LIMIT 10` over 400,000 groups takes 14.6 ms against 96.6 ms (over 1,000,000 orders 61 ms against 174 ms, normal plan 504 ms), the top 10 customers by `sum` 15.1 ms against 32.2 ms at 77,000 groups, by `count` 14.4 ms against 20.0 ms; at 100,000 groups 4.4 ms against 23.2 ms; when no group ranks after the n-th (400,000 groups of 2 or 3 rows ordered by `count`) the scan finds that in the first pass and keeps all: 47.3 ms against 46.9 ms with no drop at all (48.5 ms when a second pass keeps them). The rows are those of the normal plan, ties at the n-th aggregate value included (differential regress `agg_group_topn`: ASC and DESC, NULLS FIRST and LAST, an OFFSET, NaN and infinities, NULL sums, a second sort key, ties, parallel workers). No format change.
- Changed: pg_sift: the aggregate scan finds `mode() WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY x)` on the histogram of the values of a group, as it does `percentile_disc` (a float, integer, date, timestamp or decimal of up to 18 digits): the value of most rows, the first in the order of the sort on a tie (the smallest, the largest for DESC), where it went through the generic aggregate. Over 400,000 rows in 40 groups the mode of a decimal takes 26 ms against 175 ms and that of a smallint 7 ms against 117 ms, and both together over 1,000,000 rows 84 ms against 468 ms (the normal plan: 199 and 170 ms); the answers are the final function's (differential regress `agg_ordered_set`: ties, NULLs, NaN, DESC, a histogram that spills, parallel workers, shared with percentiles of the same argument). The mode of a text column keeps the generic path. No format change.
- Changed: pg_sift: the aggregate scan of `percentile_disc` finds the percentile_disc of a decimal, timestamp or bigint on the 64-bit values of its histogram: a `numeric` of up to 18 digits (a column or an expression of columns), a `timestamp`, a `timestamptz` or a `bigint` column keeps its values as integers (a double holds 53 bits), picks the rank by the same quickselect and returns the value in its own type (a decimal with its scale, a NaN last), where it went through the generic aggregate. Over 400,000 rows in 40 groups the median of a decimal takes 70 ms against 168 ms and that of a timestamp 66 ms against 126 ms (the normal plan: 332 and 273 ms); at 1,000,000 rows, a decimal and a timestamp median together take 205 ms against 610 ms. The answers are the final functions' (differential regress `agg_ordered_set`: NULLs, `infinity`, the extremes of `bigint`, NaN, DESC, several fractions on one argument, a histogram that spills, parallel workers). No format change.
- Changed: pg_sift: the aggregate scan of `percentile_cont` and `percentile_disc` (an `ORDER BY` of float8, float4, int2, int4 or date values) finds the percentiles of a group by a quickselect over its histogram: the positions they pick are found together, where the histogram was sorted whole (aggregates on one argument share the passes). With 1M values in 40 groups the median takes 76 ms against 193 ms and two percentiles 88 ms against 194 ms (the statement runs 0.3 G instructions against 1.0 G; the normal plan takes 592 ms). The answers are the final functions' (differential regress `agg_ordered_set`, 28 random fractions on 1M rows); a histogram that spills still goes through the tuplesort. No format change.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the rows of the scan of a CTE are lifted toward the rows its body reads from a table with a column index only through nodes that keep their rows: a body that aggregates, groups, limits or combines sets (`SELECT kind_id, count(*) .. GROUP BY kind_id`, 20 groups) was lifted toward the rows of the table below it (20 groups to 94 rows with 20,000 read), and the plans above it were priced for them. Its rows are now its own estimate. The CTEs of the 156 and 237 statements of the replay corpora are lifted as before (no line of the lift changes).
- Changed: pg_sift: `x JOIN LATERAL (SELECT g, sum(f.a) FROM f WHERE f.k = x.k GROUP BY g [HAVING ..]) ON true` (also `LEFT JOIN`, and the comma form) is decorrelated as well: `f` grouped by `k` and `g`, joined to `x` by `k`, kept when the planner costs it below the lookups of the plain plan. A row for each group stays what it was: an inner join gives none for a `k` without rows, a left join a row of NULLs (a count included), and the columns of the select list are evaluated in the grouped query, not above the join. A `HAVING` without `GROUP BY`, grouping sets, an `ORDER BY`, a `LIMIT` and an outer column in a group or the `HAVING` leave the sub-select as written.
- Changed: pg_sift: `sift.suggest_paths` (and the `paths` that `CREATE INDEX` takes from it) takes a wildcard for a map of more than 4 keys of one type (it was 32; a map of ten keys read with `jsonb_each` was not served by ten named keys), leaves out a text key whose values average over 64 bytes, reads a document that is an array as a group of the column's own path and leaves out arrays inside the elements of a group (the index of a group with a list of its own was refused): the `jsonb` columns of the web API, 8,830,976 bytes by hand, 12,058,624 with the named keys and 28 SiftAgg nodes of 237, now 9,314,304 (+5.5%) and 138 nodes; the 40 statements that read a key no document holds stay on the heap.
- Changed: pg_sift: `FROM x, LATERAL (SELECT sum(f.a) FROM f WHERE f.k = x.k)` (the comma form: an inner join of the sub-select to the item of the list that holds the columns it reads) is decorrelated like `x JOIN LATERAL (..) ON true`: the table grouped by `k` and joined to `x`, kept when the planner costs it below the lookups of the plain plan. A sub-select that reads the columns of another lateral sub-select is left as written (the rewrite of one replaces the columns the other reads).
- Changed: pg_sift: a table with several `sift_col` indexes is planned through the cheapest index that gives a plan, not the newest one: a lookup of a key reads the index cut by that key (`key_cut`) and a range of dates the index cut by block range, whatever the creation order; the cost of a scan that answers from the pre-aggregates follows the units the zones keep, as the cost of the other scans does.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.approx_count_distinct(x)`, an aggregate that returns a HyperLogLog estimate of the distinct values of `x` (16,384 registers, relative standard error 0.81%; `NULL`s are not counted, no row gives 0). It is the same number in every plan, with workers and by partition, and nothing turns `count(DISTINCT)` into it: that stays exact whatever the settings and options say. The `sift_col` reloption `hll_columns` (default none; `int2`, `int4`, `int8`, `date`, `timestamp`, `timestamptz`, `text`, `varchar` and `uuid` columns) stores the sketch of each unit written afterwards, and the aggregate scan merges the sketches of the units it reads whole and hashes the rows of the others; an index without the option writes the bytes it wrote before. The sketches of two columns over 1,000,000 orders answer in 6.4 ms where the normal plan takes 109 ms. `EXPLAIN` shows `Approximate Distinct` and `Units Without The Sketch`, `sift.explain_why` names `approx_no_sketch`, `sift.upgrade_commands()` and `sift.upgrade_check()` know the option, and `scripts/check-approx.sh` is the lint that keeps the estimate opt-in. Usage in [docs/USAGE.md](docs/USAGE.md) "Approximate distinct counts".
- Changed: pg_sift: before a page of a secondary index splits after `UPDATE`s that keep the index keys, the b-tree asks the table which of the page's versions are dead (bottom-up deletion; a simple deletion asks about the entries marked dead), and a `sift_table` answered by reading every row of the page (several hundred, in key order, each from another superblock of the column groups, which the fetch scan reloaded for nearly every one). It now answers from its tombstone log alone (a TID is never reused, so the log of its key is the row's whole history), reads a row only to learn that a simple deletion's row is gone, in TID order, and stops a bottom-up pass once the entries found free the space the index asked for: 40,000 updates by key of 2,000 rows of a 600,000-row table with 100,000 rows in the write buffer went from 9.0 s to 3.1 s (a heap: 0.7 s), 2,000 updates with 22,000 buffered rows from 3.5 s to 0.24 s, and the cost of an update no longer grows with the rows the write buffer holds (flat from 1,000 to 100,000).
- Changed: pg_sift: the extension SQL is version 3.4 (`pg_sift--3.4.sql`, `pg_sift--3.3--3.4.sql`, `default_version = '3.4'`): run `ALTER EXTENSION pg_sift UPDATE` in every database. The scripts of 3.3 are the released ones again (an install at 3.3.0 never ran the change that an edit of them made); `sift.upgrade_commands()` of 3.4 also lists an index whose `key_cut` came after its units.
- Changed: pg_sift: a column segment whose units are cut by a declared key (`key_cut`) carries a flag in its directory entry, and the readers and the merge tell it by the flag, not by the column format minor being 17: a later minor for another record no longer makes a segment keyed or not. Segments already written as minor 17 are read as keyed.
- Changed: pg_sift: a unit of a `sift_col` index in which one integer leaf is NULL in at least 99% of the rows (a soft-delete stamp) also keeps the pre-aggregates of the rows where it is not NULL (column format minor 19, no option; a merge or `REINDEX` writes them, +1.4% of the index on a table with 0.2% stamped rows), and `WHERE stamp IS NULL` is answered from the pre-aggregates as the unit's aggregate minus those rows (counts, integer, numeric and standard-deviation measures exactly, a float sum guarded against cancellation, `min` and `max` scanned): a count and an average by a key over 353,029 rows 6.1 to 2.5 ms, 171 of 171 units from the pre-aggregates instead of 5 (`Units Net Of Exceptions` in `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`; docs/COL-PREAGG.md section 11).
- Changed: pg_sift: the sorted runs of an index with `order_columns` also keep a head (column format minor 18): the first and last `n / 128` keys, at most 32, of a leaf with no NULL, each with the heap address of its row, and an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT n` of at most that many rows with one sort key, no condition and no join reads no unit that is clean and all-visible: the heap gets the addresses straight from the head (`Units Answered By Run Head` in EXPLAIN). `ORDER BY rnd LIMIT 10` over 800,000 rows takes 0.85 million instructions a statement where it took 1.20 (the scan of the units 0.25 million where it was 0.65), the index grows 0.60% (three order columns) or 2.4% (a narrow index of 24 bytes a row) and a build, a promotion and a merge cost the same. Segments of minor 13 and 18 live in one index; a key-cut segment has no head. A 3.3 library cannot read minor 18: `sift.col_format_minor` 14 or less writes the runs without heads.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.cluster_advice()` names `key_cut` (and `key_columns` for a key of over 256 values) when the key a workload looks rows up by reads many units and no other leaf is ordered by the table: the key is the first leaf of `key =>` or the leaf of `point_columns`, `point_tid_columns` or `key_columns` (not unique) that would save the most units, and with no such leaf the reason says how to choose one; a table whose id or date follows the arrival order still answers `competing leaves`, an ordered one `already ordered`. `CREATE INDEX` and `REINDEX` print the same advice as a `NOTICE` (silenced by `sift.cluster_advice = off` and the index option `cluster_advice`). No format change; the test is `col_key_cut_advice`.
- Changed: pg_sift: planning an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT` over a table with a `sift_col` index no longer scans the operator class catalog for each sort key: the type cache gives the default btree family, 58,000 fewer instructions of 406,000 per statement (a table of 200,000 rows, callgrind, one sort key), and the same plan.
- Changed: pg_sift: the aggregate scan of `Custom Scan (SiftAgg)` asks the point directory (`point_columns`, `point_tid_columns`) for the rows of a short `IN` list, an equality, a short range, and the keys of an inner or semi lookup join, and reads only the units that hold one of them, each as the rows the directory lists (`Units From Point Directory` and `Rows From Point Directory` of `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`); a 51-key request over 16,384 units read every unit (23.7 ms, 28.6 ms with the join) and now reads 51 (3.6 ms, 2.7 ms; log-log slope 0.80 and 0.74 before, 0.20 and 0.08 after); the same request on a million rows with no b-tree on the key takes 2.1 ms against 68.7 ms for the normal plan. The planner prices the units the directory leaves; the directory is unchanged, so no format change and no index growth or write cost.
- Changed: pg_sift: the access-method scan of a `sift_col` index with `point_tid_columns` merges the address lists of its segments by runs (they are ascending) and sorts nothing when the segments do not interleave, does not sort a list that one key gave, and finds the unit of an address once per superblock: `SELECT * WHERE grp = 7 LIMIT 10` over 800,000 rows (8,092 addresses of the key, four segments) takes 7.29 million instructions instead of 2.70 million, the forced index scan 0.53-0.63 ms instead of 0.14-0.25 ms (the planner still prefers the heap scan of a LIMIT over a key that is spread evenly, as the b-tree row of the same query costs 1.0 million).
- Changed: pg_sift: a `LIKE` or `^@` with a literal prefix on a column that has a point directory (`point_columns`, `point_tid_columns`) is priced by the planner as the lookups of the keys the dictionary names, and the scan turns the dictionary's table of codes into those keys by words: `count(*) WHERE ref LIKE '<4 characters>%'` over 1,000,000 rows of a 64-character key is the access method's lookup (measured in `bench/cases.tsv`, point_prefix_count) where an aggregate scan read every unit the zones kept.
- Changed: pg_sift: the access-method scan of a `sift_col` index tests the zones and summaries of the other predicates before it asks the point directory (`point_tid_columns`), so a key that is in every unit (a group, a tenant) with a range on another column (a date) hands the heap the rows of the range, not every row of the key: `grp = k AND ts BETWEEN ..` over 800,000 rows reads 806 heap pages instead of 7,456 and takes 0.45 ms instead of 3.1 ms (the b-tree on `(grp, ts)`: 0.43 ms).
- Fixed: pg_sift: an index with both `key_cut` and `point_tid_columns` wrote, for its key-cut segments, point directories of heap addresses, which name no unit of such a segment: an index-only scan of a key equality or `IN` list returned no rows, a lookup of a row in a late unit failed with "segment is corrupted", and `sift.col_verify` reported the segment. A key-cut segment now lists the rows of the segment (a lookup reads the unit of each row, as with `point_columns`), `CREATE INDEX` says so, and a key-cut segment with a table of addresses is refused with the `REINDEX` hint.
- Changed: pg_sift: `count(DISTINCT x)` of the primary key over the elements of a list under a grouping set and a spine joined from the left (`periods LEFT JOIN parsed ON ... GROUP BY GROUPING SETS (...)`, the shape of a report whose buckets are filtered distinct counts) counts the rows of each group with a marker, as the same aggregate did without the grouping set, where it kept a set of ids per group and per FILTER: PostgreSQL hands the column as a placeholder there, which the proof of its uniqueness now sees through. A report of two periods, four grouping sets and five distinct counts over 353,000 rows with a map of two keys each: 620 -> 260 ms (one backend, work_mem 4GB); the distinct rows of a grouping set over a preserved join are the bench case `distinct_rows_sets` (200,000 rows). A `count(DISTINCT x)` that keeps the set says why in `sift.explain_why` (`distinct_set`).
- Changed: pg_sift: the choice between a shared CTE and its inline plan (several planner runs of the statement) is kept in the backend, under the statement without its constants, the size class of each table it reads and the settings that move a plan, and a statement seen again is planned as the chosen variant only: up to 4,096 statements, under 1 MB, dropped when a table it reads is invalidated (DDL, an index, `ANALYZE`, `VACUUM`) and checked by a plan in full at every eighth hit. A stale verdict is the plan of the other variant, never other rows. On 156 statements of a replay planned in one session, then again with three other key values and a shorter date window, the plans of all 624 statements are the same as without the cache and 248 of the 324 choices came from it; `sift.explain_why` shows a `verdict_cached` row and `sift.stats()` counts `verdict_cache_hits`, `verdict_cache_misses` and `verdict_cache_flips`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the shared plan of a CTE that several aggregates read is made with `enable_memoize` off (restored after, also when planning fails), and the choice between it and the scans compares the scans with that plan. PostgreSQL prices a Memoize node over a lateral lookup of the CTE's rows on the cache hits it expects, and for a key that is almost unique per row (a jsonb document) there are none, so the shared plan cost less than it did and kept the CTE where the scans were faster. On a corpus of 258 statements 22 take the scans now, and each of the 18 timed runs faster with them; on a replay of 156 statements 3 change and the plans of the others are the same. A plan the shared CTE keeps has no Memoize node over its lookups.
- Changed: pg_sift: a CTE that several aggregates read, built from a table with a `sift_col` index joined to filtered tables (a dimension whose few rows own all of the table's, a list of ranges by containment), was estimated at a row or two, and the shared plan priced from it cost the same at ten times the rows as at one: the choice between the shared CTE and the scans kept the CTE (`cte_cost` in `sift.explain_why`). The rows of the CTE scan in the shared plan are now the geometric mean of that estimate and the largest result its body builds from the table, never fewer than the planner's own, and the extra charge for the aggregates that unnest the CTE stays what it was (it is computed on the planner's rows). The inline plan is not touched. A floor, not an estimate: a skewed join still needs its filter evaluated. `DEBUG1` shows the rows before and after.
- Changed: pg_sift: in a statement that reads a table with a `sift_col` index, a sub-select or CTE scan whose filters have no statistics (a comparison or `IS NULL` on a column of a window, an aggregate or a grouping sets output) expects the geometric mean of the planner's default guess and the rows it filters, not the guess alone, never fewer rows than the planner's own. Two such filters in a chain multiplied to one row, and every join above chose a nested loop that ran an aggregate once per row that came; the plan hashes now. A statement over tables without a column index keeps its plan. `DEBUG1` shows the rows before and after. In a replay of 156 statements at 4 GB and 4 MB of `work_mem`, 36 plans changed from a nested loop to a hash join with equal rows, the statements that use the scans stayed the same, and none was persistently slower.
- Changed: pg_sift: a top-N over a table with `order_columns` (`ORDER BY x LIMIT n`, the sorted run of each unit) reads the sort key of a unit's rows only for the rows it keeps, not for every row of the unit's window, and sizes its candidate buffers by the unit instead of the table's largest. 40 scans of `ORDER BY customer_id LIMIT 10..49` in one statement over 1,000,000 orders go from 48 ms to 24.8 ms; `ORDER BY rnd LIMIT 10` over 800,000 rows from 113 to 82 microseconds a scan, `LIMIT 1000` from 1.23 to 1.05 ms. The segment format does not change.
- Changed: pg_sift: a statement with a lateral sub-select the planner flattens (`LEFT JOIN LATERAL (aggregates) ON true`) plans the flat query first and, when it reads every table that has a column index in a scan, stops there: the query as written is not planned. Planning one such statement took 48 ms, now 25 ms (the same statement written with a grouping, 24 ms); the plans are the same on 657 statements at `random_page_cost` 4 and 1.1 and on 39 statements on two restored dumps.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a dictionary that a backend tests often (more than 32 equalities) is parsed and kept, but one the cache cannot hold (over half of `sift.dict_cache_kb`, 32 MB: 800,000 uuids, a million 64-character keys) was parsed again for every later test, 10 to 13 ms each. A dictionary the cache cannot hold is no longer parsed again for every test: it is searched in the stream, as before the 33rd test. A lookup of a uuid key over 800,000 rows stays at 0.036 ms (it was 13.9 ms from the 33rd), and 40 lookups of 64-character keys in one statement over 1,000,000 orders go from 782 ms to 3.3 ms.
- Changed: pg_sift: an `IN` list of up to 65,536 keys is looked up in the point directory (`point_columns`, `point_tid_columns`) by the access-method scan, where a list of more than 64 keys was answered by reading the units (the lookup cost 36 microseconds a key past the 64th and 1.2 to 2 below it): a list of more than 64 keys is still left to the units when it names a sixteenth of the rows or more. `SELECT id, amount, title FROM orders WHERE id IN (<100 ids>)` over 1,000,000 orders goes from 5.4 ms to 0.25 ms and a list of 1,000 ids from 10.6 ms to 1.75 ms. The segment format does not change.
- Changed: pg_sift: `x IS NULL` and `x IS NOT NULL` are tests of the NULL count of a unit's zone (a scalar column of the rows, in the aggregate scan of a `sift_col` index): a unit with no NULL (or only NULLs) is not read, and a unit whose rows all pass is answered from its pre-aggregates, as for a comparison its zone lies inside. `count(*), sum(amount) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL GROUP BY country` over 1,000,000 orders with 1% of them soft-deleted in one batch goes from 6.4 ms to 0.58 ms, the list of the deleted rows from 1.6 ms to 0.34 ms. The segment format does not change.
- Changed: docs: [docs/USAGE.md](docs/USAGE.md) says when to set `sift.agg_force` for a role (the cost model keeps the normal plan of a shared CTE that PostgreSQL prices for a row it expects and the scan would run better) and when not to, and `enable_nestloop = off` for a nested loop that reruns an aggregate once for each real row of an outer side estimated at one.
- Changed: pg_sift: the `DEBUG1` line `sift: shared CTE plain cost ...` of the choice between a shared CTE and its inline plan also gives the rows the scans read, the aggregates of the shared plan that unnest the CTE and what they were charged.
- Fixed: pg_sift: an `EXPLAIN` or `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` that was the first statement of a backend that had not preloaded the library showed (and `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` ran) the plan made before the library loaded, without the inline CTEs and flattened laterals. The executor start now plans the query of an `EXPLAIN` or a `CREATE TABLE AS` again through the hook, as it does for a plain `SELECT` ([docs/USAGE.md](docs/USAGE.md) section 1); a cursor and a query inside a function keep the plan they were given.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the charge of the plan that keeps a shared CTE for the rows its aggregates unnest took the rows the scans read from PostgreSQL's estimate of the table's filter, which for a filter on a jsonb path or an expression (no statistics) is a few rows of a table that keeps thousands: the rows are now the geometric mean of that estimate and the table's rows, as the group estimates have them. A statement whose scans won at the larger sizes of `sift.agg_scale_horizon` and lost the comparison at its own now takes them.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the plan that keeps a shared CTE was priced for the rows PostgreSQL guesses for what its aggregates unnest (a row for a CTE, 100 for a function) where the scans of the inline plan read tens of thousands, so the CTE was kept while inlining it let the scans win: when the scans replace most of the aggregates that unnest the CTE, those aggregates of the shared plan are charged the square root of the excess rows, in the shared work and in the total the plans are compared by. An aggregate that does not unnest, and a plan whose scans replace half of them or fewer, keep their estimate.
- Changed: pg_sift: `x LEFT JOIN LATERAL (SELECT avg(e) FROM f(x.doc) WHERE ..) s ON true` (an aggregate with no GROUP BY over a lateral function of the row, one row for each row of `x`) is read by the scan as the grouping of the elements by the key of `x`, as `GROUP BY x.id` over the lateral function would be, where `x` holds each of its keys once (a table, or a sub-select, CTE or DISTINCT whose tables are joined by their keys) and the aggregates (`avg`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `count` of an expression) have strict arguments, so that the row an outer join makes up gives what an aggregate of no rows gives. The lateral pull-up looks inside sublinks too. Anything else stays as written.
- Changed: pg_sift: a statement whose first plan still reads a table that has a column index in an ordinary plan takes its lateral-flattened plan when that plan reads every such table in a scan, whatever the plan above the scans is estimated to cost: that part is priced from the rows it takes for one, and the flattened plan was dropped for a higher total when the scans ran the work.
- Changed: pg_sift: the arms of a two-level aggregate that read the columns of a lateral `LIMIT 1` lookup (`rm.derived_cat AS category`, no `CASE` around it) are one scan when some test the lookup for NOT NULL and a third does not, also with a sub-select in front of the lookup that has a WHERE of its own (`WHERE score IS NOT NULL`): PostgreSQL leaves that condition at the join of the arms where the outer join became inner and in the WHERE of the third, and the arms were refused as `two_arms` and ran as rows. The conditions of the inner joins are compared as one WHERE.
- Added: pg_sift: `key_cut`: the units of a build, a promotion, a merge and a `REINDEX` are cut by the range of the key, whatever the heap pages of their rows, and list their heap addresses, so a lookup or a range of the key reads the units of its range only (one key value of 600: 1 of 100 units, against 8 of 8 by superblock) and keeps doing so after churn and a repack. The units hold up to 32,768 rows and have the summaries of groups of units (superunits) and the cubes of the page cut, so that a scan that does not use the key reads about the units of the page cut (1,200,000 rows of four integer columns: 37 units against 26; whole-table `sum` 0.09 ms against 0.09; `GROUP BY` of a cube column 0.17 ms against 0.45) and a key value reads 1 unit (0.11 ms against 0.64). The index is 17% smaller to 16% larger for narrow rows and 0.2% larger for wide ones, and the build takes 3.4 to 4.1 times the CPU, a promotion and a merge 3.5 times. The memory of a build and of a merge is bounded by `maintenance_work_mem`, not by the number of rows: a build writes a sorted segment each time the rows it holds pass half of it, and a merge is a k-way merge that holds one unit of each input and a window of the output.  3.2, 3.1 and 3.0 refuse minor 17 (`sift.upgrade_check` lists it; `ALTER INDEX x RESET (key_cut)` and a `REINDEX` roll it back, [docs/UPGRADING.md](docs/UPGRADING.md) section 4); the default of `sift.col_format_minor` stays 15.
- Changed: pg_sift: the plan of a statement with its laterals pulled up, when its shared and inline CTE plans cost the same as those of the first plan, is decided as the first was and is not planned again at the larger sizes (`sift.agg_scale_horizon`): the pulled-up form was the one PostgreSQL plans for the first anyway.
- Changed: pg_sift: the `cte_inline` row of `sift.explain_why` carries `scan_cost` and `normal_cost` (the totals of the plan with the shared CTEs inlined and of the plan that keeps them), for a choice either way; a `cte_cost` refusal could not be ranked by its costs before, only by the text of its reason.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.agg_force` (off; a diagnostic like `sift.plan_scale`): takes the aggregate scan wherever the planner can build it, whatever the plans cost, so the plan the cost model refused (`cost`, `cte_cost`) can be timed: the `enable_*` settings do not change the choice of a shared CTE.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the normal plan of an aggregate over the elements of a jsonb array was priced for the rows PostgreSQL guesses for a function (100 per call) and a join on a range, a few rows where the table's own filters keep tens of thousands, so the scan lost the comparison it wins: the paths that are not the scan are charged the square root of the excess.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the private memory of a `sift_col` build with many small units (a segment of thousands of units and a cube over several columns) is bounded by the segment, not by the units it holds: the merge of each group of units into its superunit summary kept what it allocated until the segment was finished.
- Changed: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT` over a sort column that does not follow the table (a top-N whose walk visits the units by the best value of their zones) keeps the order of that walk in the backend, for the same index file and directory version and the same sort keys, up to four orders of 16 MB each: the zones of every superblock are read and the superblocks sorted once, not at every query, and the superblocks that hold rows of a write buffer are visited first, as before. The rows, and the units read and skipped, are those of the walk without the kept order.
- Changed: pg_sift: a statement with laterals the scan can read is planned a second time with them pulled up only when its first plan still reads a table that has a column index outside a SiftAgg node; where every such table is read by the scan already, the second plan could not run more in it, and it was planned (and its inline CTE variants with it) for nothing.
- Fixed: pg_sift: an average or sum over a `numeric` column of a precision past 18 digits (`numeric(30, 16)`) whose units hold many distinct values was planned as the scan, which is slower than the normal plan there (each unit builds an entry for each new value); the planner now prices an entry as measured and keeps the normal plan for such a column, and the scan for the column of few values. The answers are the same in both.
- Tests: `upgrade_settings` (the newest 3.2 release writes a database with `ALTER DATABASE` and `ALTER ROLE ...
