SET sift.*` stored, one of them a setting the new library does not know): after the update the session starts with a warning ("removing it"), the stored settings apply, `sift.col_auto_merge` is on and `sift.col_auto_merge_wal_pct` 5 where nothing set them, the update prints nothing about the auto merge, `CREATE INDEX` prints its NOTICE once, and `pg_dump -C` brings the settings and verifying indexes back; `pg_upgrade` carries the stored settings of a database and of a role in it.
- Tests: `pg_upgrade` runs with a released old side (`pg_upgrade_32`, `pg_upgrade_31`: the newest 3.2 and 3.1 tag, built for the old major, `SIFT_PG_OLD_REF` in `pg_upgrade.sh`): the release writes BM25, `sift_col` (without the options of 3.3) and `sift_table` objects, `pg_upgrade --check` and the upgrade carry them, answers are equal before the extension is updated and after `ALTER EXTENSION UPDATE`, the preflight blocks nothing, and writes, VACUUM and merges pass. Across majors it needs the previous major's binaries (`SIFT_PG_OLD_BIN` or the PGDG directory).
- Fixed: pg_sift: a scan that fuses the arms of a two-level aggregate reported to the plan above the sum of the groups of its arms, though each arm has no more groups than the rows it reads: the inline plan of a shared CTE looked several times dearer than it is and the shared plan, which keeps an arm off the scan, was kept. The node reports at most the rows its arms aggregate.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.upgrade_check('3.2')` (and `scripts/upgrade-check.sh --to 3.2`) did not list an index with `point_tid_columns` (column format 16) as a blocker for a rollback to 3.2, 3.1 or 3.0, which refuse it; `SET sift.col_format_minor` and `REINDEX` is the advice it gives, and `docs/UPGRADING.md` section 4 tells how to drop the option first.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the private memory of a `sift_col` build is bounded by `maintenance_work_mem`, independent of the number of rows: the writer of a finished segment was never freed, so the memory of `CREATE INDEX` grew with the number of segments it had written. The index bytes are the same.
- Changed: pg_sift: a run of a correlated `LIMIT 1` child per distinct input is priced at half of PostgreSQL's cost of the child (`AGG_CHILD_RUN`; it was all of it, from a sweep of tables of 100 to 122,000 rows and a mapping child), and a row of a first-row lookup table at a tenth of a unit (`AGG_FIRST_ROW`; was one). The two-level scan that runs the child once for the tuples of its arms is now chosen over the normal plan above the three separate arms it replaces, and the first-row table is still read where the child would run for more inputs than the table has rows.
- Changed: pg_sift: the arms of a two-level scan may differ in the type of the join of a lateral `LIMIT 1` child (a lookup in a mapping table): a NOT NULL test of what the child returns makes PostgreSQL join it by an inner join in the arms that have the test, and by an outer join in an arm without it, and the arms were refused as `two_arms`. The scan keeps the outer join (a row the child has no row for is a tuple of NULLs, which the arm without the test counts) and the arms with the test reject that tuple, which is why PostgreSQL made their join an inner one; a join of anything else (a table, a child without `LIMIT`) that differs keeps the refusal. Test `agg_two_level_arm_where`, fuzz family `twolevel`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the first N rows of each group asked with a window function (`WHERE rn <= N`, `rn < N + 1` over a sub-select or a CTE read once with the one window function `row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY .. ORDER BY ..)`, over a table with a column index) are also planned as the group's arrays of each column, ordered, cut at N elements and unnested with their ordinal as `rn` (`array_agg(c ORDER BY ..)`, `unnest(a[1:N]) WITH ORDINALITY`), and that plan is kept when it runs in the scan and costs less than the window. The arrays hold the rows of each group: a query whose groups do not fit the memory of a hash aggregate keeps the window. `sift.explain_why` names `window_first` for a window it leaves as written.
- Changed: pg_sift: VACUUM leaves the large segments of a `sift_col` index that is due for the automatic rewrite where they are: it moved them into the free blocks below, a copy of the bytes of the index that also made the segment new, which started the WAL budget of the rewrite (`sift.col_auto_merge_wal_pct`) over, so the rewrite was refused every second cycle and the units stayed dirty. Churn of 3% of the rows in 10 cycles, 50,000 to 800,000 rows, the server's own worker: the units that answer from their pre-aggregates after the maintenance, 50 to 75% of the cycles before, are every cycle with 400-byte rows (50% with light rows, where the budget refuses every second rewrite), for the same WAL (6.2 against 6.2 MB a cycle at 400,000 rows). The peak size of the index file is unchanged (2.5 to 2.7 times the live bytes at 200,000 rows and more), the average size while the churn lasts is higher (2.7 against 2.0 times, 100,000 rows, 40 cycles) because the large segment is no longer compacted at every VACUUM; when the rewrite is not due VACUUM moves the segments as before
- Fixed: pg_sift: the VACUUM that the `sift.col_auto_merge` worker runs flushes the WAL first: with `synchronous_commit = off` the commit record of the churn was not on disk, PostgreSQL sets no hint bit for such a transaction and the pages that held only its new row versions stayed out of the visibility map (92 of 1,910 pages after a change of 5% of the rows, 0 now), so the scans that followed checked their rows in the heap until the next VACUUM; with `synchronous_commit = on` (the default) nothing changes
- Fixed: pg_sift: the worker that rewrites a `sift_col` index by partitions (`sift.col_auto_merge_partition_kb`, and every index over `sift.vacuum_rewrite_limit`) slept less than `sift.maintenance_cost_delay` asks for: the pages written were counted for each partition, and the pages under 16 of each (the rest, never carried to the next) earned no delay; a rewrite of 89 pages with a delay of 100 ms took 272 ms, 468 ms now
- Fixed: pg_sift: the aggregate scan of a `sift_table` skips the units whose zones exclude the filters of the statement (a range, an equality, an `IN` list, `IS NULL` tests, an `OR`), as it does for a `sift_col` index: it began every scan with no zone test, so a range on an ordered column read every unit (2,000,000 rows, a range of 10,000: 27 units and 4.3 ms, now 1 unit and 1.6 ms). The test of the filters moved to one place both sources use (`agg/zoneprune.c`) (test `tbl_zone_prune`).
- Added: tests: `test/fuzz/diff_fuzz.py --table` runs the random queries of the differential fuzz on `sift_table` fact tables (no `sift_col` index, jsonb paths declared with `sift.table_set_paths`) across DML, rolled back writes, VACUUM [FULL] and `sift.table_maintain`, against the normal plan of the same table; the families that need an option of the column index (`key_cut`, `order_columns`, the point directory) are skipped, and `scripts/ci.sh` runs it as the job `difftest-fuzz-tbl`.

## 3.3.0 - 2026-10-24

- Fixed: pg_sift: `UNION ALL` arms that share one pass over the index (`Arms In One Pass`) and include an aggregate of a lookup's column (`count(d.label)` over a joined table) failed with `sift: aggregate kind 14 has no partial table` whenever a parallel worker took a share of the scan; the shared pass now runs in the leader alone when its aggregates are not ones the leader can merge. It showed on a small table only when the workers happened to get a morsel (an arm64 runner); `agg_fuse_aggs` now runs with `parallel_leader_participation = off` so that the workers always scan.
- Added: bench: twelve cases of realistic query shapes in `bench/cases.tsv` (grouping_sets_report, ranked_top_per_group, window_json_report, two_level_spend, group_by_id_top, count_distinct_unique, semi_exists_self, anti_not_in_self, keyset_page, point_agg_fk, point_agg_id, jsonb_each_member): reports with grouping sets, ranks and JSON documents over the groups, aggregates of aggregates, one group per row, semi and anti joins on the same table, keyset pages, lookups by key and one member of a JSON object read through `jsonb_each`; `bench/bench cases --work-mem 4GB` times every lane at a large work_mem.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `UNION ALL` arms that share one pass over the index (`Arms In One Pass`) and include an aggregate of a lookup's column (`count(d.label)` over a joined table) failed with `sift: aggregate kind 14 has no partial table` whenever a parallel worker took a share of the scan; the shared pass now runs in the leader alone when its aggregates are not ones the leader can merge. It showed on a small table only when the workers happened to get a morsel (an arm64 runner); `agg_fuse_aggs` now runs with `parallel_leader_participation = off` so that the workers always scan.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a UNION ALL whose arms run in one pass could stop the backend (SIGSEGV) when a group key held a hashed sub-select (`CASE WHEN kind IN (SELECT id FROM kinds) THEN lookup.name END`) next to a column of a lookup join and other sub-selects of the statement came before it: the pass renumbered the sub-select's own output parameter as a column of the join and read past the end of the join's columns; the parameter keeps its number.
- Tests: `compat_matrix` runs every release tag of the 3.0, 3.1 and 3.2 series (v3.0.0 to the newest, picked from `git tag`, so a new release joins with its tag; `SIFT_COMPAT_EXTRA_REFS` adds a release in preparation): ALTER EXTENSION UPDATE with indexes of every kind, answers equal, `col_verify` clean, and the release reading what this tree writes or refusing it. It ran three releases before.
- Changed: pg_sift: the set of a `count(DISTINCT)` over an integer column of a wide range starts as a hash set, not a bitmap, when the groups hold few values (the planner's rows over its groups are fewer than 16 times the chunks of 4096 values the zones span): a bitmap costs a directory and 512 bytes for each chunk a group has a value in, 880 bytes a group for a column of 100,000 values with a row or two of each in a group. 1 million rows over 100,000 groups of a column of 100,000 values: 293 to 48 ms and 484 to 63 MB of peak memory; over 1 million groups, 444 to 116 ms and 876 to 148 MB (`work_mem = 4GB`). A group of many values (1,000 groups) keeps the bitmap. The answers are exact in both.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a parallel scan whose worker failed could report `sift: a worker of a parallel aggregate stopped without its result` (08006) instead of the error of the worker (`integer out of range`, 22003) when the leader saw the worker leave before it had handled the signal that carries the error; the leader now reads the error queues first.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `ORDER BY` a `bytea` column with `OFFSET` and `LIMIT` in the top-N scan could return other rows than the normal plan (rows of the empty string, `'\\x'`, were skipped) after the backend had run several scans over the same dictionary.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a grouped scan of a partitioned table failed with `relation N has no parent because it's being detached` when a `DETACH PARTITION .. CONCURRENTLY` of one of its partitions was in progress while the statement was planned; it now plans as it did before the group-by-partition-key finish.
- Changed: pg_sift: a row comparison in the WHERE of an aggregate or top-N scan, `(a, b) > ($1, $2)` (the page of a keyset pagination), is planned as the comparisons it means, `a > $1 OR (a = $1 AND b > $2)`, which the zones, the summaries and the kernels test: `ORDER BY c, id LIMIT 10` from a key 60,000 of 128,000 rows on reads 5 of 500 units (0.3 ms), 239 before (6.4 ms); the rows are those of the normal plan for each operator, NULLs, three columns and parameters
- Changed: pg_sift: an aggregate scan that begins for every morsel of units (SiftAgg) finds the superblocks the zones and summaries leave a unit in once for the scan, and does not begin the others: a request that prunes nearly every unit of a table of 1,000 units costs 381 instructions a unit (callgrind, 500 and 1,000 units), 1,676 before, of which 126 are the planner's sample (at most 2,048 units) and 56 the count of all-visible pages (now with the popcnt instruction where the CPU has it); the units read and pruned are the same. Finding the unit of a superblock in a segment gallops from the unit at the same share of the directory, not from its middle
- Changed: pg_sift: an aggregate scan that begins for every morsel of units (SiftAgg) finds the superblocks the zones and summaries leave a unit in once for the scan, and does not begin the others: a request that prunes nearly every unit of a table of 1,000 units costs 268 instructions a unit between 600 and 1,800 units (callgrind), 1,676 before, of which 210 are the planner's sample (at most 2,048 units, so it stops growing) and 88 the count of all-visible pages (now with the popcnt instruction where the CPU has it); the zones of each word of 64 units clear the word when none of its units can meet a range or list, so a request on a clustered key tests the words, not the units, and the scan visits only the superblocks that hold a unit (0.03 to 0.08 ms at 1,800 units, 0.22 ms at 1,000 before); the units read and pruned are the same. Finding the unit of a superblock in a segment gallops from the unit at the same share of the directory, not from its middle
- Changed: pg_sift: a group key of a two-level scan that holds a hashed sub-select (`CASE WHEN kind IN (SELECT id FROM kinds) THEN .. END`) is one every arm groups by when the arms' sub-selects are the same query: each arm plans a sub-plan of its own for it, and the keys now compare equal when they differ in nothing but that planning's numbers, so the scan compiles the key once and asks the sub-select once, where the statement was refused as `two_keys`. Arms with different sub-selects keep the refusal. Test `agg_two_level_subplan_key`, fixture `s2_per_order_dimension_subselect`, fuzz family `twolevel`.
- Changed: pg_sift: `sift.col_auto_merge` is on by default (3.2: off; it needs `sift.async_maintenance`, on), so that a `sift_col` index keeps answering from its pre-aggregates after churn without a setting: a worker vacuums the table when a thousandth of its rows changed and rewrites the index after the `VACUUM`, within `sift.col_auto_merge_wal_pct` (5) of the WAL the server writes. The rewrite writes the bytes of the index, so it waits until the server has written twenty times that since the index was written (its build or its last rewrite, read from the page LSN of its oldest large segment: nothing is stored), whatever the width of the rows, the full-page images or the other indexes make of the WAL; `0` rewrites at every `VACUUM` that finds the index due, which cost 12% to 28% more WAL without full-page images and 0% to 3% with them (1M rows, 3% of the rows changed per round, 20 rounds). With the default: +0.9% to +1.9% WAL with full-page images and a checkpoint per round, the units answerable from their pre-aggregates 32% to 51% (wide rows) and 33% to 83% (scraper mix), and nothing changes for a hot set or for new rows. `sift.col_auto_merge_wal_pct` replaces `sift.col_auto_merge_min_churn_pct` (never released), `sift.col_maintenance_status` names the wait in `blocked_by`, `CREATE INDEX` and `REINDEX` of a `sift_col` index say what the worker does and how to stop it in a NOTICE, and it applies to the indexes that exist when the library is loaded after the update (`sift.col_auto_merge = off` for the server or the database keeps 3.2). The test clusters of `scripts/pgtest.sh` run with it off.
- Changed: pg_sift: a group table that spills writes each `count(DISTINCT)` set, and the next table of the scan starts the words of its narrow sets with room for as many groups as the table before it held, where each table grew them from 64 groups by doubling and copying: the scan of 38,646 groups with two such sets under `work_mem = 1MB` executes 2% fewer instructions (454 to 445 million; the time moves less than the noise of a busy host). The words count in the hash memory as they grew before.
- Changed: pg_sift: the scan is priced for the units its join key filters keep as the data has them, so the plan no longer leaves the scan when the table grows and the request stays the same. The keys of a lookup whose rows PostgreSQL only guesses (the output of a plan) are counted as `lookup_set_ratio` counts them (the geometric mean of its estimate and the leaf's distinct values); the share of units whose zone holds one of them comes from the sample of the real zones (a few wide zones among narrow ones made a mean width that kept every unit), cut by the small-set summaries of the units; and the normal plan that reads the table by the probes of a join below a merge or hash join (or a Materialize) is charged those keys, as the probes of a nested loop alone were. At 316 and 3,160 distinct keys the scan cost 3,208 and 8,880 and the normal plan 389,929 and 738,908 for a request of 32; the scan cost 4,160 and 11,315 and the normal plan 18,989 and 6,841 before, and was refused at the larger size.
- Tests: `agg_scope_cost` adds the facts joined to a third table; `agg_semi_lut` runs its workers case without the join key filters, which now keep too few units for workers to pay.
- Changed: pg_sift: the price of a `LIMIT 1` child per distinct input, against its table read once as a first-row lookup, counts the inputs of an unnested list by the distinct values the index holds for each field of its elements (the widest field of an element, as a scoped lookup already did) and by the tuples the scan reads, not by PostgreSQL's default of 200 values a field and the rows it expects of the join, which a filter it has no statistics for leaves at a few. A child over a thousand elements with a key of their own now reads its table once (`agg_first_row_lookup` adds the statement and its three-document twin, which keeps the child).
- Tests: `agg_first_row_lookup` adds a list of elements whose filter PostgreSQL has no statistics for.
- Changed: pg_sift: a scan with workers is priced for starting them, 3 ms and 0.35 ms for each (the larger of that and `AGG_SETUP_FACTOR` times `parallel_setup_cost`), so a low `parallel_setup_cost` no longer sends a scan of a few milliseconds to workers; and a `count(DISTINCT)` is priced for the leader merging the (group, value) pairs of the workers' sets into its own one by one, so its workers do not scale and a grouped one stays serial where they do not pay. A `parallel_setup_cost` of 0 asks for the workers and prices neither. The sum, count and average scans keep their workers at the default and benchmark costs.
- Tests: `agg_distinct_cost` adds the workers planned for a sum and for a `count(DISTINCT)` at setup costs of 0, 1 and 100.
- Changed: pg_sift: the items that the participants of a scan with workers take from the counter are a quarter of the span of a superunit (were the whole span), so that a table of a few hundred superblocks gives them more than two items. The superunits an item holds whole are still answered from their summary; a serial scan keeps the span.
- Changed: pg_sift: a group table that spills writes each `count(DISTINCT)` set that is narrow (a column of up to 512 values) as the words of its window, one row for each group that has a bit, and a parallel worker sends it the same way; the receiver ORs the words into its own and counts the new bits by popcount, where it was one (group, value) pair for each value, walked once for the partitions and added one by one: the scan of 38,646 groups with two such sets under `work_mem = 1MB` executes 1% fewer instructions and takes 5% less time, and a dictionary column's words go with the values of the ids that have a bit. The blobs are the temporary state between a scan and its merge (spill files and worker messages); nothing stored changes.
- Changed: pg_sift: a backend keeps the checked head of a segment (its schema and unit directory) and the zone arrays of each leaf it has read (`dict_cache_kb`) for the next query, where each query read and validated both again: a hit on a table of 782 units costs 25 to 60 instructions a unit less (uuid, integer and text columns; callgrind), and a segment that is rewritten, a damaged header or a cache with no room reads them as before. `sift._test_col_dict_cache` counts the loads and hits
- Changed: pg_sift: counting the all-visible pages of the heap (each plan of a column index and each aggregate scan does) reads the visibility map a word at a time, not through a copy of each word: 185 instructions a unit of 256 pages less (callgrind, tables of 391 and 782 units), the count is the same
- Changed: pg_sift: a group table that spilled starts the next table of the scan with room for as many groups as the one it wrote (the keys, the hash slots and the per-group arrays), where each table grew from 1,024 groups by doubling and copying its arrays again: the aggregate scan of 38,646 groups under `work_mem = 1MB` executes 8% fewer instructions (496 to 459 million), 5% less time. The room counts in the hash memory as the empty table does; the answers do not change.
- Changed: pg_sift: the scan is no longer priced for the bitmap chunks of a `count(DISTINCT)` of a column whose values fall in a window of 512 (`dist_narrow_words`, 8 words) or less: the range of the column's zones, or the dictionary of a text column. Those sets keep a few words for each group, since the narrow form. `count(DISTINCT quantity), count(DISTINCT status)` of 38,646 groups was priced 13,953 at `work_mem` 16 MB against 2,552 at 4 MB (the normal plan 22,475): the price no longer rises with the memory.
- Tests: `leak_path_vets` (TAP): a session that recorded the heap check of an empty visibility map ends normally and another is terminated in a statement; the leak gate finds nothing left over. The context of the heap-check list is named `sift cache path vets`: a backend keeps it on purpose.
- Changed: pg_sift: `sift.explain_why` says what its `scan_cost` and `normal_cost` are: the totals of the path that was built and of the cheapest normal path at the grouping stage, before an `ORDER BY` or a `LIMIT` above it (PostgreSQL chooses by the cost of the top node, a `LIMIT` included); the numbers are unchanged. The test `agg_refusals` checks that a `LIMIT` leaves them as they are.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a partitioned table whose partitions keep different key lists (`key_columns = auto` follows the data of each partition: one keeps the list of an integer column, its neighbour does not) is one source of the scan again, counting on the lists every partition keeps, instead of keeping the normal plan with `partition_source` (a table of 128 partitions by hash of a column with a few values in each did). A unit without a list is read by its rows. Test: `agg_part_presence`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the backend's cache of parsed dictionaries and list lengths (`dict_cache_kb`) finds its least recently used entry in a list, not by walking the whole table for each entry it drops: a query that touches more units than the cache holds (about 12,800 at the default) drops one entry for each it adds, and the walk was as long as the cache.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the histogram of a date or timestamp column asked of a superunit summary (a table of more than one unit; `count(DISTINCT)` of the date) read the column array of the schema past its end, a heap overflow that an address sanitizer reports: the calendar cells of a date are not merged into a superunit, so it now says it has none, as it did in the runs that did not fail.
- Changed: pg_sift: a scan that begins for each morsel of units (SiftAgg) keeps the units its zones and summaries cleared for the next begin while the predicates stay the same (not for an OR, a point directory answer or a wide integer), where each begin tested its units again. 782 units, `count(*)` and `avg` of a column with every unit pruned: 4.5 M instructions before, 2.6 M after (`count(*), sum(col)`: 3.5 M, 2.1 M).
- Tests: `agg_visibility_refusal` plans a refused shared-CTE statement and another aggregate statement in one transaction with `sift.explain_refusals` on, and runs in the assert lane (`scripts/assert.sh`): the build before the fix of the heap-check list (3648712) fails it with an assertion failure in `lappend`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the list lengths a backend keeps for the next query of a list leaf (`dict_cache_kb`) are 16 bit and the running ends are summed again from them, 5.1 kB a unit of 2,076 rows, 18.5 kB before: the 64 MB of the cache hold the lengths of about 12,800 units, not 3,500 (a unit with a list of more than 65,535 elements is not kept). Results are the same.
- Changed: pg_sift: the scan is priced for the jsonb values that the test of a lookup join without an equality key builds: `LEFT JOIN t ON jsonb_build_array(a) = jsonb_build_array(b::date)` (a cast of the column of the table is not a hash key) tests every row of the table for every distinct value of `a`, and each test builds two jsonb values in PostgreSQL's executor, 70 operators each, at the executor's speed instead of the scan's. A 701-day series over 200,000 events was priced at 22,983 for the scan and 51,857 for the merge join, and ran slower in the scan; it keeps the normal plan now. The join on the column itself is a hash key and keeps the scan. Test: `agg_unkeyed_json_cost`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the set of a `count(DISTINCT)` over a column whose values fall in a window of 512 (a status, a month, a dictionary of few values, a small code) keeps up to 8 words of bits for each group, 64 bytes at most, where a bitmap keeps a directory and a chunk of 1,024 bytes: the state of two such sets over 38,646 groups goes from 77 MB to 3 MB, and over 100,000 groups of a 5-value column from 102 MB to 1 MB. A value outside the window widens it, and past 512 values the set becomes a bitmap; an integer column whose zones span more than the window starts as one. The limit `dist_narrow_words` (docs/LIMITS.md) sets the words; 0 turns the form off.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.explain_why` and `sift.explain_refusals` could crash the backend (a segmentation fault) on a statement whose plan keeps an aggregate scan while the visibility map of its table is empty: the heap check of each path the arms priced was kept in a memory context that the choice between the plans of a statement had deleted before it was read. It is kept in a context of its own now.
- Changed: pg_sift: a scan that begins for each morsel of units (SiftAgg) tests the zones of the units of that morsel only, and stops at the first predicate that leaves none, where each begin tested every unit of the segment for every predicate: the zone pass of a scan grew with the square of its units and with each aggregated column. 782 units, `count(*)` and `avg` of a column with every unit pruned: 14.0 M instructions before, 4.5 M after (`count(*), sum(col)`: 7.8 M, 3.5 M).
- Added: pg_sift: a serial scan of a partitioned table grouped by its partition key (the column itself), with a `count(DISTINCT)`, finishes the groups of a partition when its units end and starts the group table again, so the table holds the groups of one partition, not of all, and does not spill at `work_mem` (`EXPLAIN` shows `Groups Finished: per partition`; the cost is that of one partition's table). Not for a scan with workers, a float sum or average, grouping sets, a partition of a partition or an expression as the partition key. Test `agg_part_final`.
- Tests: `agg_group_spill_fuzz`: 64 seeded `count(DISTINCT)` mixes under the smallest hash memory, the scan forced, against the normal plan's rows; the build before the fix of short counts in a merged partition fails it on 3 seeds.
- Changed: pg_sift: a generic plan of `col = ANY($1)` on an integer column prices the scan for the table of the list (a byte table over the whole range of the column, for the `sift.agg_param_set_size` elements it assumes), as it does for a list it can see: it was left out, and the plan cache, which compares a generic plan with the average of the custom ones, adopted the scan for lists of 256 keys over a range of a million where the index plan is several times faster.
- Changed: pg_sift: the first plan of a backend on a `sift_col` index counts the pre-aggregate sections of up to 256 of its units (spread over the segments, in proportion to their units), not of every unit: it read 2.5 buffers of each, 2,921 buffers of 1,000 units of an index of seven leaves and a cube, 1,064 now (7.2 ms of planning to 2.0 ms), and an index of 15,000 units 42,000. The planner uses the share of the units that have a section, which the units counted give; an index of 256 units or fewer is counted whole.
- Changed: pg_sift: the scan is priced for the table of an `IN` list on an integer column (a byte table over the range of its values, a bit table for a long list over a wider range), which it allocates and clears when it starts whatever the rows it reads: a list of 256 keys over a range of a million values added about 2 ms to a scan that read two units, and the planner took the scan where the index plan was several times faster. A hash table (a range past 4,194,304 values) is not charged.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a `count(DISTINCT)` aggregate scan over a column source that gives no zone of a range of units (the test source `sift_colstub`) crashed the backend with a null function call, because the streaming `count(DISTINCT)` (`sift.agg_dist_stream`) asked for that zone; it now takes the streaming path only when the source has it, and the other sources are unchanged.
- Docs: docs/USAGE.md, memory of a backend: with `MALLOC_MMAP_THRESHOLD_=131072` for the server a backend that replays the mix of large aggregate statements holds 70 MB instead of 104 MB, for the same CPU per statement within the noise of the runs; the library does not call `mallopt` or `malloc_trim`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a `count(DISTINCT)` of a scan whose group table spilled could return counts that missed values: when the set of a group outgrew hash memory while a partition was merged, its values went through the sort, and the groups of the partition were returned before the counts were taken from it (`agg_group_spill`, the sets of 800 groups of 94 rows at 64kB). The scan that spills and the scan that does not give the same counts.
- Changed: pg_sift: the groups of an aggregate scan go to the hash table, not to the direct arrays, once the arrays would take more than twice hash memory (`work_mem` times `hash_mem_multiplier`): the arrays hold every aggregate of every slot of the product of the keys' rooms, a million slots for ten keys, so a statement of 28 aggregates held 218 MB for 1,200 groups.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a scan that groups by seven or more keys cleared group arrays of 4^7 slots or more before it read a row, even when the query found a handful of groups or none: when the planner expects far fewer groups than the arrays have slots, the groups start in the hash table.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.col_verify(index, true)` failed with `invalid memory alloc request size 1342177280` on a `sift_col` index of more than 16.7 million rows: the hashes of the rows it compares with the heap (40 bytes each, in one array that doubles) outgrew the 1 GB limit of an ordinary allocation. The array may be larger now, so the check runs on any size the memory allows (about 80 bytes a row).
- Changed: pg_sift: a statement whose CTEs the planner hook weighs (shared or inlined, as written or with its lateral sub-selects pulled up) keeps the memory of the plan it uses, not of the up to four plans it made: each plan is made in a context of its own and the other ones are deleted (planner memory of a CTE statement with many aggregate arms, 134 MB at most before, 44 MB; the plans are the same). Planning takes up to a quarter longer when the C library returns the freed blocks to the system at once (with `MALLOC_MMAP_THRESHOLD_` and `MALLOC_TRIM_THRESHOLD_` raised it takes as long as before).
- Changed: pg_sift: a group table that spills writes each `count(DISTINCT)` set (and each distinct `sum` and `avg`) in one walk for all its partitions instead of one walk for each, and a `count(DISTINCT)` whose groups hold few values starts the tables after the first spill as hash sets instead of building and moving bitmaps again; the answers are the same.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the access-method scan of a `sift_col` index (`sift.enable_col_scan`) by what it does: a start-up for opening the index and pruning the units (which a `LIMIT` does not wait for), then the units kept, the values filtered and the rows handed over, in about microseconds; an equality or short `IN` on a column with a point directory (`point_columns`) is priced from the rows of its keys (text, uuid and a parameter of a generic plan included, which cost 4,000 and now cost as the constant does), and the zones of a long `IN` list are bisected instead of tested against every key (a list of 50,000 values adds about 3 ms to the planning). Against the measured time, the median error of the price falls from 2.3x to 1.9x (6.7x to 2.1x for the point lookups).
- Changed: pg_sift: the VACUUM of a `sift_col` index leaves alone a tier of clean segments that share at most the superblock at their edge (the segments of a parallel build, of rows appended and promoted) when they total more than `sift.vacuum_rewrite_limit` and the directory has room: the merge gave a reader nothing and wrote all their bytes, so the first VACUUM after a parallel build rewrote the index (nine segments of 900,000 rows: 1.3 MB of WAL for the VACUUM against 8.8 MB). A dead row in one of them, or a full directory, merges them as before.
- Fixed: pg_sift: before PostgreSQL 18 the aggregate scan returned a constant group key as the constant in the sets that leave it out (`SELECT 'cur' AS label, status, count(*) .. GROUP BY GROUPING SETS ((1, 2), ())` gave `'cur'`, not NULL, in the grand-total row): PostgreSQL's setrefs.c never replaces a bare constant of a custom scan's output with a column of its scan tuple, so the output now reads the constant through a relabel to its own type. Test `agg_grouping_set_keys`, fixture `k_group_const_sets` (its `ORDER BY 1` on the constant is no order before 18: it orders by the other columns).
- Fixed: pg_sift: `CREATE INDEX` and `REINDEX` of a `sift_col` index with parallel workers showed no heap blocks in `pg_stat_progress_create_index` (`blocks_total` and `blocks_done` stayed 0) and counted rows only while the leader scanned; the workers now report the blocks and rows they did through the leader, as the BM25 build does (`test/isolation/specs/col_build_progress.spec`).
- Changed: pg_sift: the access-method scan of a `sift_col` index probes the point directory before it walks the zones and set summaries of the units (when the directory answers, the walk would only narrow what it already narrowed), and skips the pruned units a word of the keep bitmap at a time, so an equality or short `IN` list costs less for each unit of the index; the rows and the units read do not change (a regress counts them over a segment of 65 units).
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.col_index_status`, one row per `sift_col` index of a table the caller can read: its size and that of its segments, the free and reusable blocks, the segments against the directory's 128, the dead rows, the write buffer and the state of the worker, joined with `sift.col_maintenance_status`, so the state of an index on a table of hundreds of millions of rows is one `SELECT`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a scan that groups by several keys paid a fixed 10 ms before it read a row: its group arrays are indexed by the keys' ids together, so their room is the product of the keys' rooms (a date key of 60 days, a category, a sub-category, a period: 32,768 slots for 62 groups), and each doubling of a key's room copied and zeroed the arrays of every aggregate in all of it. When the planner expects fewer than a quarter of the slots to be groups and fewer than a sixteenth hold one, the groups go to the hash table. A daily series of an average (a calendar of 60 days left-joined to the rows of 51 parents, unnested json, 7 aggregates of which 5 `count(DISTINCT)`), 195,000 rows, warm, minimum of 5: 47.5 ms -> 5.5 ms (the normal plan 19.1), one parent 11.8 -> 1.4 ms (normal 0.9); the 258 shapes of a query family of six endpoints, natural plans with a full map: 27.8 s -> 24.9 s. Test `agg_group_sparse`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a top-N scan (`ORDER BY <interval column> LIMIT n`, `sift_col` and `sift_table`) takes an interval column as a sort key, as it took a numeric of more than 18 digits: the entries of the dictionary of its datums are compared with `interval_cmp`, so `1 mon`, `30 days` and `720:00:00` tie and each row shows its own spelling. At 200,000 rows `LIMIT 10`: 28.4 ms to 12.7 ms for distinct values (`LIMIT 100` 32.3 to 14.8, `LIMIT 1000` 27.6 to 17.7), 26.1 ms to 4.8 ms for 300 distinct values, 11.3 ms to 0.38 ms with a `WHERE` that leaves 1% of the rows.
- Changed: pg_sift: a top-N scan (`ORDER BY <enum column> LIMIT n`, `sift_col` and `sift_table`) takes an enum column as a sort key, in the order of its type: the column units hold the OIDs of the values, which are not in that order (`ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE BEFORE/AFTER` changes it without touching a row), so the scan reads the order from `pg_enum` when it opens and ranks each OID; the zones of the column (of OIDs) do not bound the scan. At 200,000 rows `LIMIT 10`: 21.4 ms to 3.2 ms for a type of 5 values, 20.4 ms to 6.8 ms for a type of 301 values, 8.4 ms to 0.14 ms with a `WHERE` that leaves 1% of the rows.
- Changed: pg_sift: a top-N scan (`ORDER BY <numeric column of more than 18 digits, or without a precision> LIMIT n`, `sift_col` and `sift_table`) takes the column as a sort key, as it took a numeric of up to 18 digits: the entries of the dictionary of its datums (a NaN among them, which sorts last, the lanes keep it as NULL plus a flag) are compared with `numeric_cmp`, so a query that sorted the whole table and kept `n` rows reads the column units. At 200,000 rows of `numeric(30,4)`, `LIMIT 10`: 24.5 ms to 15.6 ms for distinct values (`LIMIT 100` 25.0 to 15.9, `LIMIT 1000` 30.4 to 20.4), 27.0 ms to 2.8 ms for 300 distinct values, 8.9 ms to 0.25 ms with a `WHERE` that leaves 1% of the rows.
- Changed: pg_sift: a top-N scan (`ORDER BY <text under C, uuid or bytea column> LIMIT n`, `sift_col` and `sift_table`) compares the rows of a unit with the bar by the order code of the dictionary of their segment, two integers, not by their bytes: the position of each entry in byte order when the dictionary has it (stored, or sorted after the scans of a backend compared four times its entries) or the first 8 bytes of each entry (made once the rows compared reach half of its entries, and dropped for a dictionary where a quarter of the comparisons tie), for dictionaries of up to 1,048,576 entries; a `bytea` has the prefix code only. At 200,000 rows `LIMIT 10`/`100`: a text column of 20,000 distinct values 3.7 ms to 1.2 ms, 200,000 distinct 2.8 ms to 2.2 ms (2.5x when the query repeats on a backend), six values 4.6 ms to 3.0 ms, strings that share their first 8 bytes 2.9 ms to 2.4 ms, `bytea` 6.6 ms to 2.5 ms; with a `WHERE` that leaves a few hundred to 20,000 rows no change. EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows `Top-N Units By Order Code`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a top-N scan (`ORDER BY <uuid or bytea column> LIMIT n` over a `sift_col` index) reads a uuid or bytea sort key as the codes of the dictionary the segment stores, not through a dictionary of aligned datums built for every unit, when no other part of the scan reads the leaf (a `WHERE` or an expression on it keeps the old read; so does a `sift_table`): about 2x faster at 200,000 rows (`ORDER BY ref LIMIT 10` 5.2 ms to 2.4 ms, `LIMIT 100` 6.5 ms to 3.3 ms, `bytea` `LIMIT 10` 6.6 ms to 2.9 ms; with a `WHERE` that leaves a few hundred rows no change). EXPLAIN shows `stored dictionary` in the `Top-N` line.
- Changed: pg_sift: a partitioned table whose partitions cost the scan more to open than the whole normal plan costs keeps the normal plan before any partition is looked at: the opening of the partitions is priced from their number alone (the planner already charges it), and was compared with the normal plan only after the layout, statistics and visibility map of every partition's index had been read. Hash partitions of 62 rows, `GROUP BY country`, planning only: 3,200 takes it too, planning in the time it takes with `sift.enable_agg = off` (43.5 ms against 44.8, 94.9 ms before), and 4,000 the same (48.9 against 52.2, 112.6 before), and the first plan of a new backend takes 168 ms where it took 4,480 ms (it read the facts of every partition); 400 partitions keep the scan and plan as before. `sift.explain_why` names the opening and the normal plan's cost. Test `agg_partitions_many`.
- Changed: `scripts/pg-assert.sh` builds the assertion server with `-DCATCACHE_FORCE_RELEASE`, so the `assert` lane finds a catalog tuple or a slot used after its release (a full `make pg-assert` regress of 18 tests takes about 6 minutes).
- Fixed: pg_sift: an aggregate scan whose lookup table, combination or join key holds a sub-select that PostgreSQL hashes (`NOT IN (SELECT ..)`) in an expression it evaluates for each distinct value kept the slots of the sub-plan in memory that the scan frees when it ends, before the executor frees the slots: a freed pointer in the tuple table, an assertion failure or a crash at the end of the statement. The state of such an expression is built in the query's memory, as the lookup tables were.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the first statement of a session that has not preloaded the library (`session_preload_libraries`, `shared_preload_libraries`) was planned without the planner hook, because the library is loaded while the planner opens the index: a shared CTE was not planned inline and a lateral sub-select was not pulled up, so the aggregates that every later statement runs in the scan ran their ordinary plan. A plain `SELECT` without parameters is now planned again through the hook when it starts, and the plan cache is reset; other first statements keep their plan (docs/USAGE.md).
- Changed: pg_sift: planning a query with a long `IN` list or `= ANY (array)` no longer repeats the work of the planner: the selectivity of the list is the one the planner kept for the condition (it was estimated again for every element, and again for each path), and `EXPLAIN` shows a constant array of more than 1000 elements as its first 8 elements and the count.
- Added: pg_sift: a long `IN` list or `= ANY (array)` over a range of values wider than the 4 million of a byte table (1024 values or more, up to 2^28 wide) is tested by a bit table instead of a hash table, and the zone test of a set travels to the column layer up to twice the units of the scan (it was a fixed 256 values), once per scan instead of once per range of units.
- Changed: pg_sift: the rows a lookup join loads (`IN (SELECT ..)`, `EXISTS`, a join) are read by the heap scan, not through an index, when an equality of the joined table keeps a third of it or more, as an `IN` list of the table already was.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner takes the span of a timestamp key's buckets (`date_trunc`, `::date`, `extract` of a `timestamp`) from the smallest and largest value the index holds, where it needed the histogram of the column in `pg_statistic`: a table not analyzed since it was loaded, or whose histogram is missing its extremes, was planned with PostgreSQL's 200 groups (24 months now). The plans of the 258 captured API shapes are the same. No format change. Test `agg_group_estimates`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner expects the groups of a calendar key on a date column (`date_trunc`, `extract`, `date_part` of a `date`) from the days the column takes, over the days one value of the key covers, where it took PostgreSQL's count of distinct dates (a month key of 700 days was planned as 700 groups, now 24). The scan's estimated rows, and the memory it is charged for the group table, follow. The plans of the 258 captured API shapes are the same. No format change. Test `agg_group_estimates`.
- Added: pg_sift: the `neon` level of `sift.simd` for AArch64 (Linux arm64 and Apple silicon; NEON is part of the architecture, so nothing is detected): NEON kernels of the column filters, selections, zone maps and unpacking, the aggregation operators, the shredder output, the BM25 scoring, the bitmap population count and the unpacking of the chunk codec; the other kernels are the scalar ones, where NEON was not faster. Every level returns the same results bit for bit. `sift.simd` takes the level names of both architectures and the names of the other architecture cap nothing, so one configuration file serves a mixed fleet; `auto` is the best level of the CPU and the build needs no `-march` flag on arm64. SVE is not used.
- Fixed: `make check` and the test tools on macOS arm64 and on other CPUs than x86-64: one checksum helper (`scripts/sha256.sh`; the BSD `sha256sum -c --quiet` of macOS fails without a file operand, which stopped the 20 Perl TAP tests), `-march=x86-64` and the x86 tables of the standalone tools and the fuzz targets only on x86-64, the pinned digests of `kernels_simd` and `aggkern_simd` no longer depend on the compiler (the order of the arguments of a call) or the CPU (the sign of a NaN), `shred_kernels` states its level against the CPU, the harness keeps its Unix sockets under the 103 bytes of macOS, and what a host cannot run is skipped with a message. The CI workflow runs the PostgreSQL 18 tiers on Linux arm64 as well.
- Changed: pg_sift: an inline shared CTE whose scans cost less than the work of the shared plan they replace, but not by the margin, is planned again at the square root of `sift.agg_scale_horizon` (default 100) times the rows and at that many times (with `sift.plan_scale`), and the inline plan is kept if it wins at both: a close call at the size the table has no longer flips to the shared plan at a size in between where the group tables of the scans outgrow `work_mem` and the plan of the shared CTE still grows faster. `sift.agg_scale_horizon = 1` keeps the choice at the size it is.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.workers` failed with `could not open file` in a database that has a `sift_col` or `sift` index on a partitioned table, because the view listed the index of the partitioned table itself, which has no storage; it lists the indexes of the partitions only.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner charges the heap check of a scan on a table whose visibility map is not full (after `CLUSTER`, `VACUUM FULL`, `pg_repack`, or churn before the autovacuum) by the pages it reads, not by the rows: the pages of the kept units that hold a selected row, each at the price of a page read from shared buffers (about 1.3 us measured), from the cache of the operating system (3 us) or from the disk (`seq_page_cost` to `random_page_cost`, by the density of the pages the scan skips), weighted by `shared_buffers`, `effective_cache_size` and the size of the table, and 4 ns a row; it charged one cost unit for each selected row. 258 query shapes of six families on a 195,000-row table of wide rows, clustered and not vacuumed: 108 of them plan on the scan before (254 after, as with a full map), and the natural plans take 113.8 s in all before and 34.9 s after (33.0 s with a full map). A full map prices as before. The refusal text and `sift.explain_why` quote the new charge. Test `agg_visibility_refusal`.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.plan_scale` (default 1, PostgreSQL up to 18): plans as if the tables with a `sift_col` index had that many times the pages and rows, with the dimension tables and the statistics of the columns as they are, so that `EXPLAIN` and `sift.explain_why` show the choice of the planner and the cost of the scan and of the normal plan at a larger size without the data. Only the plan changes; the rows are those of the table.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a row that the aggregate scan reads again from the heap (a value its index cannot store, a recheck) could crash the backend when the index had a toasted column and an invalidation of the index (another session's `VACUUM` or `ANALYZE`) was pending: building the index row detoasts the value, which locks the toast table and reloads the relcache entry, and the loop read the schema and the column arrays from the cached state it had freed. They are read before the first value. Test `cache_discard`. The same code is in 3.2.2; the repro that reaches it is the 3.3 re-read of a `(doc ->> 'k')::float8` path.
- Fixed: pg_sift: planning an index scan of a `sift_col` index (`sift.enable_col_scan`; off by default in 3.3) with two or more conditions on its columns could crash the backend when an invalidation of the index (another session's `VACUUM` or `ANALYZE`) was pending: the cost estimate of the units the zones and the small-set summaries leave kept the index's cached state across the statistics lookups of the conditions, and read it again after the entry had been reloaded. It asks for the state where it uses it. Test `cache_discard` (a server built with assertions reloads the entry at every lock and catalog read). Not in 3.2.x.
- Added: pg_sift: `count(DISTINCT x)` of an integer column whose superblocks hold ranges of `x` that overlap little visits the superblocks in the order of the zone minimum and drops from its sets the values below the minimum of the superblocks to come, so the sets stay within `hash_mem` where the plain scan would spill or sort them; the answer is the same, the sweep is given up for the plain scan when the table of the groups would spill, and `sift.agg_dist_stream = off` restores the plain scan. `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` shows `Distinct Stream Superblocks`.
- Added: pg_sift: the aggregate scan answers `LEFT JOIN LATERAL jsonb_to_recordset(..)` / `jsonb_array_elements(..)` of a list of the scanned row, `ON true` or with conditions of its own: a row none of whose elements passes (no list, an empty one, all failing) is one tuple with NULL element columns, so a per-row group keeps its row (a per-row `avg(x)`, `max(y)` and `count(*)`). A `WHERE` above the join that sees the NULL tuple, or a lateral condition that may raise, keeps the normal plan; `sift.agg_left_lateral` (on) turns it off. Test `agg_left_lateral`, fixtures `j_left_lateral*`, fuzz family `leftlateral`.
- Added: pg_sift: the two-level scan computes `count(col)` over the per-row arms when the column is the row id of the arms (a unique NOT NULL column): it counts the inner groups, as `count(*)` does. A per-row aggregate with a sum of its own and `count(row_id)` above it was refused as `two_aggs`. Test `agg_arm_cells`, fuzzer family `twolevel`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a text group key is read as dictionary codes, which gives an id to every entry of the dictionary of each unit the scan reads, whether a row that passes the filters holds it or not, so a near-unique text key under a filter made a table of ids as large as the rows read for the few groups it returned (it outgrew `work_mem` and spilled over and over, walking the whole table each time) and the planner priced none of it. The scan is now priced for the entries it maps, which cost more as the table of ids outgrows the caches, and for the ids in the group table and the spill passes over them; the normal plan is kept for such a statement, and a key without statistics, or with few values, is priced as it was.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.col_verify(index, true)` on a table with toasted values could crash the backend when an invalidation of the index (another session's `VACUUM` or `ANALYZE`, `ALTER INDEX`) was pending while the check read a toasted value: the first fetch from the toast table locks it and reloads the relcache entry, which frees the index's cached state, and the check read the schema from it afterwards. The check now works on a private copy of that state for the whole call. Test `verify_relcache` (toasted values under `debug_discard_caches` on a server built with assertions). Present in 3.2.2.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a scan with two sets of values on one leaf (an `IN` list or the dimension filter of a table, and the keys of a lookup join or a second list: `k IN (51 values)` and `k IN (SELECT .. LIMIT 5)`) read the units the first set's zones left, narrowed only to the range of both; a unit is now read when its zone holds a value of both sets (a filter over 93 units read 39, as against 9 for the same five values as a list). Test `agg_zone_sets`.
- Changed: `bench/bench cases` keeps its scratch cluster in `/dev/shm/sift-cases` (tmpfs, `CASES_TMP` for another directory) instead of `/var/tmp/sift-cases`: the cluster is disposable and a 200,000-row run no longer writes to the disk.
- Added: three more cases in `bench/cases.tsv` (a short `IN` list over a range a byte table covers, a near-unique text group key under a filter, and `count(DISTINCT)` by group at `work_mem = 1MB`, where the group table spills and pg_sift is slower than PostgreSQL), and a `-- set: a = 1; b = 2` line in a case file for the settings of every lane.
- Added: six cases in `bench/cases.tsv` on a second shared dataset, `keys` (`bench/sql/keys/gen.sql`): `IN` lists of 50,000 and 500,000 values over a 50-million-wide key and over a narrow one, and the `IN (SELECT ..)`, `EXISTS` and join forms of a 500,000-key lookup. A case SQL file takes `{ids:TABLE:N}` for the comma-separated ids of the rows of a table with that `n`.
- Added: `bench/bench cases --discover` (`bench/steps/cases_discover.py`): measures the statements of a corpus (`test/plan/shapes.sql`, the query families of the differential fuzzer, or results measured elsewhere) on PostgreSQL and on pg_sift and proposes, for a person to accept, the shapes the scan answers at least twice as quickly that no case of `bench/cases.tsv` covers, ranked by weight times gain; a different answer is listed first and fails the run.
- Changed: `CREATE INDEX`, `REINDEX` and `sift.col_refresh` of a `sift_col` index tell, as they did for a cube dim over its cap or a set summary that is a bloom filter, the options that wrote nothing in a share of the units: a `key_columns` leaf whose keys are one value or fill their range, or whose key list or bitmap would take over 3% of the unit, a `rollup_dims` rollup with more cells than a unit may keep, and the date columns that `calendar_columns` (default `all`) leaves out because the cube holds at most 16 dimensions.
- Changed: pg_sift: a shared CTE is planned inline when the scans cost 1.15 times less than the work of the shared plan they replace (it was 1.6), and when an ordinary plan still reads the table the inline plan may total up to 1.25 times the shared one (it had to total less than the shared plan by more than the scans cost). With the visibility map empty, 27 statements of a 195,000-row slice (14 of one SQL form, 13 of the other) take the scans instead of the shared CTE.
- Added: pg_sift: a semi or anti join (`IN (SELECT ..)`, `EXISTS`, `NOT EXISTS`) whose right side is a tree of joined tables (an inner join of small tables, or `IN (SELECT coalesce(a.k, b.k) FROM a LEFT JOIN b ..)` behind a table of its own, the shape PostgreSQL makes of nested sub-selects) is no longer refused as `from_shape`: the plan PostgreSQL made of the tree runs once when the scan starts and gives the key set, as the plan of a sub-select does (NULL and duplicate keys, an empty set and the anti join as in the normal plan; a tree that reads the query's other tables, or whose keys are an expression over two of its tables straight under the scan, keeps the normal plan). Test `agg_semi_tree`, fixtures `j_semi_tree*`, coverage row J35.

- Added: eleven more cases in `bench/cases.tsv` (a text sort key, sorted runs with and without a btree, the zones of the keys after the first, a long offset, five sort keys, the histogram of `count(DISTINCT)`, a numeric group key, `rollup_dims`, a dictionary equality and a prefix), `default+col` in the `index` field for a column the default index lacks, `{cols}` in a `setup` section, and the block names the variants (`runs_off`, `zone_off`, `forced`), the cases where the planner kept the normal plan ("same plan", no ratio) and the settings that bring no gain.
- Added: two cases in `bench/cases.tsv` for the scan of a partitioned table (hash partitions: 400, where the scan is kept, and 3,200, where the planner keeps the normal plan), and the block shows the time of pg_sift with the same plan as PostgreSQL's as a ratio when it is outside the 5% tie, instead of hiding it in "same plan".
- Added: ten more cases in `bench/cases.tsv` (a numeric sum, the whole-table total, a top-N through sorted runs with a filter, and on a second dataset, the events of the plan-choice gate (`test/plan/gen.sql`, built once per run in the schema `c_events`, `dataset` = `events` in the registry): a top-N through a join that only filters, a series joined on `jsonb_build_array`, the first-row lookups of a `LIMIT 1` child, a shared common table expression, a filter that keeps a few rows of every unit, and the elements of a list), and the block lists the losses first, then the ties and the plans kept, then the wins.
- The stage `scale` of `bench/bench refresh --stages scale --budget 0` measures it (opt-in, never in the default pass: 20 million orders at 100x of 200,000).
- Added: five more cases in `bench/cases.tsv` (six sort keys, 5,000 partitions of six rows, the dirty-document share at 3% and 50%, and three `UNION ALL` arms over the elements of a list above a spine, the shape the scan does not take yet), `bench/bench cases --plan` (the points, builds, disk and time of a run, nothing started; `bench/bench refresh --stages scale --dry-run` prints it), and the run drops the schema of each orders case after its measure so that a scale run does not keep a copy of the table per case. The events dataset of the plan gate is generated for its own schema, and the plan of the sift lane is taken after its first statement (a backend that has not loaded the library plans its first statement without the scan; the note says so).
- Fixed: `ALTER INDEX ... SET` of an option that names columns (`calendar_columns`, `key_columns`, `order_columns`, `rollup_dims`, `return_columns`) was not checked against the index in the first statement of a session that had not used sift yet, so a bad name, a column of the wrong type or a calendar list on an index with `paths` was stored and then failed every query, insert and `VACUUM` of the table until `RESET`. The check, and the analyzer warning, now run from an object access hook that PostgreSQL reads when the catalog row is updated, in every session.
- Added: pg_sift: `point_columns`: every segment written afterwards lists the rows of those columns by value (a table with a slot for each distinct key, a 32-bit fingerprint over the key's only row or the offset of the list of its rows, at the end of the dictionary section), and the access method finds the rows of an equality, an `IN` list or a short range with a probe and has the heap check them again, where the units that can hold the value are each read and tested. 200,000 rows, `SELECT id FROM t WHERE rnd = X` by a random integer, warm, median of runs 11 to 20: 0.119 ms without the option, 0.040 with `key_columns`, 0.020 with `point_columns`, 0.006 for a b-tree (an `IN` list of 5 keys 0.193, 0.059, 0.028, 0.009; the first query of a new backend 0.287, 0.142, 0.092, 0.037); a text equality once its dictionary is cached 0.032, 0.023, 0.016, 0.006. The price is size, 8 bytes a slot and 4 bytes a row of a key that repeats, so a key in very many rows never takes the leaf's table away: 200,000 rows, a directory on one leaf adds 4.21 MB for a unique key, 0.97 MB for 22 rows a key, 0.81 MB for 333 rows a key and 0.80 MB for 4,000 (a b-tree on the integer: 4.5, 1.6, 1.5, 1.4 MB). Keys with hundreds of rows gain little (333 rows a key, rows returned: 0.72 to 0.60 ms, b-tree 0.44) because the heap fetch of the rows is the cost; the access method scan runs only with `sift.enable_col_scan` on. `sift.col_verify` checks every row against its directory. An index without the option, or at `sift.col_format_minor` below 14, is read as before; `sift.upgrade_check` lists an index with the option and older segments as a gain from `REINDEX`. Tests `col_point_dir`.
- Added: pg_sift: `point_tid_columns`: the point directory of those columns lists the heap addresses of the rows instead of their numbers in the segment, so an equality or a short `IN` list of the access method returns the addresses without reading a unit, and the heap checks them again. A unit with a dead row is read instead (VACUUM frees an address to be used again), and so is every unit for an index-only scan, which wants the values. 1,000,000 rows, `SELECT * FROM t WHERE id = X`: 0.013 ms, and 0.011 to 0.013 ms at 26,700 and 100,000 rows (`point_columns`: 0.022, 0.015, 0.015; a btree 0.006), an `IN` list of 10 keys 0.022 ms (0.052; btree 0.013), a uuid 0.042 ms (0.078; btree 0.007). The size is that of `point_columns` within 3% (91.4 MB against 93.7 MB for five columns, four of them unique; a key of many rows takes 1 to 3 bytes a row, not 4), the build 65% longer, a promotion of the write buffer 5% longer and the inserts unchanged. The default of `sift.col_format_minor` stays 15 and only a segment with the option is minor 16; an index written at 14 or less lists rows, and an index written before the option is read as before until a `REINDEX` (`sift.upgrade_commands()` says so).
- Changed: pg_sift: a `sift_table` row fetch (a SELECT or UPDATE of a few rows, the heap fetch of an index scan) reads the text of a row from a segment whose dictionary is stored in blocks to be read in place (column format minor 14) one string at a time, where it loaded the whole dictionary of the segment first: the first statement of a new backend on a table of 200,000 rows with unique 104-byte text takes 1.4 ms, where it took 28.6 ms (median of 3 interleaved runs, 10 segments of 20,000 rows). After about a sixteenth of the dictionary has been read that way the dictionary is loaded and cached as before, so a scan that reads many strings is not slower.
- Added: pg_sift: a page of rows with the total of its set, `SELECT cols, count(*) OVER () FROM t WHERE .. ORDER BY k LIMIT n OFFSET m`, is a top-N of the column scan (`sift_col` and `sift_table`): the window functions that are aggregates over the whole set (`count`, `sum`, `avg`, `min`, `max` and the other aggregates the scan computes, with `FILTER`; no `PARTITION BY`, no `ORDER BY`, a frame of all the rows) are computed from every row that passes in the same pass as the heap of the n best, and every row of the page carries them (no unit is left out for the zone of the sort key, and the scan is serial). 200,000 rows, a filter that keeps 20,233 of them, `count(*) OVER (), sum(amount) OVER ()`, the planner's own choice, normal plan against the scan: `LIMIT 10` 47.1 ms against 2.7, `LIMIT 100` 53.3 against 3.9, `LIMIT 1000` 50.1 against 5.2; a filter that keeps a few hundred rows keeps the normal plan, which is priced cheaper. A window with `PARTITION BY`, `ORDER BY` or a frame that cuts the set, and a window function that is not an aggregate (`row_number`, `rank`, `first_value`), keep the normal plan under `topn_shape`, with the reason. Test: `test/sql/agg_topn_window.sql`.
- Added: pg_sift: `ORDER BY <expression of the columns> LIMIT n` is a top-N of the column scan of a `sift_col` or `sift_table` index, with any direction and NULLs clause, alone or among other keys. Until now any sort expression other than a column, `coalesce(column, constant)`, `lower(column)` and `upper(column)` kept the plan's sort of every matching row and lost the sift filtering with it. The scan runs PostgreSQL's own expression evaluator for every row that passes the `WHERE`, as the plan's sort does, so the operators, casts, functions, overflow, division by zero, NULLs, NaN and the collation of strings are the backend's, a query that raises raises the same SQLSTATE in both plans, and a `CASE` branch or a later `coalesce` argument that is not taken is not evaluated. The expressions are arithmetic (`a + 1`, `1000 - a`, `-a`, `a * b`, `total / 3`, `a % 7`), casts (`a::bigint`, `a::text`), `CASE` (searched or simple), `coalesce` of several columns, `NULLIF`, `GREATEST`/`LEAST`, boolean expressions (`AND`, `OR`, `NOT`, `IS NULL`, `IS TRUE`, `IS DISTINCT FROM`), `IN (..)` of constants, comparisons, and any immutable function or operator: concatenations (`lower(s || v)`), functions of functions (`upper(lower(s))`), `left`, `substr`, `initcap`, `md5`, `abs`, `mod`, `round`, `sqrt`, `date_trunc`, `extract`, `make_date`, `length(b)` of a bytea, `host(ip)` of an inet, a plpgsql function declared `IMMUTABLE`; the result is a number, a date, a time, a string, a uuid, a bytea or an inet. No zone bounds such a key, so the scan reads every unit that holds matching rows, and the planner prices the evaluation per row (the expression's cost, a charge per leaf, per numeric and per string) so that it keeps the plan's sort where the scan would be slower. A parameter of the statement (`ORDER BY a + $1` in a generic plan) and a function that is not `PARALLEL SAFE` keep the scan in the leader. A volatile or stable expression (`random()`, `now()`, `concat()`, `timestamptz + interval`, the text of a date), a sub-select, an interval, array or jsonb result and a numeric column of 19 or more digits keep the normal plan under `topn_shape` with the reason.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a common table expression that several aggregates read, and that selects from another CTE of the same `WITH` instead of from the indexed table, kept PostgreSQL's materialization: the check for a shared CTE over a column index looked at the tables of its own FROM list only. It follows the CTEs it reads now (a chain of up to four), so the inline plan is weighed against the shared one as for a CTE that reads the table (2 arms over 30,000 rows: the arms are scans, the same rows as the normal plan; an explicit `MATERIALIZED` still keeps the copy).
- Changed: pg_sift: `sift.table_auto_defrag_dead_pct` is 5 (was 20): the background pass of a `sift_table` starts when 5% of its rows are tombstones, not 20%, so a table that took 16% churn (the update, update and delete phases of the store benchmark) is cleaned before the next read instead of waiting for a `VACUUM`. 200,000 rows, the read after the churn 35.8 ms -> 7.6 ms (0.5 ms fresh); four clients of an update, insert and delete mix, 160,000 transactions, medians of 3, pass on: 3,206 -> 3,452 transactions a second, WAL 164 -> 157 MB, range read p99 45 -> 38 ms, all-client stalls of 20 ms or more 295 -> 187 (pass off: 2,217, 95 MB, 60 ms, 666).
- Changed: pg_sift: a backend reads the tombstone log of a `sift_table` at its first statement 2.2 times faster: a stable radix sort on the key replaces `qsort` with a comparison call (the batch is in log order, so it is the sort on key and position), the first batch goes into the main array and not through the recent one and a second copy, and the batch is allocated once from the count in the log header. A table with 32,400 tombstones (16% of 200,000 rows after the update, update and delete phases of the store benchmark): the sync takes 5.1 ms (0.96 read, 3.67 sort and merge) and then 2.3 ms (0.8, 1.5), measured inside the backend on a loaded host; it was 88% of the 5.8 ms a new session needed for a one-row `UPDATE`. The background pass asks the log header for the number of entries, and reads and sorts the log only when that number could cross `sift.table_auto_defrag_dead_pct`: a backend that ticks without reading the table no longer syncs it.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.col_auto_merge` (off, 3.2) also vacuums the table, [docs/USAGE.md](docs/USAGE.md): the merge only helps after a `VACUUM`, so one setting keeps the table answerable. After churn spread over a table every heap page is not all-visible, so a scan of a `sift_col` index checks its rows in the heap one by one and answers none of its units from their pre-aggregates, until a `VACUUM` that autovacuum starts after 20% of the rows changed. A backend that inserts a row version, or whose scan checked 1,000 rows or more, now looks at the statistics of the table once per `sift.maintenance_naptime` and starts a worker when 0.1% of the rows changed (100,000 rows at most, 8.5 ms of rows a query checks in the heap, where autovacuum waits for 20%: 20 million of 100 million); it vacuums as autovacuum does, at most once per five naptimes (ten times the average `VACUUM` of the table on PostgreSQL 18), stays until the table is clean and leaves a table with `autovacuum_enabled = false` alone. 200,000 rows, the update, update and delete phases of the store benchmark, the interval scaled to one second, medians of 3: the monthly revenue read after the churn 18.7 ms -> 1.2 ms (0.6 ms with `sift.col_auto_merge`; 0.2 ms fresh), the second query 11.3 -> 1.9 ms; the rows a second of the three phases are the same, and the WAL of the churn rises from 11.7 to 16-17 MB by what the `VACUUM` after it wrote (16.8 MB in all before).
- Changed: pg_sift: the automatic rewrite of `sift.col_auto_merge` waits while a snapshot holds back the blocks the last rewrite freed (half the index or more): it would answer no more units and leave as much again behind. Under a snapshot held through 16 rounds of churn the index no longer outgrows the one without the setting.
- Changed: pg_sift: the calendar cells of the dates come first in the default cube of a `sift_col` index with more than 16 columns that can be dimensions: the plain columns filled its 16 dimensions in their order and left the cells out, so `GROUP BY date_trunc('month', d)` scanned every unit (21 columns, 60,000 rows: the month of a date, the month of another, a window of years and the day 60,000, 60,000, 29,280 and 60,000 rows scanned before, 0 after). The default dims give way, the dates' own histograms first and then the last columns, and a NOTICE names the columns left out; nothing changes when everything fits, or for a `cube(...)` written by the user. An index of more than 16 columns takes the new choice at its next `REINDEX`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a `sift_col` index with a `jsonb` column and no `paths` option takes the paths from a sample of the documents at `CREATE INDEX` (`sift.suggest_paths`, 2,000 documents from 16 evenly spaced stretches of blocks): the spec is written to the option as `auto: spec`, so a `REINDEX` and the workers of the build keep the same schema, and a NOTICE names it. It used to be an error ("the jsonb column needs paths"), and it still is for a table where no key qualifies; paths written by hand are untouched. `col_paths_auto`: the index verifies, the grouped and filtered queries on it equal the plain plan's.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `ALTER INDEX ... SET (paths = 'auto')` and `RESET (paths)` on a `sift_col` index with a `jsonb` column were refused ("needs paths"), so the paths chosen at `CREATE INDEX` could not be read again from the documents; the next `REINDEX` decides again now (`col_paths_auto`).
- Added: pg_sift: `calendar_columns` writes the calendar cells of the dates of an index with `jsonb` paths (3.3 refused them; not the default there: +9.3% to +12.1% of the main index of an application), and a unit whose date has too many days for a histogram answers a comparison, an AND or an OR of comparisons on it from the cells, each cell decided by its span, [docs/COL-PREAGG.md](docs/COL-PREAGG.md) (`agg_cal_paths`).
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.suggest_paths(table, column, sample)`, [docs/REFERENCE.md](docs/REFERENCE.md): the `paths` option that a `jsonb` column asks for, read from 2,000 of its documents (16 stretches of blocks evenly spaced over the table, so the same table gives the same answer; advice only, nothing is changed): a typed path for each key that is a scalar of one type in most of the documents that have it (`int4`, `int8` beyond 32 bits, `float8` for numbers with fractions, `text`, `bool`, `astext` for a mix), a wildcard for a map of more than 32 keys of one type, a group for an array of objects; rare keys and keys without a single shape are left out. `col_suggest_paths`: the spec is accepted by the option, the index built with it verifies and the grouped, filtered and unnested queries on it are pushed and equal the plain plan's.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.index_advice(index)`, [docs/REFERENCE.md](docs/REFERENCE.md): the profile of the columns of a `sift_col` index and what its options could still use, advice only (nothing is changed): per column its kind, what the index holds of it and, over 32 evenly spaced units, the share that satisfy the rule of a dimension (at most 256 distinct values: a histogram of the cube); per pair of dimensions the share of units whose combinations fit a rollup of the rows, with `rollup_dims` advised when that is 90% of the units for within 3% more index. The same rows after a `REINDEX`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the automatic rewrite of `sift.col_auto_merge` no longer skips an index over `sift.vacuum_rewrite_limit` (128 MB, about 6 million rows, above which the merge did nothing): it rewrites it by partitions of that size, one step each with the lock let go between them (`sift.col_auto_merge_partition_kb` sets another size). 1,000,000 rows with the limit at 8 MB, a rewrite every round: the units answerable from their pre-aggregates 42% -> 100% (uniform churn, grouped sum 7.4 -> 0.10 ms) and 71% -> 100% (scraper pattern, 8.4 -> 0.16 ms), for the WAL of the whole-index rewrite.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.col_maintenance_status(index)`, [docs/REFERENCE.md](docs/REFERENCE.md): where the chain that keeps a `sift_col` index answerable stands (the table, its last `VACUUM`, the rows changed since and the number that starts the next, why the rewrite is due, the last request of a worker) and `blocked_by`, what keeps a worker from running: the setting or `sift.async_maintenance` off, `autovacuum_enabled = false`, a standby, a snapshot holding back the last rewrite.
- Added: pg_sift: `order_columns` (`sift_col` reloption, default none; `int2`, `int4`, `int8`, `numeric`, `date`, `timestamp`, `timestamptz`, `float4` and `float8` columns) and `sift.topn_runs` (default on): every unit written afterwards keeps the ordinals of its rows in the order of the column (2 bytes a row for a column that does not ascend with the table, 16 bytes a unit for one that does: +50% on an index of ten columns, eight of them unordered), and `ORDER BY col LIMIT n` reads the first rows of each unit and not all of them (EXPLAIN shows `Units Read By Sorted Run`). 1,000,000 rows (230 units), `ORDER BY k LIMIT n` on a random `int4`: n = 10, 0.25 to 0.34 ms before, 0.17 to 0.22 after; n = 100, 1.7 to 1.9 before, 0.9 to 1.3 after; n = 1,000, 6.5 to 8.9 before, 1.9 to 4.9 after (a btree needs 0.35); n = 10,000, 29 to 32 before, 23 to 27 after. A scan with a `WHERE` that removes rows, and the rows of a write buffer, are read as before; the rows and their ties are the same as the plain plan's (240 shapes checked three ways). Column format minor 13 (11 is the write buffer of a `sift_table` with a wide row, 12 the superunits), written only for an index with the option under `sift.col_format_minor = 13` (the default; 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10 and 12 keep the formats their libraries read): an index without the option writes the bytes it wrote before. A 3.2 library refuses a minor 13 segment with the REINDEX hint. `sift.upgrade_commands()` lists an index with the option and older units as a gain from `REINDEX`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a top-N scan with `order_columns` reads the sorted run of a unit also when a `WHERE` leaves few of its rows (it read them one by one before) and walks a start such as `k > $last` testing the row before it reads its key. 1,000,000 rows, `ORDER BY k LIMIT n`, before and after, median of 4 rounds of 15: a filter of 10% of the rows 1.50 to 1.32 ms at n = 100 and 2.53 to 2.16 ms at n = 1,000; `k > 500000` 5.18 to 1.96 ms at n = 100 and 5.71 to 2.54 ms at n = 1,000; no filter, 50% and a late start unchanged, 1% of the rows 1.44 to 1.58 ms at n = 1,000. No format change; the rows and their ties are the plain plan's (160 more shapes of `col_sorted_run`).
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the bitmap chunks of a `count(DISTINCT)` per group (a chunk of 4096 values is allocated and zeroed when a value of the group first falls in it, about 0.7 microseconds), while they fit the memory of the set. A query with thousands of groups and a column of a few thousand values (200,000 rows, 4,907 groups, `LIMIT 100`: sift 9.4 ms, the btree plan 5.2 ms) took the scan, for the `count(DISTINCT)` was priced like `count(*)`; it keeps the normal plan now, and a query with a hundred groups, or a column whose sets leave the bitmap for a hash set, is priced as before.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner charges the heap check of the rows on pages that are not all-visible for the pages of the units the zones leave, not for all the pages of the table: after `CLUSTER`, `VACUUM FULL` or `pg_repack` (an empty visibility map) the scan of a clustered table is no longer priced as if it read the whole heap. Tables whose map is full price the same as before. On a 195,000-row slice clustered by its key and not yet vacuumed, 87 of the 301 corpus queries plan on the scan (37 before; 238 after the `VACUUM`), and the 50 gained run in 7.8 s against 19.1 s for the normal plan. The heap check itself costs more per page than the model charges (15 to 20 us on a narrow-row table), so a narrow table with a btree on the clustered key can still choose a slower scan than its index plan until a VACUUM.
- Added: pg_sift: a `cost` refusal (`sift.explain_why`, the notice of `sift.explain_refusals`) that the heap check of the pages that are not all-visible decided says how many pages and what they add to the scan, and to run `VACUUM (ANALYZE)`: a rewrite by `CLUSTER`, `VACUUM FULL` or `pg_repack` leaves the visibility map empty, and the normal plan wins until a VACUUM. No plan changes.
- Added: pg_sift: a `cte_cost` refusal that the heap check of the pages that are not all-visible decided (the inlined scans would have won without it, and the shared plan does not pay it too) says how many pages and what they add to the scans, and to run `VACUUM (ANALYZE)`, as the `cost` refusal does. No plan changes.
- Fixed: pg_sift: docs: `CLUSTER`, `VACUUM FULL` and `pg_repack` leave the visibility map of the table empty; run `VACUUM (ANALYZE)` after them, or the scan of a `sift_col` index is priced for a heap check of every row and the normal plan wins (200,000 rows, a scan of 133,660 rows: 320 ms against 82 ms when forced; a table of wide rows: 180 ms against 7.7 ms for the scan, the normal plan 160 ms). The text said no `ANALYZE` was needed.
