- Added: pg_sift: `ORDER BY coalesce(<int2, int4, int8, date, timestamp or timestamptz column>, <constant of that type>)`, `lower(<text or varchar column>)` and `upper(<text or varchar column>)` `LIMIT n` is a top-N of the column scan, with any direction and NULLs clause and in the sort of every row that passes: none of them can raise, so the scan that evaluates the key for every row that passes returns the rows the normal plan does, in its order (a NULL is the constant, with its zone widened to it so units are skipped as for the column; the string goes through PostgreSQL's own `lower()` / `upper()` under the call's collation once per distinct entry of a unit). 200,000 rows, `LIMIT 10`, the planner's own choice, normal plan against the scan: `coalesce(int, 0) DESC` 66.7 ms against 0.6, `coalesce(id, 0)` 69.1 against 0.6, `lower(text)` 67.5 against 34.2, `upper(text)` DESC 73.0 against 35.9, `lower(text)` with a filter 32.0 against 11.2; a locale collation (`lower(name COLLATE "en_US.utf8")`) is priced above the normal plan and stays with it. Arithmetic, casts, `CASE`, `coalesce` of two columns and other functions keep the normal plan under `topn_shape` (they can raise for a row far from the top, which only the normal plan reaches). A locale collation is now told from `C` by the collation's own flag, so a database created with `C` prices a text key at the byte order. Tests: `test/sql/col_topn_expr.sql`, the `topn_xf_*` coldiff fixtures, the `topn` family of the differential fuzzer.
- Changed: pg_sift: a `WHERE` over the columns of the nullable side of a preserved join (`calendar LEFT JOIN rows`, the filter PostgreSQL pushes into a grouped sub-select from the caller's `WHERE category = 'x'`) keeps the aggregate scan when the row of NULL columns passes it: the scan tests it on the matched tuples, so a calendar day whose matches it drops is no row and an empty day is. It was refused as `outer_join`, and a client had to fence the sub-select with `OFFSET 0`. A condition the NULL row fails keeps the normal plan. The `from_shape` refusal names the join or table, and the other orientation of a preserved join replaces the refusal of the first. Test `agg_preserved_where`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the two-level scan of arms joined to a spine takes `GROUPING SETS`, `ROLLUP` and `CUBE` (it kept the normal plan): each cell is added to the group of every spine row its keys equal in every set, and a `LEFT JOIN` spine row no cell matched is a group of NULL arm columns in every set. The `spine_shape`, `two_keys` and `two_aggs` refusals say which condition, key or aggregate. Test `agg_two_level_spine_sets`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the choice between a shared common table expression and the inline plan compares the scans with the plans of the CTEs and the aggregates that read them (a margin of 1.6), not the totals of the two plans: those differ by the plan above the scans, which each plan prices from the rows it expects of the arms (the shared CTE one row and 100 per unnest, the scans a group count, a join on an expression key the default selectivity). Three `UNION ALL` arms over the elements of a jsonb array, counted `DISTINCT` above a spine, 100,000 rows: 2.8 s on the shared CTE, 0.9 s in the scan, which the totals refused (PostgreSQL 18, 2026-10-22, loaded host). When a reader of the CTE stays an ordinary aggregate the totals still decide, and an inline plan priced 20 times the shared one is refused. No format change. Tests `agg_cte_inline_rows` (100,000 rows now), `agg_plan_invalidation`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a sum over a `numeric` column without a precision was priced a tenth of the parallel sequential scan and ran four times slower in the scan (200,000 rows in 19 units: 137 ms against 34 ms): each unit builds a dictionary whose every new entry is spelled as text and read back by `numeric_in` (0.75 us), and the estimate charged a share of the rows equal to the column's distinct fraction. The scan is now priced for the entries each unit builds (300 operators each for such a numeric); a column of few values keeps the scan (10 ms against 22 ms). The same price made a clustered heap with a btree choose the scan for a key list. No format change. Test `agg_wide_numeric_cost`.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.cluster_advice(rel, min_rows)` recommends the physical order (`CLUSTER`, `pg_repack --order-by`) that lets the scans of a `sift_col` index skip the most units, by a virtual sort of the zones: the key, the units read now and after, the rows saved, the churn, a verdict with the reason (small table, already ordered, a competing ordered leaf, rows changing too fast) and the `pg_repack` call with the `VACUUM (ANALYZE)` that follows a rewrite; the argument `key` asks about an order of your own (`'a, b'`). It is optional at two levels, the setting `sift.cluster_advice` (on) and the `sift_col` index option `cluster_advice` (on); neither is part of the format.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.cluster_advice(rel, min_rows)` recommends the physical order (`CLUSTER`, `pg_repack --order-by`) that lets the scans of a `sift_col` index skip the most units, by a virtual sort of the zones: the key, the units read now and after, the rows saved, the churn, a verdict with the reason (small table, already ordered, a competing ordered leaf, rows changing too fast) and the `pg_repack` call. It is optional at two levels, the setting `sift.cluster_advice` (on) and the `sift_col` index option `cluster_advice` (on); neither is part of the format.
- Changed: pg_sift: the arms of a two-level scan may differ in their `WHERE`: a condition that every arm has is the scan's, one that only some arms have is a filter of those arms (a `NOT NULL` test of a lookup in two arms and none in a third), and a constant in the `GROUP BY` of the arms splits no group. The three-arm shape of an aggregate over a `LEFT JOIN LATERAL .. LIMIT 1` lookup, which the normal plan ran, is one scan of the index. The `two_arms` and `two_keys` refusals name the arm and the condition.
- Added: pg_sift: `ORDER BY` a text, varchar, uuid, bytea or inet column `LIMIT n` is a top-N of the column scan. The entries of the unit's dictionary are the keys, compared by the sort operator's own comparison (`varstr_cmp` under the query's collation, `uuid_cmp`, `byteacmp`, the inet comparison), so any deterministic collation, `C` or a locale of the C library or ICU, returns the rows of the normal plan; a nondeterministic collation, `char(n)`, `name`, `cidr` and `text_pattern_ops` keep the normal plan (`topn_shape`, with the reason). The keys have no zones, so every unit that holds matching rows is read; the heap keeps its own copy of the strings (a heap of keys wider than `work_mem`, or more rows than it holds, sorts every row in a tuplesort under the same operator and collation), and workers send their strings to the leader. The table source reads a wide leaf (uuid, bytea, inet) of a sort key after the first. 200,000 rows, `LIMIT 10`, serial, the planner's choice, median of 3 (PostgreSQL 18.6, a host at load 50), normal plan against the scan: text 52 against 17 ms, text with a filter 46 against 14, text first key of 4 values 61 against 8, `en_US.utf8` 79 against 25, uuid 59 against 24, inet DESC 71 against 30, bytea 66 against 29, integer then text 71 against 13. Tests `col_topn_text`, `topn_key_text*`, `topn_key_uuid*`, `topn_key_bytea*`, `topn_key_inet*`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `pg_repack` of a `sift_table` destroyed the table (it swaps in a heap copy of the rows, and the next read failed with "invalid page layout"): the new event trigger `sift_table_repack_guard` refuses the `repack.repack_trigger` of `pg_repack` on a `sift_table` before anything is copied, with a hint to use `CLUSTER` or `VACUUM FULL`; `pg_repack` of a heap table with `sift` and `sift_col` indexes, and `--only-indexes` of a `sift_table`, are unchanged.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a dictionary of a string leaf is kept by the backend up to half of `dict_cache_kb` (64 MiB), where one over a quarter was parsed again by every statement that read a row of its segment: the dictionary of a free text leaf of 150,000 rows is 18 MB, and four clients of an update, insert and delete mix spent most of their time inflating it (200,000 rows, `sift_table`, 30 s: 1,073 transactions a second, 5,092 after).
- Changed: pg_sift: `sift.table_auto_defrag` is on by default (3.2: off; it needs `sift.async_maintenance`, on): a `sift_table` that deletes, updates or reads past `sift.table_auto_defrag_dead_pct` dead rows, or holds `sift.table_auto_defrag_free_pct` free blocks, is cleaned by a background pass, without `VACUUM`. Four clients of an update, insert and delete mix, 30 s, medians of 3, pass on against off: 25,000 rows 16,672 against 3,427 transactions a second, range read p99 3.4 against 10.7 ms; 200,000 rows 7,536 against 5,092, read p99 16.8 against 34.2 ms, file 10.7 against 18.1 MB; 1,000,000 rows no pass starts in the 30 s and the rate is the same. The pass adds WAL on that mix (200,000 rows, same transactions: about 60% more than without it, 237 MB against 142-154 MB). A table that only appends starts no pass (200,000 inserts a second, 25,000 rows, 3 rounds: 0.352 kB of WAL per transaction with the pass on, 0.353 off, no worker, 188,000-231,000 transactions a second against 169,000-220,000; the first trigger gave 0.41 kB and 8 to 9 workers in 30 s): the free blocks of a table that is being written are its promoted write buffers, about to be taken again, so the space is asked for only when no write buffer was opened since the backend last looked. A worker whose first pass found nothing leaves at once. `sift.table_auto_defrag = off` (server or database) restores the 3.2 behaviour; the test clusters of `scripts/pgtest.sh` run with it off, as without autovacuum.
- Compatibility: pg_sift: `sift.col_format_minor` defaults to 15 in 3.3 (3.2: 6). A database written by the 3.3 library with its defaults can no longer be read by a 3.2 library: a new `sift_col` index carries `cube_minmax` and `superunit` (minors 7 and 12) and the calendar cells of its date and timestamp columns (8), a string dictionary of 1,024 strings or more of a column named in `key_columns`, `point_columns` or `return_columns` (or of any column with `dict_lookup = on`) is stored for a lookup (14), a `sift_table` with a date or timestamp column holds calendar cells (8), and `rollup_dims` and `order_columns` add 9 and 13, `key_columns` 10 and 15, and `point_tid_columns` 16 (only for an index with the option). To go back, run the sequence of docs/UPGRADING.md section 4 (`sift.col_format_minor = 6`, REINDEX the indexes, VACUUM FULL the tables and DISABLE the two event triggers that `upgrade-check.sh --to 3.2` lists); to stay readable from the start, create the index with `cube_minmax = false, superunit = 0, calendar_columns = 'off'`, or set `sift.col_format_minor = 6` before it is written. `ALTER EXTENSION pg_sift UPDATE` warns of it, and `sift.upgrade_check('3.2')` lists what a rollback cannot read. `test/t/compat_matrix.sh` runs the refusals of 3.0.2, 3.1.0 and 3.2.0 for every minor and the downgrade to 3.2.0 end to end.
- Changed: pg_sift: `cube_minmax` and `superunit` are on by default for a `sift_col` index (`cube_minmax = true`, `superunit = 16`; `WITH (cube_minmax = false, superunit = 0)` keeps the 3.2 bytes). 200,000 rows of 13 columns, 53 units, minimum of 15 runs: `GROUP BY` with `min`/`max` of an integer 5.5 -> 0.34 ms (0.058 ms with the groups), a whole-table `count`/`sum` 0.26-0.37 -> 0.05-0.08 ms, index +1.5% (+0.17% and +1.0%), insert, promotion, merge and VACUUM CPU inside the noise; a range query on a table of fewer than 16 units costs about 40 microseconds more. A new index is column format 12, which a 3.2 library refuses (`SET sift.col_format_minor = 6` before it is created, or the two options off); an index built before keeps its units and `sift.upgrade_commands()` lists the REINDEX that gives them the sections. See docs/UPGRADING.md "Defaults changed in 3.3".
- Changed: pg_sift: `calendar_columns` of a `sift_col` index defaults to every date, timestamp and timestamptz column (`'off'` keeps the 3.2 bytes; not for an index with JSON paths, `preagg = 'off'` or a `cube(...)` given dim by dim), as a `sift_table` has always had them: 200,000 rows with a `date` and a `timestamptz`, `GROUP BY date_trunc('month', d)` 2.4 -> 0.54 ms, the UTC month of the `timestamptz` 2.6 -> 0.70 ms, the sidecar +1.8% of the index. A superunit no longer merges the calendar cells of its units (+1.2% of the index and no query faster). Column format 8.
- Changed: pg_sift: an index scan of `sift_col` that selects a few rows of a unit (a point lookup, an `IN` of a few values) decodes the TIDs of those rows only (the pages that hold them), not of all the 4,352 rows of the unit: a random-int equality on 1,000,000 rows takes 0.62 ms (was 1.10) and 0.21 ms with `key_columns` (was 0.39), the instructions of the scan half. No format change.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a delete or an update of a `sift_table` no longer waits for the background pass: the lock a writer takes to append a tombstone and the one the pass holds for its whole length were the same page lock (stalls of up to a quarter of a second at a 10% threshold under sustained churn).
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.table_auto_defrag_max_active` (0, never waits) and `sift.table_auto_defrag_wal_rate` (kB of WAL a second, 0 for no limit): the background pass of a `sift_table` waits for a quieter moment, between its merges as well, and keeps to a WAL rate; `sift.col_auto_merge_max_active` shares the wait. The pass is asked for only when tombstones older than every snapshot reach the threshold, where a long transaction made it read the table to remove nothing, and it merges the small groups next to the one it rewrites (`sift.table_auto_defrag_coalesce`).
- Changed: pg_sift: the table `VACUUM` merges until it finds nothing left to merge, where it stopped after 16 merges; a background pass still stops at `sift.table_auto_defrag_budget`.
- Added: pg_sift: `key_columns` takes uuid columns (it refused them: "not a plain integer or text column"): a unit keeps the set of the uuid's dictionary codes like a text column's, in the same minor 10 section (no format change; a library that knows minor 10 reads it). 30,000 rows in 24 units, a point lookup of a uuid scattered over the table reads 5 units, not 21.
- Changed: pg_sift: an equality or a `LIKE 'abc%'` / `^@` on a text or uuid column of a `sift_col` index finds its dictionary codes without testing every string: the first lookups in a dictionary of 1,024 strings or more scan it, the next ones use a hash table (equality) and a byte-order permutation of the dictionary (prefix, a bisection to the range of strings, the work proportional to the matches), both built lazily on the parsed dictionary the backend keeps. 1,000,000 rows, a prefix count: 15 to 20 ms before, 5.2 ms warm; the first query of a new backend (it parses the dictionary, still a cost proportional to the strings) uuid equality 192 to 223 ms before, 26 to 39 ms. The dictionary cache limit is `dict_cache_kb` in the registry (`sift.test_limits`, docs/LIMITS.md), replacing the testing GUC. No format change. `bench/col_scan.py` measures it, docs/COL-SCAN.md shows the numbers. Tests `col_dict_lookup`, `col_dict_cache`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a dictionary of 1,024 strings or more of a text, varchar or uuid column of a `sift_col` index or a `sift_table` is stored in blocks of 64 strings that a reader decompresses one at a time, where that adds at most 3% to the segment, and with the codes in byte order too where a lookup structure reads it. The row fetch of a `sift_table` and the strings an index-only scan returns no longer load the dictionary: the first fetch of a new backend on 200,000 rows with a unique 110-byte text takes 1.0 ms, where it took 47.4 ms (a page of 50 rows 1.2 ms, where 58.7 ms), and a backend whose cache cannot hold the parsed dictionary (23 MB; a segment of 700,000 such rows is over the 32 MB one entry may take) no longer parses it again for every unit it reads (89 to 96 ms each at a 16 MB cache); the dictionary costs 8 kB of the backend instead of 23 MB. The blocks tie with the compact form for ids, hashes, uuids and long strings (index of 200,000 uuids +0.8%, of unique 37-byte text +2.2%, of unique 110-byte text -1.9%) and lose to it for short strings that repeat words (titles and names of real text: +19% to +30% of the index of one column), which is why the writer estimates the extra from 16 blocks and the start of the dictionary and keeps the compact form for those (index growth 0.0% on every column measured above 3%). The order for an equality, an `IN` list, a prefix (`LIKE 'abc%'`, `^@`) or a range test costs 4 bytes a string (+10% to +70% of an index of one column): the option `dict_lookup` is `auto` by default and writes it for the columns of `point_columns`, `key_columns` and `return_columns`; `on` writes it for every column and `off` for none, and a `sift_table` writes it under `sift.table_dict_lookup` (default off). The first lookup of a new backend with the order: uuid equality on 300,000 rows 12.1 ms before, 0.43 ms; text equality 29.3 ms before, 0.54 ms; text prefix 36.7 ms before, 6.5 ms; a dictionary tested more than 32 times by a backend is parsed and cached as before. A 3.2 library refuses a segment written in the new forms (REINDEX hint) unless `sift.col_format_minor` was lowered to 6 first; the segments already written are read as before and are not rewritten. The zstd contexts of the column codec are reused between calls (the bytes are the same). The limit `dict_lookup_min` (registry, `sift.test_limits`) is the smallest dictionary that gets a form. Tests `col_dict_stored`, `tbl_fetch_dict`, `col_enc`.
- Added: pg_sift: the reloption `return_columns = 's, u'` of a `sift_col` index makes the named text, varchar and uuid columns returnable, so a query that needs only the index's columns, strings included, is an Index Only Scan (the strings of a few rows are read in place out of a dictionary stored for a lookup or plain, without parsing it; a value the index could not keep comes from the heap). Off by default: with the heap in memory the Index Scan is cheaper (300,000 rows, 100 rows of `id, u, s` 0.055 ms against 0.155 ms; a `SELECT id` 0.066 against 0.158 ms, because the executor asks for every returnable column), so it is meant for tables larger than memory. No format change. Test `col_index_return_str` (all three dictionary forms, overflow values, NULL, empty, long and non-ASCII strings, write buffer, DML, promotion, merge), `col_scan` runs with the option on.
- Changed: pg_sift: the plan of a top-N that sorts every row that passes (more than 4 keys, or more than 100,000 rows to skip) charges what the sort reads and spills: each key past the first is read at every row (4.8 operators a row, 11 ms for 200,000 rows) and a sort past `hash_mem` pays 4 operators a step, not 1 (172 ms more for 200,000 rows and 4 keys, 2.6 times what the plan said). Six keys on 200,000 rows ran 200 to 350 ms in the scan against 35 to 80 ms in the normal plan and were avoided only because the plan priced the sort at the normal plan's cost; a deep offset on 2 or 3 keys keeps the scan (59 / 65 ms against 124 / 184), on 4 or more keys with a spill the normal plan wins (212 to 264 ms against 192 to 210). The plan gate (96 shapes) is unchanged. Test `col_topn_deep` (`six_keys_wide`).
- Changed: pg_sift: the UNION ALL arms of a two-level or fused scan go from 4 to 16 and the virtual leaves of a scan (expressions on a jsonb path with a key) from 8 to 16; one more of either keeps the normal plan under the slug `shape_width`, and `sift.explain_why` says which limit it passed. The limits of the arms, virtual leaves, columns of a joined table read by expressions and aggregates of a rows set are read through the registry, so `sift.test_limits` lowers them. Tests `agg_shape_widths` (each width at N and N + 1, scan against the normal plan), `agg_lookup_budget`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the member of an element of a list that a `sift_col` index declares typed (`doc.parts[]{score float8}`), read as `(p ->> 'score')::float8` over `jsonb_array_elements(doc -> 'parts') p`, keeps the aggregate scan when some elements hold a value the leaf cannot store: the scan reads those rows again from the heap, converts a numeric string as the cast does, and raises the error of a value SQL cannot convert for a row that keeps the element, as the normal plan does. A condition on the elements, which could drop the element that raises and keep another (the scan keeps one error for a row), keeps the normal plan under `dirty_units`. Tests `agg_scalar_dirty_elems`, `agg_scalar_dirty_share`, `agg_scalar_overflow` and the `vd_scalar_elems_*` fixtures of the differential suite.
- Changed: pg_sift: the `FILTER` of an aggregate that casts a typed scalar path of a `jsonb` column (`count(*) FILTER (WHERE (doc ->> 'n')::int4 = 3)`) keeps the aggregate scan when some documents hold a value the leaf cannot store, as does a cast in the argument of an aggregate with a `FILTER` (`sum((doc ->> 'v')::float8) FILTER (WHERE grp = 1)`). The scan reads those rows again from the heap and raises the error of a value SQL cannot convert for the rows the aggregate evaluates the cast for: every row that reaches it for the cast in a `FILTER`, the rows the `FILTER` keeps for the cast in an argument, and none that the `WHERE` or the `FILTER` drops. Before, any such row kept the query on the normal plan (`dirty_units`). Tests `agg_scalar_dirty_filter`, `agg_scalar_dirty_share`, `agg_scalar_overflow` and the `vd_scalar_filter_*` fixtures of the differential suite.
- Changed: pg_sift: a condition of the `WHERE` that casts a typed scalar path of a `jsonb` column (`WHERE (doc ->> 'v')::float8 > 3`, a comparison with a constant, an IN list of integers or a NULL test, for `float8`, `int4`, `int8` and `bool` paths, or the cast of `doc -> 'v'` for `float8`) keeps the aggregate scan when some documents hold a value the leaf cannot store: the scan reads those rows again from the heap, converts a numeric string as the cast does, and raises the error of a value SQL cannot convert only for a row that every other condition keeps. PostgreSQL tests the conditions of a scan by cost and then in the written order, so the scan answers as it does exactly when each other condition is cheaper than the cast or as cheap and written before it; otherwise (a costlier condition, a condition that may raise, two casts of paths that hold bad values, an OR) the query keeps the normal plan, and `sift.explain_refusals` names the new slug `dirty_order`. Before, any such row kept the query on the normal plan (`dirty_units`). Tests `agg_scalar_dirty_where`, `agg_scalar_dirty_share`, `agg_scalar_overflow` and the `vd_scalar_where_*` fixtures of the differential suite.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the rows the aggregate scan reads again from the heap (a value a typed leaf cannot store) from a sample of the flagged units: the statistics of a `sift_col` index count the overflow slots of up to four flagged units of each segment (one chunk read each, once, when the statistics are made; no format change), and the share of the rows read again is that density times the share of flagged units, where it was a flat 2%. A row read again is priced at what it measured (about 1.6 microseconds, five times what the model charged). A table with a few percent of dirty documents keeps the scan (3%: 8.7 ms against 28.1 ms for the normal plan at 100,000 items); one with a third or more keeps the normal plan, which is cheaper there (50% dirty: 27 ms against 83 ms for the scan). Test `agg_scalar_dirty_share`.
- Added: pg_sift: `CREATE INDEX` of a `sift_col` index with a wildcard path (`paths = 'm.* int4'`) says when the keys passed the dictionary of 65,536 keys, with the share of the units that hold a key beyond it (`NOTICE: the keys of m.* passed the 65536 keys the index keeps in 3 of 40 units (7.5%)`, a hint to declare the keys queries read by name). The keys and values past the limit were already stored as overflow and read from the heap, and a query that reads the leaf keeps the normal plan (`dirty_units`); nothing said so. Testing builds: `sift.test_limits = 'shred_keys=16'`. Test `col_wildcard_keys`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a scan of thousands of partitions took time quadratic in their number: each partition's source registered the scan's snapshot with the resource owner, which then held thousands of registrations of one snapshot (its hash adds and forgets them in time proportional to the equal keys it holds: about half of the instructions of a query over 5,000 partitions), and each begin of a range of units found its partition by walking the partitions from the first. The scan registers its snapshot once (`SIFT_COLO_SNAP_HELD`), finds the partition by bisection, and a source no longer zeroes the 10 kB of directory copy and test cache it has not filled. A query over 5,000 partitions of 6 rows each: 907 to 312 million instructions per run (the normal plan: 300 million); backend CPU time, with the host at a load of 30, 2.7 times the normal plan's before, 1.7 to 2.1 after (last-level cache misses, from the pages of the index of each partition, are three times those of the normal plan: the remaining difference). Tests `agg_partitions`, `agg_partitions_many`, `agg_par_partitions`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the spine of a two-level aggregate (a series or table of buckets joined to the arms) is matched through a hash of its join values, not by a pass over its rows for each distinct key of the cells, and it is no longer limited to 10,000 rows (slug `spine_rows`): it is bounded like the other tables the scan loads, by `sift.agg_lookup_max_kb` (new, 256 MB by default; rows times their width plus 64 bytes a row, as the planner estimates them; `sift.agg_lookup_max_rows` still limits the rows alone when it is set, and is -1 by default). Spine of 1,000 / 10,000 / 100,000 rows over 20,000 facts and 5,000 cell keys: 60 / 425 / 693 ms (the 100,000 rows were refused) before, 10.6 / 17 / 130 ms now. Tests `agg_two_level_spine`, `agg_refusals`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the rows of a joined table are shared with the other backends by default (`sift.agg_lookup_shared_kb` is -1: the smaller of `sift.agg_lookup_cache_kb` and 5% of `shared_buffers`; 0 turns it off, as it was before): sessions that run the same query at once wait for the one that loads the table (30 s at most) and read its rows in place; a system with no room for the segment (a small `/dev/shm`) gives a notice and each backend loads its own, as before. 32 sessions that join a 200,000-row table: 254 MB (private) and 88 MB (shared) in all, 7.9 MB and 2.5 MB for each session after the first. Test `020_lookup_shared_concurrent` (16 sessions, one load).
- Changed: pg_sift: a scan holds up to 32 lookup-joined tables (was 8), 128 value combinations (was 16), 16 columns of a joined table read by expressions (was 6) and 8 ORDER BY columns of a first-row lookup child (was 3); the arrays of the tuples of a chunk are sized by the query, so a narrow query costs what it did. One more of the first three keeps the normal plan and `sift.explain_why` names the limit and its value (slug `shape_width`); one more ORDER BY column runs the sub-select once per key as a lookup subquery.
- Changed: pg_sift: a numeric `IN` / `NOT IN` list of more than 64 constants is pushed into the scan as a hash set of the scaled values (it was evaluated by PostgreSQL for each distinct value, or refused), with NaN equal to NaN only and the constants no stored value can equal left out. Test `agg_numeric_in_list`.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.table_wide_rows` (3.3, default on; `off` refuses the row again): a row of a `sift_table` that does not fit one write buffer (4 MB by default, 8 MB at most; it raised `row of sift_table is too large`) is written to tail pages beyond the page table of its buffer, up to PostgreSQL's 1 GB. The buffer that takes one is closed behind the row and marked directory format 11, which a build that does not know it refuses to write to; a row that fits keeps the layout of earlier versions, and a buffer without a wide row stays format 0. 5 / 50 / 200 MB rows insert in 45 / 400 / 1,400 ms; after `VACUUM` the pages are free again and the rows are in column units. The continuation pages are validated when read (a page that is not one, or holds no bytes, is data corruption). Tests `tbl_wide_rows`, `tbl_wide`, `errors_hints`.
- Added: pg_sift: every refusal of the aggregate pushdown has a slug that is a `semantic` shape or a `limit` (`sift.refusal_slugs()`, generated into the tables of USAGE and COL-PLAN), and a refusal by a fixed limit of docs/LIMITS.md names the limit and its value (`the limit group_keys is 32`) in `sift.explain_refusals` and `sift.explain_why`. The session counters `sift.stats()` count `refused.<slug>` and `limit.<id>`, with `sift.explain_refusals` or the new `sift.count_refusals` (no notice). New slugs `shape_width` (joined tables and value combinations) and `error_parity` (a cast of a JSON null before PostgreSQL 18, a `jsonb_typeof` guard around a read of its path, which used to report `other`).
- Added: docs: `docs/REFERENCE.md` and a README block list every user-facing object of schema `sift` (signature, result, release that added it, description), generated from the install scripts: the description is its `COMMENT ON`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a number or boolean path of a `jsonb` column that a `sift_col` index declares typed (`doc.v float8`) and a query reads as `(doc ->> 'v')::float8` or `(doc -> 'v')::float8` in an aggregate argument or a grouping expression keeps the aggregate scan when some documents hold a value the leaf cannot store (a numeric string, a boolean, a container): the scan reads those rows again from the heap and answers as the normal plan does, a numeric string converted and a value SQL cannot convert raising the same error for the same row, and nothing for a row a condition on another column drops. Before, any such row kept the whole query on the normal plan (`dirty_units`), or made a plan made before the value was written raise `0A000`. The path read in a `WHERE` condition, a join or a sub-select, or under a `CASE` or `FILTER`, and a query with an unnest keep the normal plan as before. Tests `agg_scalar_dirty`, `agg_scalar_dirty_share`, `agg_scalar_overflow` and the `vd_scalar_*` fixtures of the differential suite.
- Changed: pg_sift: `count(DISTINCT)`, `sum(DISTINCT)` and `avg(DISTINCT)` of a numeric column or numeric expression can run with workers (they ran on the leader alone): a worker sends the (group, number) pairs of its set with the numbers themselves, since the ids of its table of them are its own, and the leader counts or adds each number once per group. 200,000 rows, `count(DISTINCT amount) GROUP BY grp`: 8.5 ms in one process, 14.7 / 12.8 / 14.3 ms with 1 / 2 / 4 workers forced (the start of a worker is about 6 ms: the planner keeps the leader alone below the cost of that, as for every aggregate), `sum(DISTINCT amount), avg(DISTINCT amount)`: 72 ms alone, 79 / 74 / 61 ms. `EXPLAIN (VERBOSE)` shows `Serial Because` for a scan that cannot have workers whatever the cost. COVERAGE X02 is Yes, the aggregates that stay on the leader (a generic aggregate with DISTINCT, an integer expression through a combination of values) are X06. Test `agg_distinct_parallel`; the diff fuzz generates the aggregates.
- Changed: pg_sift: the errors say what to do and carry a code that tells a limit from a bug. Every error with SQLSTATE 54000, 0A000 or 55000 has a hint or a detail; no error is XX000 any more except `SIFT_INVARIANT` (an `Assert` in an assert build) for what the code rules out; a row or unit of a `sift_table` that a concurrent merge or defrag replaced is 40001 (retry); a numeric beyond 38 digits in a wide leaf is 22003; a lost parallel peer is 08006 with a hint; the format messages name the pg_sift version of the build. `scripts/check-errors.sh` (lint job, `scripts/ci.sh`) enforces it. `sift.score()` outside a sift scan says where a score exists. `CREATE PUBLICATION` and `ALTER PUBLICATION` of a `sift_table` without pg_sift in `shared_preload_libraries` now fail at once (3.3 scripts: the event trigger `sift_publication_check`) instead of at the first write. Tests `errors_hints`, `007_tbl_logical`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a scan that meets a value its index cannot store in a leaf its plan did not know of (a generic plan kept by a transaction, a value committed after the plan was made) invalidates the cached plans of the table in every session before it raises its `0A000` error, and the hint says to run the statement again: the next execution is planned with the leaf flagged (the normal plan, or a source that reads the rows again). PostgreSQL has no way to plan a statement again once it runs, so the statement that meets the value still fails. Test `agg_vleaf_overflow`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the planner prices the memory of the group table of a `Custom Scan (SiftAgg)`: the groups it expects times the bytes of a group (the group arrays of each aggregate, the ids of the keys; about 185 bytes for one key and five aggregates, measured) against the memory of a hash aggregate (`work_mem` times `hash_mem_multiplier`). Past it the scan is charged the spill of sorted runs of partial states, priced as `cost_agg` prices the spill of a hash aggregate, and when that makes it dearer than the normal plan the arm keeps the normal plan with the slug `group_memory` (`sift.explain_why`); a scan that fits is priced as before. Test `agg_group_memory`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the group table of a `Custom Scan (SiftAgg)` is bounded by `hash_mem` (`work_mem` times `hash_mem_multiplier`, 128 groups at least) for the aggregates whose partial states merge by key value (`count`, `sum`, `avg`, `min`, `max` over integers, floats and numerics, with any number of keys, no `DISTINCT`, no top-N): when the table passes the budget at the end of a unit, its groups are written to hash partitions on disk in the partial blob form of the parallel workers, the table is emptied, and the partitions are merged one at a time at the end (a partition that still does not fit is split again, up to `group_spill_depth` levels, then ERROR 54000 with the settings to raise). Results equal the unbounded table's (sums of floats within 1e-9 relative, their order depends on the merge). The planner asks for no workers when the table must spill (each would hold a table of its own), and `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` shows `Group Table Spills`. 200,000 groups of one integer key, `count/sum/avg/min/max`: peak backend memory (`VmHWM`, 16 to 19 MB of it the shared buffers the query touches) +44 MB unbounded, +20 MB at `work_mem` 4 MB; 115 to 145 ms unbounded, 313 to 407 ms spilling at 4 MB (the normal plan: 218 to 274 ms), unchanged when the table fits. An aggregate that cannot spill (`count(DISTINCT)`, a two-level arm, a set key, several grouping sets) keeps the scan and its whole table until the table is more than `group_nospill_factor` (32) times the larger of hash memory and 8 MB, and then the normal plan with the slug `group_memory` and the detail naming the factor. Registry rows `group_nospill_factor`, `group_spill_parts`, `group_spill_depth`. Tests `agg_group_spill`, `agg_group_spill_limits`.
- Changed: pg_sift: `count(DISTINCT x)` and the distinct `sum` and `avg` of numbers spill with the group table (they kept the scan and the whole table until it was 32 times hash memory): the set of a group goes to its partition as the (group, value) pairs and bitmap chunks the parallel workers send the leader, and the partitions united when they are merged. 150,000 rows, 20,000 groups, `count(DISTINCT val), count(DISTINCT tx)` at `work_mem` 64 kB: 6 spills, the rows of the normal plan. Still on the scan and its whole table until `group_nospill_factor`: the arms of a two-level aggregate, grouping sets, set keys, top-N, ordered-set and generic aggregates (their values are one sort or a state the blob does not hold per group) and `sum`, `min`, `max` over combos (numeric and text columns). Test `agg_group_spill`.
- Changed: pg_sift: `DISTINCT ON` in the aggregate scan accepts a unique index column that can be NULL in the `ORDER BY` when a strict condition of the `WHERE` clause (`col > 0`, `col IS NOT NULL`, `col = ANY (..)`) leaves no row with a NULL there, since no two rows can then tie; a table row of the nullable side of an outer join keeps the rule.
- Changed: pg_sift: an integer or date key of the joined table that is a cast of its columns (`dims.at::date = facts.ts`, a widened integer) is a hash key of the lookup join in the aggregate scan, evaluated for each row loaded, as a column is (a series of days joined to timestamps: 51 / 54 / 54 / 55 ms before and 2.3 / 2.6 / 4.7 / 7.7 ms after at 25k / 50k / 100k / 200k rows); an expression that can raise or is not immutable stays a condition of the table's rows.
- Changed: pg_sift: SiftAgg reads a partitioned table of up to 32,768 partitions after PostgreSQL's pruning (was 4,096, and a table above it kept the normal plan under the slug `partition_source`, the one of a partition without a usable index): above the limit `sift.explain_why` names `partition_count` with the number of partitions and the limit. The cost of opening the partitions a scan reads still decides against the normal plan. The remainders of a hash level are computed once per level for the values of the keys (was once per partition and combination of values, and none above 4,096 combinations: now up to 1,048,576), so `k = ANY (..)`, a join on a hash key and a parameter prune a hash table of thousands of partitions in a pass over the partitions. Documented: `max_locks_per_transaction` needs about 2 x the partitions of the plan whichever plan runs (the scan takes no more locks than the normal plan: 1,204 and 1,204 at 600 partitions). Tests `agg_partitions_many` (limit lowered to the edge of 100 partitions, lock count), `agg_partitions` (hash remainders, limits lowered), `agg_par_partitions`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a `sift_col` leaf whose strings in one unit (256 heap pages, up to 74,496 rows) pass 1 GB no longer stops `CREATE INDEX`, a merge or a promotion (`the variable-length values of a unit exceed 2 GB`, `54000`; 74,000 rows of 30 KB values reach it with the values detoasted). The values after the budget are stored as overflow (NULL and flagged, as a value the leaf cannot store is), a NOTICE names the leaf and the superblock, and a query that reads the leaf keeps the normal plan (`dirty_units`); the other leaves are scanned as before. `sift.col_verify` accepts such a string against the heap. A wide-numeric value that does not fit 128 bits is stored as overflow too, where it raised `XX000` (no column value reaches it: `numeric(p, s)` bounds the digits). Testing builds: `sift.test_limits = 'unit_bytes=65536'`. Test `col_wide_values`.
- Changed: pg_sift: an `ORDER BY .. LIMIT n OFFSET m` with `n + m` past 100,000, or with more than 4 sort columns, no longer keeps the normal plan by rule: the top-N scan keeps every row that passes as its keys and TID (in memory up to `hash_mem`, then a `Tuplesort` that writes runs to disk), sorts them, and the planner takes it by cost against PostgreSQL's sort (`EXPLAIN` shows `Top-N: .. (limit N, sorted)`). A heap of more than 1024 rows is priced against that sort and loses to it where it costs more. When the output columns are plain columns of the table, the first `m` rows that the `Limit` throws away are handed to it as rows of NULLs and are not read from the heap (`Heap Fetches` is `n`, not `n + m`). 200,000 rows, `ORDER BY ts, id LIMIT 10 OFFSET 150000` 52 ms against 113 ms for the normal plan (`OFFSET 50000`: 45 against 81 ms, `OFFSET 0` unchanged at 1.5 ms); the limits are the registry rows `topn_rows` and `topn_keys` (`sift.test_limits` lowers them in a testing build). Tests `col_topn_deep`, `col_topn_deep_limits`; the diff fuzz generates up to 6 sort keys and offsets past the table.
- Changed: pg_sift: the rows a `Custom Scan (SiftAgg)` reports to the plan above are the groups of the key columns' distinct values where a filter of the table has no statistics (a jsonb path, an expression), not the groups of the few rows PostgreSQL guesses that filter keeps; a join or window over the aggregates is planned for the groups the scan makes.
- Changed: pg_sift: three errors of the aggregate scan that the normal plan does not have are gone: the outer `sum` of an int8 aggregate of the arms is a 128-bit sum that comes out as numeric like `sum(int8)` (it raised "a sum of the aggregate scan exceeds bigint"), and a value-combination cache that reaches 16,777,216 entries (a lookup join or an expression over a column with that many distinct values) starts again instead of raising "too many distinct value combinations" (`EXPLAIN ANALYZE` shows `Combo Caches Restarted`; `sift.test_limits = 'combo_entries=N'` lowers the limit in a testing build).
- Fixed: pg_sift: two words of a text index that share their first 255 bytes were one term (a query for either returned both documents, not rechecked). A word over 255 bytes (folded) is now its first 236 bytes (cut at a character boundary), U+FFFF and a 64-bit hash of all of it, as the keyword analyzer already did, and is not stemmed; a prefix query longer than 236 bytes asks for the first 236. Indexes built before keep such words cut at 255 bytes (no format change, the analyzer version is unchanged so they stay readable): `REINDEX` an index whose documents hold words over 255 bytes (base64 blobs, hashes, long URLs); until then a query for such a word finds nothing in the old segments. Test `analyze_long_token`.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.rls_scores` (superuser setting, off by default): a role under a row-level security policy gets the ranked scan and `sift.score()`, where it got an error. The policy's quals run on every row the ranker offers before any qual of the query (the order of a sequential scan: a function of the query that raises for a hidden row never sees one), the ranker is asked for more rows until `k` pass, and no hidden row is returned; policies that call functions or read other tables, `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY`, `BYPASSRLS` roles and partitioned tables are tested against the full ranking filtered by the tenant. The scores are those of the whole index, so they depend on the hidden rows (the caveat is in docs/USAGE.md, "Row-level security"): the plan's estimates do not read the index, `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` prints none of the index's counters for such a scan, a keyed scan and `sift.max_score()` are not offered, and a role cannot switch the setting itself. Ranked top 10 at 100,000 documents, 50% / 5% / 0.5% of the rows visible, five queries from a term in 5% of the documents to a two-term query, median of 3: 0.15 to 0.9 ms (the owner without a policy 0.15 to 0.8 ms); the plan the role had before, the policy's bitmap and the match tested on each visible row, takes 5 to 31 ms to return the matches unranked and 4.9 to 28 ms for ten of them. Tests `rank_rls`, `rls_stats`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: with `sift.rls_scores` on, a policy that is itself a match (`USING (tenant = current_user AND body @@> 'open')`) was a term of the ranked scan's query, so the scores of a role under it counted that word and were not the table owner's for the same rows; it is a qual of the heap row now, as any other qual of a policy. Test `rank_rls`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the row estimate of a `@@>` condition on a partition or an inheritance child of a table under a row-level security policy came from the index, so `EXPLAIN` (no setting needed) told a role whether a word is held by rows the policy hides (the child carries the parent's policy in its restrictions, not in its range table entry, which is what the check read); it is the planner's default now, as for a table without partitions. The score refusal names the table when a partition or child is under a policy (it said "needs a sift index on every partition"). Test `rls_stats`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.score()` in a query level that PostgreSQL moves: a score read only in `HAVING` (`HAVING max(sift.score(ctid)) > c`) and the source of an `INSERT ... SELECT` raised "must be computed by a sift scan" (the insert said "FOR UPDATE and FOR SHARE") after the planner pulled the subquery up; both are planned with the scan. `UPDATE`, `DELETE`, `MERGE` and `FOR UPDATE` re-check the rows they read, which a scan cannot: a subquery, view or `WITH` query of such a statement that reads the score is now planned apart (as with `OFFSET 0` or `MATERIALIZED`), so `UPDATE t SET s = q.s FROM (SELECT id, sift.score(ctid) AS s FROM docs WHERE body @@> 'x') q WHERE t.id = q.id` works; the same score read straight from a table the statement joins stays an error, and its hint says how to write it. The other `sift.score() must be computed by a sift scan` outputs of the tests (no `@@>` condition, a score of another relation, `sift.enable_scan = off`, a score in `WHERE` that is not a cutoff) are the documented errors. Test `rank_score_shapes`.
- Added: pg_sift: `docs/LIMITS.md`, the table of the fixed limits (value, unit, what happens at the limit, the setting that changes it), generated by `scripts/gen-limits.sh` from the registry in `src/core/limits.h`; `scripts/gen-limits.sh --check` runs in lint and fails when a `*_MAX_*` or `*_LIMIT*` macro has neither a row nor a reason in `scripts/limits.allow`. Testing builds: `sift.test_limits = 'id=value,...'` lowers a limit so that a test reaches it on a few rows, and `sift._test_limits()` lists the registry.
- Added: pg_sift: `ALTER EXTENSION pg_sift UPDATE` to 3.3 (any older version: it is the last step of the path) prints what is left to do, as warnings with the commands (the first 20 of each and the count of the rest) or a notice that nothing is: a `REINDEX` of every `sift` text index except `analyzer = keyword` (**queries miss rows** without it: a word over 255 bytes is stored as its start and a hash since 3.3, an older index holds it cut at 255 bytes, and the index cannot say whether it holds one, so it stays listed), a `REINDEX` of a `sift_col` index whose option came after its units (`cube_minmax`, JSON `paths`, `rollup_dims`, `key_columns`), and a `VACUUM FULL` of a `sift_table` written below the column format of its schema (calendar cells of date and timestamp columns, minor 8; complete JSON kinds, minor 6), which takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock. `sift.upgrade_commands()` (3.3 scripts) lists them again, one row per command (`kind`, `object`, `reason`, `command`, `required`); `sift.upgrade_check` takes its option and table rows from it. The check only reads, and never fails the update: when the library cannot answer it says to run the function after reconnecting. Tests `extension_lifecycle`, `compat_matrix`, `upgrade_check`.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.explain_why(query)` (3.3 scripts), the public form of what the test function `sift._test_explain_why` returned: one row per arm of the aggregate pushdown (`arm`, `used`, `slug`, `reason`, `scan_cost`, `normal_cost`) for one `SELECT` that is planned and not run. Test `agg_refusals` no longer needs the test build; `extension_lifecycle` and `compat_matrix` check the function after each update path.
- Changed: pg_sift: `sift.stats()` reports the skipped units as `units_pruned_zone` (zone maps), `units_pruned_set` (small-set summaries, the `Units Pruned By Set Summary` of `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`) and `units_pruned_search` (units without a row of the search selection) in place of the one `units_pruned` (their sum is the `Units Pruned` of `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`). The units a small-set summary cleared are counted when the scan passes them, as the others are, where they were counted when it began: a scan that ended early (a top-N) over-reported them.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.upgrade_check` warns when `key_columns` or `rollup_dims` is set on an index whose older units lack the section (column format below 10, below 9): they are read or scanned until a `REINDEX`. Test `upgrade_check`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the README PostgreSQL badge says 17, 18 and 19.
- Added: pg_sift: a distribution kit: container image recipes on the official `postgres` image (PostgreSQL 17 and 18; `packaging/docker/`, [docs/CONTAINERS.md](docs/CONTAINERS.md)) with a Docker Compose example and a CloudNativePG image-volume extension image, a five-minute [quickstart](docs/QUICKSTART.md), `bench/bench kit` (the ten store questions at 100,000 orders in docker, every answer compared with `sift.enable_agg = off`), the metadata files `packaging/pgxman.yaml` and `META.json`, and [docs/SUPPORT.md](docs/SUPPORT.md) (supported versions, upgrade paths, the downgrade rule). No image is published.
- Changed: pg_sift: an `ALTER TABLE` that adds a column to a `sift_table` past the 127 it holds, or changes or renames a column so that its declared paths count again, fails at once with the error of `CREATE TABLE` (`a sift_table holds at most 127 columns`, `54000`; an event trigger, `sift_table_ddl_check`, in `pg_sift--3.3.sql` and `pg_sift--3.2--3.3.sql`), partitions included. It used to succeed and fail the next write. `DROP COLUMN` is not checked, so that a table that 3.2 let grow too wide can be narrowed.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.table_space(tbl)` reports `vblocks_left`, the virtual blocks of TIDs a `sift_table` has not handed out yet (up to 291 rows each, never reused; `VACUUM FULL` renumbers them). The function is replaced in `pg_sift--3.2--3.3.sql`: `ALTER EXTENSION pg_sift UPDATE`.
- Changed: pg_sift: planning and starting a scan of a partitioned table cost less for each partition: the layout, statistics and pre-aggregates of a partition's index are kept for the backend (`sift cache partition facts`) and read again only when its directory, the pages of its table or their visibility map, or a catalog entry of the index or the table change; the all-visible pages are counted from the bytes of the visibility map the table uses, not from all of every map page; a partition's statistics are kept packed (the leaves in use, not 15 kB); the partitions of a range key that its conditions leave out are found by bisecting the bounds PostgreSQL keeps, not by a proof for each; and a partition's heap is opened when the scan reaches it or its bound is first read. One aggregate over 1,000 partitions plans in about half the instructions, a generic `EXECUTE` that prunes 4,000 to one starts in less than half.
- Changed: pg_sift: a lookup join keyed by columns of an earlier lookup's table (`LEFT JOIN map ON map.kind = e.kind AND map.cat = i.cat` after `JOIN e`) makes one cache entry per distinct value of those columns, not per row of `e` that holds them: 500 entities of 4 types are 4 entries, not 500 times the combinations of the other keys, and the entry table stays in cache. On a neutral two-lookup shape (workload table) 200,000 rows: 211.9M to 184.5M instructions per execution (-13%), 105M to 78M cycles. Test `agg_lookup_chain`.
- Changed: pg_sift: the aggregate scan counts the tuples of a chunk once for the groups seen and for every aggregate that takes all of them (`count(*)`, the count of an unfiltered sum or average), in one pass over the group ids where it made one per aggregate. User instructions per execution, 100k orders: items top-N (float sum, count) 36.22M to 33.71M (-6.9%), a 100k-row entity top-N 7.09M to 6.71M (-5.5%), three keys 33.17M to 32.48M (-2.1%); shapes whose aggregates skip rows are unchanged. Results are the same (`agg_dense`, `col_agg_*`, `agg_distinct`, `agg_numeric`).
- Changed: pg_sift: the aggregate scan finds the NULL tuples of an integer, date or dictionary key in one branch-free pass and keeps them as a bitmap, where it made three passes with a branch per tuple (the id patch, the range of the non-null values, the fix after the kernels). A date-bucket aggregate with a text key, 100k rows: 15.60M to 15.03M user instructions and 15% fewer branch mispredictions per execution; three keys 25% fewer mispredictions (instructions +1.1%). Results are the same.
- Changed: pg_sift: the value of the member of an object by key (a dynamic key of a jsonb object, `metrics -> 'key'`) is read by walking the elements the key marks and finding the row of each by the offsets, where it searched every row's elements for the last marked one: 17.84M to 17.19M user instructions (-3.7%) on a one-key filtered metrics shape, 100k rows. Results are the same.
- Changed: pg_sift: the backend keeps the decoded list lengths of a unit of an immutable segment (the counts of a jsonb array leaf and their running sums, which every read of its elements starts from) with the dictionaries it already keeps (`sift.test_col_dict_cache` is the budget in test builds, 64 MB), so a scan that keeps a few rows of every unit does not decode the length chunk of the whole unit again on each query. A list scan with one key of 300 over a neutral table (2 to 3 elements per row, three runs of 15, ms): 25k rows 0.183 to 0.183, 50k 0.261 to 0.210, 100k 0.445 to 0.342, 200k 0.838 to 0.560 (normal plan 0.637); user instructions at 200k 6.98M to 4.89M. No on-disk change. Test `col_dict_cache`.
- Added: pg_sift: `CREATE INDEX`, `REINDEX` and `sift.col_refresh` of a `sift_col` index say, as a NOTICE, where a cap of its options leaves 10% of the units or more without what was asked: a `cube(..)` dim that has no histogram in a unit with more groups than the cap (4,096), and a `key_columns` leaf whose summary is a bloom filter, not its keys, past 32 keys. A query on them scans those units; `sift.col_rollup_misses` counts the scans.
- Added: pg_sift: the automatic rewrite of a `sift_col` index can be by range and bounded: `sift.col_auto_merge_partition_kb` (off by default) keeps the index as segments of about that size and rewrites one at a time, the one with most units holding a dead row (over `sift.col_auto_merge_dirty_pct` of its own units, whatever the share of the whole index), then the small segments the promotions left over the partitions, so that an index churned in one place (a retention window, a hot tail) writes that place only, holds the maintenance lock for one step and needs free space for one partition (the merge tiers leave the partitions alone; the lock is let go between steps). `sift.maintenance_wal_rate` (kB per second, 0 = no limit) makes the merges of the maintenance worker wait before the page that would pass that rate of WAL, and `sift.col_auto_merge_max_workers` (0 = no limit) caps the rewrite workers of a database that run at once (advisory-lock slots, nothing in shared memory). No format change. Tests `col_auto_merge` (regress), `col_auto_merge_partition`, `col_auto_merge_part_crash`.
- Added: pg_sift: the background pass of a `sift_table` (`sift.table_auto_defrag`, still off by default) is asked for by a delete, an update or a read (an index scan, a fetch, an aggregate) as well as by the writer that closes a write buffer, each backend at most once per `sift.maintenance_naptime`: a table that is only deleted from, or whose writes are too small to close a buffer, was never defragmented. `sift.table_auto_defrag_dead_pct` (20, the autovacuum scale factor; 100 never asks for dead rows) is the tombstone share that asks, and a pass rewrites at most `sift.table_auto_defrag_budget` MB (128) of segments, the most dead first. 200,000 rows, 12 cycles of 4% deleted and as many inserted (item/event churn, PostgreSQL 18, scratch cluster): file 19.2 MB without maintenance, 8.2 MB with the pass (4.96 MB with autovacuum on), 50 bytes a live row instead of 118, the 3-column aggregate 11.5 ms instead of 27.8; WAL 113 MB instead of 88. Delete-only churn: 6.8 MB without, 5.5 MB with. Writes too small to leave dead rows: nothing is asked for. Tests `017_tbl_autodefrag`, `019_tbl_defrag` (a heap twin, `wal_consistency_checking = generic`, a standby, a worker killed with SIGKILL in the middle of a pass), isolation `tbl_maintain`.
- Changed: pg_sift: `VACUUM` and the passes of a `sift_table` rewrite the segments that are most dead first (`tbl_defrag_order`), and a segment of an older layout no longer stops the rewrite of the ones after it; a pass bounded by the byte budget has freed the most it could.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the table `VACUUM` honours the cost delay (`vacuum_cost_delay`, `vacuum_cost_limit`; a background pass takes `sift.table_auto_defrag_delay`, now 0 to 100 ms as the cost delay allows) at every segment and page it rewrites, where it ran flat out; the worker applies it as an autovacuum worker does.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a role that is neither a superuser nor the owner of the extension could not drop anything once pg_sift was installed (**known issue of 3.2.0 and 3.2.1**): the trigger `sift.table_paths_drop` that clears the paths of a dropped `sift_table` ran as the dropping role, so every `DROP TABLE`, `DROP INDEX`, `DROP SCHEMA ... CASCADE` and `DROP OWNED` of that role (a table or index of any kind, with or without pg_sift) failed with `permission denied for schema sift`; `sift.table_set_paths` failed with `permission denied for table table_paths` for the owner of the table. Both functions are `SECURITY DEFINER` with `search_path = pg_catalog, pg_temp` now (the new script, and `ALTER EXTENSION pg_sift UPDATE` to 3.3 for an installed 3.2.x); `sift.table_set_paths` still requires the owner of the table (the session's role). Workaround on 3.2.0 and 3.2.1 until the update, as a superuser in each database with the extension: `ALTER FUNCTION sift.table_paths_drop() SECURITY DEFINER SET search_path = pg_catalog, pg_temp;` (a restore from a dump recreates the extension from the 3.2 script: run it again; `docs/UPGRADING.md`). Test `tbl_nonsuper_ddl`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the aggregate pushdown (`Custom Scan (SiftAgg)`) no longer refuses what the normal plan answers (since 3.2.0): a role with `SELECT` on the columns a query reads but not on the whole table, or on a view and not on its table, got `permission denied for table` at run time. The scan asked for table-level `SELECT` of the invoking role; PostgreSQL's executor has checked the privileges on the columns, as the owner of the view, before it starts, and the scan adds no check. A table with row-level security read through a view of its owner (to whom no policy applies) keeps the normal plan under `table_kind`, where the scan's own row-security check refused the query.
- Changed: pg_sift: the install and the update to 3.3 grant `USAGE` on the schema `sift` to `PUBLIC`, so a DBA no longer grants it for a role to call `sift.score(ctid)` or the read-only diagnostics (the functions check the table or index as before; no table or function grant changes); `REVOKE USAGE ON SCHEMA sift FROM PUBLIC` takes it back, and an update grants it again.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.upgrade_commands()` failed for every role but a superuser (`permission denied for table table_paths`) though the REINDEX hint of the update is for the table owners: it lists the indexes and tables the caller owns or may maintain (the privilege `REINDEX` and `VACUUM FULL` ask for; a superuser sees all), and reads the declared paths through the new view `sift.table_paths_maintained`, which lists the paths of those tables only (an owner could not read the paths of its own table).
- Changed: docs: `DROP EXTENSION pg_sift` leaves the schema `sift` empty with its privileges (it did in 3.2): PostgreSQL makes the schema from the control file and refuses it as a member of the extension; `CREATE EXTENSION` gives a fresh install's objects and privileges again (`extension_lifecycle` checks it), `DROP SCHEMA sift` removes it.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `CREATE PUBLICATION` and `ALTER PUBLICATION` by a role that is not a superuser failed on a server with `wal_level = logical` (`permission denied to examine "shared_preload_libraries"`, from the trigger `sift.publication_check`, 3.3 only): it runs as the extension's owner now.
- Fixed: pg_sift: `sift.workers`, `sift.col_rollup_state` and `sift.col_rollup_requests` could be read by superusers only (`permission denied for view`): every role with USAGE on the schema can, as for the functions they list (`sift.workers` shows the indexes of the tables the caller can read).
- Added: tests: `roles_ddl`, `roles_dml_query`, `roles_maintenance`, `roles_settings`, `roles_upgrade` (SQL) and `roles_cluster` (pg_dump, pg_restore, tablespace, publication) run what the features do for ordinary roles (a table owner with only CREATE on a schema, a member of the owner, roles that read, write or have no access, a role under row-level security, a role with MAINTAIN): the suites that run as a superuser could not see the DROP failure of 3.2.0 and 3.2.1.
- Added: pg_sift: `key_columns` (`sift_col` reloption, default `auto`; `off`, or a list; the one option for the keys of a column in a unit, in place of `set_columns`, `set_limit` and `key_presence`): every unit written afterwards keeps, for the columns it names, the set of its distinct keys, as a key list or a bitmap where that takes the share of the unit's bytes that the lists of its columns have left (3%), else, for a column the user named, exactly while there are at most 32 keys and as a bloom filter beyond. A key list or bitmap is the keys of a top-level `int2`, `int4` or `date` column: a flat `count(DISTINCT key)` ORs them and reads no key chunk (1,000,000 rows in 123 units of 8,130 rows, 6,313 keys, median of 9 interleaved runs: 16.1 ms scanning the units, 1.11 ms (14.5x; the normal plan takes 314 ms); 2.0x to 8.6x on the foreign keys of three 200,000-row tables), and an equality, `IN` or range test skips the units that cannot hold a match where the zone map cannot (a key spread over all units). **`auto` is the default:** `CREATE INDEX` keeps the key list or bitmap of the `int2`, `int4` and `date` columns that are a member of a foreign key or are named `*_id`, that are not unique and that the cube does not hold, where a list fits in half of the units (+1.1% to +1.4% of the index on the three copies, never a bloom filter), writes them to the option as `auto: a, b` so that a rebuild keeps them, and tells as a NOTICE which columns, how many bytes and which it left out and why; `key_columns = 'off'` turns it off, `'a, b'` names the columns (a column the list names that cannot have a list gets the filter). Column format minor 10 for exact keys and filters and 15 for lists and bitmaps (`sift.col_format_minor` 10 and 15; a default 3.3 index already carries minors 7, 8 and 12, which a 3.2 library refuses, so this adds no refusal; at a setting below 15 the default writes none). `sift.col_verify` recomputes them, `sift.col_sections` lists them as `sets`, EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows `Units Pruned By Set Summary`, and the cost of a scan counts the units they skip. `sift.upgrade_commands()` lists an index with the option and older units as a gain from REINDEX. Tests `col_set_summary`, `col_key_presence`, `col_key_columns_auto`, `col_caps`, the minor 10 and 15 steps of `extension_lifecycle`, `compat_matrix`, `compat_31_column`, `upgrade_check`, `col_crash_matrix`.
- Fixed: pg_sift: the choice between a shared common table expression and the inline plan added up the cost of the `Custom Scan (SiftAgg)` arms below a `Custom Scan (SiftAggRows)` node, which reads the table once for all of them and costs less than they do added up, so a plan that is a third of the shared one kept the shared common table expression: three `UNION ALL` arms over the elements of a jsonb array, counted `DISTINCT` above a spine, 30,000 rows: 1,084 to 1,224 ms on the normal plan, 212 to 229 ms in the scan now (PostgreSQL 18, 2026-10-22, loaded host, 3 runs). The node now counts as its own cost. Rows are the normal plan's. No format change. Test `agg_cte_inline_rows`.
- Changed: pg_sift: a common table expression that two aggregates read is planned inline, and kept when the scans are the cheaper part, where it was kept only when the whole inline plan cost half of the materialized one: the plan above the scans (a `FULL JOIN` of two series on a jsonb array and a window over it, with the 2,000 rows PostgreSQL assumes for a series in a select list) costs the same in both and hid a gain of 60 times. Two series of weeks over two jsonb documents of the same events, `FULL JOIN`ed and windowed: 591, 1,068, 2,559 and 5,395 ms (25,000, 50,000, 100,000 and 200,000 rows) and now 27, 33, 60 and 90 ms with both series in the scan. No format change. Tests `agg_cte_inline_upper`, `agg_natural_cost` (a scope of a few entities now takes the scan, 0.6 ms against 1.8 ms).
- Changed: pg_sift: the extension SQL goes from 3.2 to 3.3 (`pg_sift--3.3.sql`, `pg_sift--3.2--3.3.sql`): run `ALTER EXTENSION pg_sift UPDATE` in every database. The functions added since 3.2.0 (`sift.col_refresh`, `sift.col_clustering`, `sift.stats`, `sift.stats_reset`, `sift.col_rollup_misses`, `sift.explain_why`) are in the 3.3 scripts only; the 3.2 scripts are the released ones, checked by `scripts/ci.sh --selftest`. Test `test/t/extension_lifecycle.sh`.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.table_bulk_groups` (on by default since 3.3, `off` keeps the write buffers): `COPY`, `CREATE TABLE AS`, `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW` and the rewrite of `ALTER TABLE .. SET ACCESS METHOD` into a `sift_table` write column groups straight away (the rows of a run of four superblocks are held in the backend, written and published together, with the versions of the loading transaction, or none for `COPY FREEZE`), where they went through write buffers that a later promotion copies into groups. 200,000 rows, one client, median of 3, PostgreSQL 18 (the host was busy): 286 bytes of WAL per row for the `COPY` and 23 more for the promotion, 79 in all with the groups; 2.8 microseconds per row from `COPY` to promoted, 2.3 with the groups; the file is 3% smaller. Only a table nothing reads while the load runs qualifies, and any other keeps the write buffers: one with a trigger (foreign keys included), a unique or exclusion index, a publication, a domain column, or a user-defined function in a default, generated column, `CHECK`, index expression or index predicate (`sift._test_tbl_bulk_refusal` says why). `INSERT .. SELECT` and single-row inserts are unchanged. No format change: the groups are the unfrozen groups of the 3.1 write path, and an older library refuses them as it refuses those (`scripts/old-lib-check.sh`). Tests `tbl_bulk_eligible`, `tbl_bulk_groups` (a heap twin for every kind of load, rollback, savepoint, error, many loads in one transaction), isolation spec `tbl_bulk_groups` (what other sessions and the transaction see, the rows waiting in the backend with their index entries in the btree), `t/019_tbl_bulk_groups.pl` (primary and standby under `wal_consistency_checking = generic`, `kill -9` in the middle of a `COPY`, immediate stop). Made the default after measuring a `COPY` of 200,000 rows (13 columns) into a `sift_table`, backend CPU 0.52 -> 0.39 s, WAL 44.2 -> 1.1 MB, the relation 1.2 MB either way; 20 loads of 2,000 rows: WAL 8.8 -> 0.3 MB, relation 6.1 -> 2.5 MB.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.col_clustering` reports `depth_max` per leaf, the most units whose zones hold one value (the deepest overlap; `point_units` is the average and hides a few deep values), so the effect of rows that arrive out of order can be watched. SQL only: no format change.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.col_clustering(index)` reports per integer, date and timestamp leaf how many units an equality or a range touches on the zone maps and whether the rows are in order (`clustered`, `partial`, `scattered`), so that a user knows if sorting the table helps; `sift.stats()` and `sift.stats_reset()` expose the counters of the `Custom Scan (SiftAgg)` scans of the session (scans, workers, units read, pruned and answered from pre-aggregates, rows decoded and read again, lookup cache hits and misses); `sift.col_rollup_misses()` counts, per index and reason slug, the scans the pre-aggregates did not answer and whether another `rollup` or `preagg` option would. The counters are folded in once per scan from the counters `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` shows, in backend memory (no shared memory, no `shared_preload_libraries`, no change to the scan loops). No format change.
- Changed: pg_sift: PostgreSQL 19 is a supported major (17, 18 and 19), no longer experimental: the tree compiles against its release candidate without a warning (an intended case fall-through is marked with `sift_fallthrough`), and the focused planner, table access method, isolation and TAP tests give the results they give on 18, also with the new `enable_eager_aggregate` on.
- Changed: pg_sift: the ON clause of a lookup join `jsonb_build_array(a1, ..) = jsonb_build_array(b1, ..)` (how a series of buckets is joined to rows by several columns) takes each element of an integer, date or text type that equals a column of the joined table as a hash key of the lookup, a NULL equal to a NULL, and keeps the other elements as a condition of the table's rows, where it tested every row of the table for every distinct input. A series of 701 days LEFT JOINed to the unnested documents of the table, one date element: 160, 173, 181 and 186 ms (25,000, 50,000, 100,000 and 200,000 rows, a cost that does not follow the rows) and now 3.0, 6.1, 7.8 and 15.5 ms; the same join on a plain `=` takes 2.7 to 13.8 ms. No format change. Test `agg_join_array_key`.
- Changed: pg_sift: planning on a partitioned table reads each partition's column index once per plan however many arms or CTEs scan the table, and keeps the statistics of up to 8192 indexes per backend (it kept 8, so a table of more partitions read every segment again for every plan); the planner no longer looks up the float operator family for every clause of every partition.
- Changed: pg_sift: every execution of a generic plan on a partitioned table (a parameter on the partition key) translated the bound of each partition again to drop the ones the parameter refutes; the bounds are kept for the backend until a relation changes, which takes the start of a scan of 4000 partitions from 48 to 30 million instructions.
- Fixed: pg_sift: a column segment written under `sift.col_format_minor = 4` or 5 (the setting for readers of 3.1) carried the zone flag of a JSON null that only 3.2 knows, so `sift.col_verify` of pg_sift 3.1 reported such an index as corrupt (its answers were right); the flag is now written from minor 6 on. Test `compat_matrix` (the releases 3.0.2, 3.1.0 and 3.2.0 write, this tree takes over, and they read what it writes at the lowest and at their newest minor).
- Added: pg_sift: the `sift.col_*_ops` classes of a `sift_col` index cover boolean, real, double precision, numeric(p <= 18, s) and char(n) (`col_bool_ops`, `col_float4_ops`, `col_float8_ops`, `col_numeric_ops`, `col_bpchar_ops`), `col_text_ops` takes `LIKE 'abc%'` and `^@` with a literal prefix (an exact prefix test, or a superset the heap scan filters for other patterns), and the index offers `Index Scan` and `Index Only Scan` (`amgettuple`, `amcanreturn` for the integer, date, timestamp, boolean, float and numeric columns; off with the rest under `sift.enable_col_scan`): a plan that stops early or reads a few rows builds no bitmap, a covering query does not read the heap (the visibility map decides), and `pg_stat_user_indexes` and `EXPLAIN` count the scans. The cost of a scan counts the units the small-set summaries (`key_columns`) skip: 1,000,000 rows, `rnd = c` returning rows 1.9 to 2.5 ms without the option and 0.18 to 0.23 ms with it (btree 0.04), `id = c` 0.09 ms (0.06), the six columns of the table in one index 28.9 MB (30.3 MB with `key_columns = 'id, rnd, s_short'`) against 170 MB for six btrees. Text and uuid equality and `LIKE` still test every entry of the segment dictionary (30 to 150 ms at 1,000,000 rows). Test `col_scan` (142 queries over the heap, a btree, the bitmap scan and the index scan through the build, the write buffer, updates, deletes, a rollback, the promotion and a merge).
- Added: pg_sift: a `sift_col` index built with the operator classes `sift.col_int2_ops`, `col_int4_ops`, `col_int8_ops`, `col_date_ops`, `col_timestamp_ops`, `col_timestamptz_ops`, `col_text_ops` and `col_uuid_ops` is a bitmap index scan (`sift.enable_col_scan`, off by default): `WHERE id = 5`, `IN` lists, ranges and `IS [NOT] NULL` on any subset of the columns of the index return rows through `Bitmap Heap Scan`, with `BitmapAnd`/`BitmapOr`, parameterized nested loops, partial and expression indexes. Nothing stored changes and the default class is the same, so an existing index is untouched; `col_text_ops` refuses a nondeterministic collation. 100,000 / 1,000,000 rows, `WHERE id = c` returning rows 0.10 / 0.11 ms (the heap scan 10.0 / 78.8, a btree 0.04 / 0.05), a range of 20% 3.4 / 24.3 ms (btree 2.5 / 29.7), `grp = c AND ts BETWEEN` 0.35 / 1.59 (btree 0.20 / 1.71); a random int equality 0.26 / 1.99 (btree 0.03 / 0.05) and a distinct text or uuid equality 4.5 / 41.7 and 4.2 / 64.4 (btree 0.06 and 0.04) are slower than a btree. The index is 0.03 to 0.56 of the btree's size on the same column (200,000 and 1,000,000 rows). Test `col_scan` compares 53 queries with the heap and a btree over the build, the write buffer, updates, deletes, a rollback, the promotion and a merge; docs/COL-SCAN.md has the verdict for each btree workload.
- Added: pg_sift: `scripts/plangate.sh`, a plan-choice gate: 96 neutral query shapes (`test/plan/shapes.sql`: a filtered group by one to three keys, top-N, joined and lateral lookups, two-level aggregates, spine joins, `EXISTS` over elements, partitions, pre-aggregate answers, `count(DISTINCT)`, fused arms, windows over aggregates, text search) on a generated table of 20,000 to 50,000 rows, each run as the normal plan, as the planner chooses and as `Custom Scan (SiftAgg)`. It fails on a result that differs between the paths, a plan other than the one the shape expects and a cost ratio (scan over normal) outside the shape's band; the times (win, tie, lose at 1 ms and 20%) fail it only without `--warn-time`. The job `plan-gate` runs without timings in the quick tier and at 50,000 rows with them in the full tier.
- Added: pg_sift: a top-N scan (`ORDER BY key LIMIT n` over a `sift_col` or `sift_table`) can run with parallel workers: each takes windows of superblocks from a shared counter and keeps a heap of its best rows, they share the first-key rank of their n-th row so that units and rows after it are left out, and the leader merges the heaps by the keys and the TID, so the rows and their order are those of one process (`EXPLAIN` shows `Workers Planned` and `Workers Launched`). The planner prices the start of the workers, so scans of a few milliseconds stay serial: 200,000 rows, a row-wise arithmetic filter and a random key, `LIMIT 1000`, workers forced: 63 ms without workers, 32 with 2, 21 with 4. Test `col_topn_parallel` and its isolation spec.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.explain_refusals` (off by default; no plan changes and no cost when off): a query that kept the normal plan instead of `Custom Scan (SiftAgg)` reports, as a notice, `SiftAgg not used: <slug>: <reason>` with the first reason each candidate path gave up for (30 stable slugs in one registry, `src/agg/refuse.h`, listed in docs/USAGE.md; a refusal no site names is `other`), or `cost` when the path was built and the normal plan is estimated cheaper. The planner is asked without running the query by `sift.explain_why(query)` (arm, used, slug, reason, scan and normal cost; in the 3.3 scripts). Test `agg_refusals` asserts the slug of 34 shapes in one table and that every plan is the same with the setting on.
- Added: pg_sift: `sift.col_refresh(index [, max_segments])` rewrites the segments of a `sift_col` index whose pre-aggregates are not what the options ask now, so that `ALTER INDEX .. SET (cube_minmax, preagg, rollup)` reaches the units written before it without a `REINDEX`. A header-only census of the stored cubes finds the stale segments; each is rewritten alone as a merge of it does (the same rows and answers) and replaces the old one in one WAL-logged directory change under the maintenance lock, which readers and writers do not wait for, so a cancel or a `kill -9` leaves the segments done and the one in hand as it was and the call, made again, goes on; `max_segments` bounds a call and 0 counts. A segment of a minor above `sift.col_format_minor` is left as written, so that under 3 the index stays what the 3.0 library reads (the real 3.0.1 library verifies and answers from the refreshed segments). The planner counts the units that hold the histogram of a dim, its min and max and the rollup of a list, and prices the scan answered from them by that share: a half refreshed index costs between the two (min/max `GROUP BY` plan cost after 0, 1, 2, 3 steps and done, 200,000 rows: 676, 664, 645, 613, 543). Adding the min and max to four segments of 25,000 / 50,000 / 100,000 / 200,000 rows takes 23 / 36 / 50 / 81 ms (a rebuild 24 / 33 / 44 / 76 ms) and the index grows 7.5 / 16.4 / 15.2 / 17.3 kB of pre-aggregates over 270 / 426 / 754 / 1,401 kB; the space of the old segments is reused after `VACUUM`. No format change. Tests `col_refresh`, `col_preagg_minmax` (the census of cut and damaged cubes), the isolation spec `col_refresh`, `test/t/col_refresh_crash.sh`, `test/t/col_refresh_compat.sh`.
- Changed: pg_sift: `count(DISTINCT col)` of a 32-bit or text column the cube stores a histogram of (no key, or the column as the key; tests of the column or zone-covered tests of other columns) is answered from the groups of the histograms of the units whose rows are all visible, and the other units are scanned into the same set (`sift.agg_preagg_distinct`, on; `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` counts the units in `Units From Pre-aggregates`). No stored data changes. 25,000 to 200,000 rows, 8 values: 0.25, 0.64, 0.94, 1.47 ms scanned and 0.10, 0.15, 0.33, 0.52 ms from the histograms (median of 3, load 10 to 18).
- Changed: pg_sift: the walk of a top-N scan (`sift.topn_walk`) bounds the units by the zones of the sort keys after the first too (`sift.topn_zone_keys`, on): a first key that ties between units (`ORDER BY status, id LIMIT 10` with a few statuses) is decided by the zone of the next integer key, where every unit whose zone ties with the n-th row was read. 25,000 to 200,000 rows, 1,280 rows a unit, LIMIT 10: `ORDER BY g DESC, id DESC` reads 20, 40, 79, 157 units without it and 1, 2, 2, 2 with it; `ORDER BY k_mix, id` 16, 34, 71, 143 and 5, 5, 5, 5. The rows are those of the plain plan (`col_topn_order` fuzz).
- Added: pg_sift: a top-N scan (`ORDER BY key LIMIT n` over a `sift_col` or `sift_table`) takes `bool`, `time` and `money` columns as sort keys, besides the integer, date, timestamp, `numeric(p <= 18)` and float ones: each is one integer leaf whose order is the order of the type. `text`, `interval`, `uuid` and the other wide types, and expressions, keep the normal plan.
- Added: pg_sift: the coverage of two README rows (`ORDER BY .. Every refusal slug of `sift.explain_refusals` now says whether it is by design (`src/agg/refuse.h`, `docs/USAGE.md`), and a query that calls a volatile function reports the new slug `volatile` instead of the slug of the site that met it.
